\begin{document}
\maketitle


\begin{abstract}
We introduce \ftwoz, a hash-based Polynomial Commitment Scheme (PCS) for committing to multilinear polynomials $\bff$ with coefficients in an arbitrary finitely generated ring $S$, e.g.\ a finite field $\FF$, the integers $\ZZ$, a cyclotomic ring, etc. Given another arbitrary ring $R$ and a ring homomorphism $\psi:S\to R$, \ftwoz\ then proves evaluation claims over $R$ for the polynomial $\psi(\bff)$.   \ftwoz's costs depend almost exclusively on the number of bits in the coefficients of $\bff$, and not on $S$, $R$ or $\psi$. Moreover, \ftwoz\ provides range checks (or more generally, bit-size checks) essentially for free. 

\ftwoz\ can thus be used as a PCS in essentially any proof system. We do so to build a SNARK, called \ftwoz-SNARK, for integer polynomial constraints, following the fingerprinting technique of Campanelli and Hall-Andersen, where one commits over $\ZZ$ and proves the constraints over a random prime field $\FF_q$, i.e.\ \ftwoz\ is deployed with $S=\ZZ$, $R=\FF_q$, and $\psi$ reduction modulo $q$. \ftwoz\ applies equally to other ring-based proof systems, or field-based ones.

Our approach decomposes the witness into a string of bits and commits to it over a binary field $\comfield$, in packed form. Then, if necessary, transforms the evaluation claim over $R$ into one over $\ZZ$ via a randomized process. 
Finally,  using the tensor structure of ``eq'' vectors, it decomposes the  claim into square root many inner-product claims over $\ZZ$, and proves each of these in the exponent of a generator of $\comfield^*$ via the GKR protocol for batched grand products. We design specific optimizations that exploit that the factors across all the grand products have low entropy (i.e.\ they take a ``small'' amount of different values).   


 We implement \ftwoz-SNARK and use it to prove, among others, SHA-256 hashing followed by ECDSA signature verification; RSA modular exponentiation and Poseidon hashing;  integer multiplication; and SHA-256 hashing followed by multiplication modulo $2^{32}$, consistently obtaining better performance than prior approaches on most tasks. As an example, we achieve a throughput of $5$ million proved 32-bit integer multiplications per second on a  MacBook Air M5 24\,GB (10 threads, CPU-only) with proof sizes under $150$\,kB. We prove a SHA-256 hash of a $2$\,kB ($2^5$ compressions) message followed by a P-256 ECDSA signature verification with $50$\,ms and $3.3$\,ms prover and verifier time, respectively, and with a proof of $76$\,kB, single-threaded on a MacBook Air M5 24\,GB (CPU-only). With $10$ threads the times are $21$\,ms and $3.3$\,ms. 
 

\end{abstract}

\setcounter{tocdepth}{2}

\section{Introduction}\label{s:intro}



A Succinct Non-interactive Argument of Knowledge (SNARK) for a relation $\Relation$ is a non-interactive protocol that enables a prover to convince a verifier that, for a given public input $\inp$, they know a witness $\wit$ such that $(\inp,\wit)\in \Relation$, and where communication and verifier runtime are sublinear (i.e.\ the scheme is succinct).

In the vast majority of scenarios, $\Relation$ is defined over an algebraic object $R$, most commonly a finite field, and $(\inp,\wit)\in \Relation$ if and only if $(\inp,\wit)$ consists of elements that satisfy some polynomial equations over $R$, called  constraints. The choice of $R$ has a crucial impact on the design and  performance of the proof system, for two reasons: 
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
\item
\label{i:arithmetization}
\textbf{(Non-)Nativity of the computation.} Usually the computations one wants to prove are not native to $R$, so one has to perform a more or less complex emulation of the computation into polynomial equations over $R$. This can affect the size of the input-witness pair $\inp,\wit$ significantly \cite{xJsnark,jea_codes,EFpaper,zinc,binius}, which is the main cost driver in SNARKs. Additionally, the emulation step can be technically involved (thus requiring experts to perform it) and prone to  security errors \cite{snark-vuln-sok,zk-circuit-bugs,Edi23,Zkb} (thus requiring extensive security audits).  
\item
\label{i:field:performance}
\textbf{Proof system efficiency.} The efficiency and design of the prover and verifier algorithms are affected by the choice of $R$: for example, modern CPUs are more performant for operations in certain small fields (e.g.\ M31 \cite{cryptoeprint:2023/824,CircleSTARKs}, Babybear \cite{risczero}, Goldilocks \cite{plonky2:repo}) than in arbitrary fields, especially if these are larger. Importantly, another class of fields where CPUs operate exceptionally fast are \emph{binary fields}, i.e.\ fields of order a power of two.
%
\end{enumerate}
\noindent Recently, two independent lines of research have received increased attention:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
    \item Designing proof systems for relations expressed over  the ring of integers $R=\ZZ$, and over other rings that are not fields \cite{zaratan,zinc,zincplus,qaren,limber,rinnochio,soria2022doubly,baum2022moz,new_rings_work_one,new_rings_work_two}. The motivation behind this is to address \cref{i:arithmetization} above: in many practical scenarios, the computation to be proved is most succinctly and easily expressed over such rings, see \cite{zinc,zincplus,limber}. 
    \item Designing proof systems for $R=\comfield$ a binary field, for example \cite{stark,cryptoeprint:2022/1542,binius2,hashcaster:repo,flock}. The reason for this is twofold: first, some computations like classic hashes (SHA-256, Blake, etc.) are easier to express over  $\comfield$ than over other structures such as prime fields (however, these hash functions remain relatively un-native to  $\comfield$ since arithmetic modulo $2^{32}$ or $2^{64}$ remains relatively foreign to them). Second, binary fields are highly performant on modern CPUs, and further enable using a series of protocol techniques that greatly benefit performance, most importantly \textit{packed} polynomial commitment schemes \cite{binius,binius2}. 
    %and sumcheck optimizations \cite{yuval,more?}.
\end{itemize}


\subsection{Our contributions}\label{s:intro_contributions}


 In this work we bring the  two aforementioned lines of research together. Concretely, we  present \ftwoz, a hash-based Polynomial Commitment Scheme (PCS) that can commit to a witness vector $\bff \in S^\codedim$\footnote{With the entries of $\bff$ belonging to a publicly specified finite subset of $S$.} over an arbitrary finitely generated ring $S$. For example, $S$ can be any finite field $\FF$, the ring of integers $\ZZ$, a cyclotomic ring (for lattice-based cryptography), the integers modulo $2^{32}$, etc.

 \ftwoz \ can then prove linear constraints on $\bff$, such as Multilinear Extension (MLE) evaluation claims, over $S$, of the form $$\langle \bff, \vv\rangle = \mu \quad \text{in } S$$ for some public $\vv\in S^\codedim, \mu\in S$.
 More generally, given another finitely generated ring $R$ and a ring homomorphism $\psi: S\to R$, \ftwoz\ can prove claims of the form $$\langle \psi(\bff), \vv\rangle = \mu \quad \text{in } R$$ for public $\vv\in R^{\codedim}, \mu\in R$. For example, $S$ could be the ring of integers, $R$ could be a prime field $\FF_q$, and $\psi$ could be reduction modulo $q$. See below for an example of where such setting is useful. 

 \iflncs 
 \else 
 \bigskip
 \fi


% [AI-drafted (Claude Opus 5), 2026-09-18: availability footnote pointing at the anonymous artifact, at the author's request; please review]
We implement \ftwoz\footnote{Our implementation is available as PoC at \iflncs the anonymous repository \url{https://github.com/bitzcodes/BitZ.git}\else\url{https://github.com/albert-garreta/BitZ-pcs}, and as WIP production-grade in \url{https://github.com/worldfnd/BitZ}, see \cref{s:experiments} for the difference between the two.\fi.} and use it, among others (see below), to build a SNARK, called \ftwoz-SNARK, for proving R1CS constraints over the integers, following the \emph{fingerprinting} paradigm from Campanelli and Hall-Andersen \cite{zaratan}. Concretely, we commit to witnesses over the integers (setting $S=\ZZ$); then the verifier samples a random prime $q$; then the R1CS is projected to $\FF_q$ through the morphism $\pi_q$ consisting in reduction modulo $q$; then a PIOP is run on the resulting R1CS, yielding a linear claim (an MLE claim) on the projected witness $\pi_q(\bff)$ of the form $\langle \pi_q(\bff), \vv\rangle = \mu$; finally, this claim is proved with \ftwoz. The commitment is made before $q$ is sampled, which is required for the soundness of the fingerprinting technique. The PIOP may output its claim over a field extension of $\FF_q$, which \ftwoz\ can prove (\iflncs\cref{s: general_bitz}\else\cref{s:to_general_rings}\fi). An example end-to-end SNARK is described in \cref{s:to_snark}. 

We also build a SNARK that can prove constraints simultaneously over the integers and binary fields, and which uses \ftwoz\ as PCS. This SNARK also supports \emph{virtualization} of $\FF_2$ addition (XOR) between integer witness, in the sense that it can commit to  vectors of integer bits, to be used as witness in a constraint over $\ZZ$, and at the same time prove MLE evaluation or inner product claims of the XOR of these vectors.  We discuss this further in \cref{s:intro_hybrid_proofs}.  

We emphasize that \ftwoz\ can also be used to prove more exotic constraints, such as lattice-type operations, by using as constraint system and PIOP those of, for example, Zinc+ \cite{zincplus}, see \cref{s:to_general_bitz}. Overall, our \ftwoz-based SNARKs support highly expressive constraints that allow to  arithmetize many computations of interest  with little technical complexity, and with small circuits. 

The table below shows highlight benchmark results of our implementation of \ftwoz-SNARK. We compare against the most performant prior SNARKs we could find for each task at hand. %Perhaps surprisingly, in our experiments we find that, when proving integer constraints, Binius64 \cite{binius64}, a proof system fully native to binary fields, greatly outperforms other schemes that are specifically designed for the task.

\noindent \ftwoz\ presents the following properties:
 \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
     \item  \textbf{Pay-per-bit and algebraic independence.} Its performance  depends essentially exclusively on the bit-size of the witness $\bff$. In particular, the choice of ring $S$ (and $R$ and $\psi$ if it applies) has a small impact on its performance. 
     \item \textbf{Composability and universality.} Because of its flexibility in its choice of rings, and because of the point above, \ftwoz\  can be used as a PCS in essentially any proof system. This includes standard proof systems based on finite fields, or more exotic ones working over different rings, e.g.\ the integers \cite{zaratan,limber,zinc,zincplus,qaren}, polynomial rings \cite{zincplus}, lattices \cite{beullens2023labrador,Greyhound,SALSAA,Hachi}, etc.
     
     For example, a proof system using a PCS that is not plausibly post-quantum secure could replace its PCS with \ftwoz \ (which is plausibly post-quantum secure), regardless of the proof system's underlying field. %As mentioned above, the underlying rings barely affect \ftwoz's performance. 
     \item \textbf{``Free'' range checks.} \ftwoz\ provides range checks ``for free''. For example, when  committing  to integer witnesses, \ftwoz\ exposes a bit-size parameter $B$. Then, \ftwoz\ can be used to commit to integers of that bit-size, and it automatically guarantees that a malicious prover cannot commit to larger integers. This allows to omit many range checks that a standard proof system would need to enforce.
\end{enumerate}



\subsection{\iflncs Technical overview: \else\fi \ftwoz\ in a nutshell}\label{s:intro_nutshell}

 \ftwoz \ proves the aforementioned claim $\langle \psi(\bff), \vv \rangle = \mu$ over $R$ as follows. Assume first that $R$ is a finite prime field $\FF=\FF_q$. We discuss the general case later.  We  describe first an initial, mostly insufficient, attempt.
 \medskip
     \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
         \item  \textbf{Bitification.} First, the prover and verifier transform the  initial claim into a claim of the form  $\langle \psi(\bitstext(\bff)),\lncsbreak \uu \rangle = \mu$ over $R$, for some $\uu$, and where $\bitstext(\bff)$ denotes a vector  with entries in $\{0,1\}\subseteq \ZZ$. Intuitively, $\bitstext(\bff)$ is simply the result of  bit-decomposing the entries of $\bff$ in a natural way. This decomposition is linear, and so an inner product on $\bff$ equals an inner product on $\bitstext(\bff)$, for a different weight vector. For example, if $S=\ZZ$ and $\bff$ is filled with $B$ bit nonnegative integers, then $\uu=  \vv\otimes(1,2,\ldots, 2^{B-1})$.
         \item \textbf{Commitment.} The prover commits to $\bitstext(\bff)$ over a binary field $\comfield$ (by looking at integers bits as elements from $\FF_2$), using a packed commitment scheme such as WHIR \cite{whir} over $\comfield$, with the ring-switch techniques from \cite{binius,binius2}. Here \emph{packed commitment} means that every $\comfieldexp$ bits of $\bitstext(\bff)$ are grouped together into a single field element of $\comfield$, and that we encode and Merklize the resulting vector  of length $|\bitstext(\bff)|/\comfieldexp$ over $\comfield$. \emph{Ring-switching} allows to prove linear claims for $\bitstext(\bff)$ using only the commitment to the packed vector. 
         \item \textbf{Lift to integers.} Assuming $\FF$ is a prime field of size $q$ and $\psi=\pi_q:\ZZ\to \FF$ is reduction modulo $q$, the prover computes and sends $\mu'\in \ZZ$ such that $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\bff)\rangle = \mu'$ in $\ZZ$.
         \item \textbf{Proof in the exponent of $\comfield$.} Let $g$ be a generator of the multiplicative subgroup $\comfield^*$. Inspired by \cite{binius64}, we have prover and verifier  prove that $g^{\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\bff)\rangle} = g^{\mu'}$ in $\comfield$. Precisely, we have
         \begin{equation}\label{e:intro}
         g^{\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\bff)\rangle} = \prod_{i} \left((g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu_i)} -1) \cdot \bitstext(\bff)_i + 1 \right) = g^{\mu'} \qquad \text{in } \comfield,
         \end{equation}
         which is a polynomial expression over $\comfield$ on the vector $\bitstext(\bff)$ that we committed over $\comfield$.  This is a standard setting where there is no ``wrong-field'' arithmetic, hence \emph{in principle} one can use any binary-field based proof system over $\comfield$ to prove \eqref{e:intro}.   In \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev} and below in the ``key point'', we compare our method to using and proving \eqref{e:intro} with other approaches \cite{binius64,lev}.
%
     \end{enumerate}  

%\iflncs\else % LNCS: hidden
%     \begin{remark} Proving constraints ``in the exponent''  is a well-known idea that goes back at least to \cite{binius64,lev}. One of our main contributions is 1) in what context this technique is used, namely inside the PCS to prove MLE evaluation claims (or inner product claims) of the witness, as opposed to proving the initial \emph{non-linear} arithmetized computation, see \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev} for more details;  2) the optimizations we design for such a specific ``proof in the exponent'' setting, see \cref{s:to_low_entropy_gkr,s:gkr_low_entropy}; and 3) extending it so it can be used to prove constraints over arbitrary rings, and not just, say, integers or \emph{prime} fields with small  values (to avoid overflowing the multiplicative order of $g$).\end{remark}
%\fi
     
\noindent There are several issues with the attempt above:
\medskip
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
    \item \textbf{How to lift from general rings?} First, $R$ may not be a prime field, in which case there is no natural lift to integers in Step 3; rather, there is a lift to integer polynomials $\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k]$. 
    \item \textbf{How to lift large values?} Second, even if $R=\FF_q$ is a prime field,  $q=|\FF|$ may be large and the linear expression $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\bff)\rangle$ may overflow the order of $g\in \comfield^{*}$, which is $2^{\comfieldexp}-1$. In this case, proving  $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu), \bitstext(\bff)\rangle = \mu'$  ``in the exponent of $g$'' is unsound. 
    \item \textbf{Expensive grand product.} Third, even if neither of these problems hold, \eqref{e:intro} is, in principle, an expensive constraint to prove: indeed, it is a length $|\bitstext(\bff)|$ grand product of, essentially, arbitrary elements from $\comfield$ (which in our instantiation are of $128$ bits, i.e.\ not small).  Grand products can be expensive, and furthermore the fact that the factors are large prevents us from using known sumcheck optimizations such as univariate skips \cite{gruen} and others from \cite{flock,sumcheck_opt_1,sumcheck_opt_2}.
    \item \textbf{Non-succinct verifier.} Fourth, when proving \eqref{e:intro}, typically one ends up with an MLE evaluation claim on the vector $(g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu)_i})_{i\in[\codedim]}$ (and another on $\bitstext(\bff)$). If the verifier checks the claim itself, then it pays a linear cost. If otherwise the prover has to prove this claim, then it is essentially back to square one.
\end{itemize}

\bigskip



\noindent We solve these issues as follows: 


\paragraph{Issues 1 and 2:} Inspired by the fingerprinting techniques from \cite{zaratan,zincplus} (which we generalize to arbitrary finitely generated rings in this work), these are solved by lifting the inner product claim to $\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k]$ (after observing that all finitely generated rings are a projection of a ring such as $\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k]$), and then projecting the resulting claim to a new claim over a sufficiently small random prime field. Then we proceed as in Steps 3 and 4 with this new claim over $\FF_{q'}$.  %Thus we generalize the fingerprinting techniques from \cite{zaratan,zincplus} to arbitrary finitely generated rings.

\iflncs
\else \fi
\begin{comment}
\paragraph{Issues 1 and 2: lift to $\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k]$ and project to a random prime field, then lift again.} If $R$ is a ring with generators $1, \gamma_1, \ldots, \gamma_k$, we define a canonical ring homomorphism $\picanon: \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k] \to R$ as $\picanon(X_i) = \gamma_i$ and $\picanon(1)=1$, and at Step 3 we lift to a claim $\langle\bitstext(\bff), \picanon^{-1}(\uu) \rangle = \mu'$ over $\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k]$ (for a prime field, $k=0$ and this is the integer claim of Step 3). Then the verifier sends a random prime $q'$ of $\approx |\comfield^*|/|\bitstext(\bff)|$ size (small enough to avoid the issue of overflowing in the exponent) and a random morphism $\pirand:\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k] \to \FF_{q'}$ (e.g.\ reduction modulo $q'$ followed by evaluation at a random point), the claim is replaced by its image under $\pirand$, and we re-enter Step 3 with this new claim over $\FF_{q'}$.
\bigskip 
\end{comment}

\iflncs \else
\bigskip
 
\noindent \emph{\underline{128 bits of security using $\FF_{2^{128}}$, arbitrary rings $R$, and arbitrary sizes of the entries of $\vv$.}} %\lncsnote{Review} 
We manage to instantiate \ftwoz\ with 128 bits of security, using $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$, while having essentially no limitation on the size of the ring $R$ we start with, see \cref{s:instantiation}.
\fi

\paragraph{Issues 3 and 4: batched low entropy grand products.} We split  \eqref{e:intro} into $\approx\sqrt{|\bitstext(\bff)|}$ grand products of the same form as \eqref{e:intro}, so that, even though in total there are $|\bitstext(\bff)|$ factors among all of them, these factors can only take $\sqrt{|\bitstext(\bff)|}$ values. More concretely, we assume $\uu=\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}$ tensor-decomposes into $\approx \sqrt{|\bitstext(\bff)|}$ sized factors $\uu^{(1)}, \uu^{(2)}$. This is typically the case since $\vv$ is usually an ``eq'' vector, and $\uu=L^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv$ for a highly structured matrix $L$\footnote{For example, in our previous example, $\uu= \vv \otimes (1,2,\ldots, 2^{B-1})$. Then, if $\vv$ is an ``eq'' vector, then $\uu$ admits highly customizable tensor decompositions.}. Then, assuming the notation of Step 3 above, we aim to prove  in the exponent $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle = \mu_j'$ in $\ZZ$  for all $j\in [|\uu^{(2)}|]$, such that $\sum_{j} \pi_q(\mu_j') \cdot \uu_j^{(2)} = \mu$, where we index   $\bitstext(\bff)$ with indices $i\in [|\uu^{(1)}|], j\in [|\uu^{(2)}|]$. We  set up a grand product for each of these claims, obtaining 
  \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_2}
         g^{\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle} = \prod_{i} \left((g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu_i^{(1)})} -1) \cdot \bitstext(\bff)_{ij} + 1\right) = g^{\mu_j'} \qquad \text{for all } j.
         \end{equation}
There are $|\bitstext(\bff)|$ factors in total, but only $\approx \sqrt{|\bitstext(\bff)|}$ different values these factors can take. We say \eqref{e:intro_2} is a \emph{low entropy batched grand product}. 

To prove \eqref{e:intro_2} we use a batched GKR protocol with the following  optimization, among others: because of the low-entropy phenomenon, we build several small lookup tables of size $O(\sqrt{|\bitstext(\bff)|})$ (for example, of less than 1 MB for $|\uu^{(1)}|=2^{16}$, see \cref{tab:gkr_tables}) which are used during several of the most expensive sumcheck rounds of the GKR protocol. \iflncs \else Concretely, the last sumcheck (the most expensive one) uses  lookup tables to compute the first three messages of the prover; the second last sumcheck does the same for the first two prover messages (with two tables); and the third last sumcheck does the same for the first prover message (with one table).\fi

\bigskip

\noindent \emph{\underline{A key point}:} The key is that these square-root sized tables can be used in each of the grand products from \eqref{e:intro_2}, producing a large amortization, and allowing us to build several tables for different sumcheck rounds, while keeping them in cache, where table reads are cheaper than field multiplications. 

The reason this is possible is that  we only seek to prove witness MLE evaluations (or inner products) in the exponent. In this specific setting, we can   decompose  the initial claim into the subclaims $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle = \mu_j'$. The reason we only need to address this simple setting is that our scheme starts off in a ring $R$, with its \emph{non-linear} constraints arithmetized over $R$. Then the constraints are reduced via a PIOP to a \emph{linear} integer claim on the witness,  which we then prove ``in the exponent''. 

Other proof systems using the ``proof in the exponent'' technique, e.g.\ \cite{binius64}, aim to prove entire integer constraints in the exponent. Ultimately, this means one has to prove products of the form $y\cdot x$ in the exponent, for $x,y$ \emph{two witness elements}, while we only prove products $a\cdot x$, for $a$ a  value coming from a public vector admitting a square-root sized tensor decomposition. 

See \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev} for discussion on this and other usages of the ``proof-in-the-exponent'' approach.




\paragraph{Issue 4.} The above solution also takes care of Issue 4: the batched GKR protocol outputs MLE evaluation claims on $(g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})_i})_{i\in[\codedim]}$ (and $\bitstext(\bff)$, which is dealt with by the PCS over $\comfield$). This is an $O(\sqrt{|\bitstext(\bff)|})$ vector, and the verifier can check this claim by itself cheaply.


\subsubsection{Using \ftwoz\ to prove constraints with hybrid arithmetics}\label{s:intro_hybrid_proofs}


In our implementation, we use \ftwoz-SNARK to prove R1CS constraints simultaneously over integers and binary fields. We explain how next. We stress again that one can use \ftwoz\ to build all sorts of proof systems for all sorts of constraints. Nothing forces one to use R1CS-like constraints, nor constraints over integers and binary fields. 

 %\paragraph{Virtual $\FF_2$-linear transforms, NP-complete dually linear relations, and one arithmetization of SHA-256.} 
 Observe that, at the end of \iflncs\cref{c:core_iop}\else the steps of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}\fi, we have a standard linear claim on $\bitstext(\bff)$ in $\comfield$, say \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_comfield_linear}\langle \bitstext(\bff), \uu'\rangle = \eta \quad \text{ in } \comfield.\end{equation}
  Say we would like to add an extra constraint, over $\comfield$, on $\bitstext(\bff)$ of the form $\bitstext(\bff) = M \cdot \bfg$ for some matrix $M$ and vector $\bfg$ with entries in $\FF_2$. Then, we could instead of committing to $\bitstext(\bff)$ \iflncs as in \cref{c:core_iop}\else in Step 1 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}\fi, commit to $\bfg$. The claim  \eqref{e:intro_comfield_linear} becomes
 %
 $$
\langle \bfg, M^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \uu'\rangle = \eta \quad \text{ in } \comfield,
 $$
 where $\mathsf{T}$ denotes transposition. This is a linear claim on $\bfg$, the vector we committed to. We can prove this claim with a standard PCS over $\comfield$ such as \cite{whir}. 
 



 To keep the verifier succinct, we need to ensure that $M^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \uu'$ admits a succinct representation. In our applications, the vector $\uu'$ admits such (see \iflncs\cref{r:tensor_dec_u}\else Step 2 in \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}\fi), and so one needs to ensure $M$ carries some succinct structure, such as for example a tensor decomposition, so that $M^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \uu'$ does not become a dense vector.  
 
 This allows \ftwoz\  to prove polynomial constraints over $R$ on witness vectors $\bff$ whose bit-decomposition is a virtual $\FF_2$-linear combination of a committed vector $\bfg\in \FF_2^{\codedim'}$. For example, \ftwoz \ can efficiently prove the following R1CS-like relation: 
%
\iflncs\begingroup\small\fi
\begin{align*}
&\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)\\ & \defeq \left\{ \begin{aligned}
    &\inp= (\xx, A,B,C, M)\in \FF_2^{\inplen} \times (\ZZ^{m\times \codedim})^3 \times \FF_2^{\codedim \times (\inplen+\codedim')}\\
    & \wit = \bfg \in \bits^{\codedim'} \subseteq \FF_2^{\codedim'}
\end{aligned}\  \left| \  \begin{aligned} %&f=\Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bff)\\ 
&(A\cdot \bff) \circ (B\cdot \bff) = C \cdot \bff\ \text{in } \ZZ \\
&\bitstext(\bff)= \left(  M\cdot \bfg\right)    \text{ in } \FF_2\end{aligned} \right.\right\}.
\end{align*}
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
%

A particularly interesting subclass of this relation is that obtained by setting $A=B=\mathbf{0}$, in which case the resulting relation, which we call the \emph{$\mathsf{CM}$ relation}, degenerates to linear constraints over $\ZZ$ and over $\FF_2$, i.e.\ a ``dually linear'' constraint. 
\iflncs
% [LNCS pointer (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-16: to the appendix section that now holds the virtual-transform material, the SHA table and the CM lemma; please review]
% [AI edit (Claude Opus 5), 2026-09-18: "this relation" replaced by the name of the relation (the ECDSA part needs $A,B\neq \mathbf{0}$, so the arithmetization is not a CM instance); please review]
 Our arithmetization of SHA-256 + ECDSA signature verification is an instance of $\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)$ (see \cref{tab:sha_arithmetization} and \cref{r:CM_is_NP_complete} in \cref{s:to_f2_virtual}). Concretely  we arithmetize SHA-256 with $4.4\times$ smaller witnesses than Binius64 \cite{binius64} and Flock \cite{flock} ($3.6\times$ smaller than the AI-optimized arithmetization), see \cref{tab:sha_arithmetization}. However, if only seeking to prove a large amount of SHA-256 compressions, using a binary field is still preferable (see \cref{s:experiments_sha_modmul}).
\else

% [AI-drafted (Claude Opus 5), 2026-09-18, at the author's request: in the paragraph below, the sentence on SHA-256 + ECDSA as an R1CS-bits-virtual instance and the pointer to s:experiments_sha_modmul, both taken from the submission version; please review]
In \cref{r:CM_is_NP_complete} we show that the CM relation is NP-complete. Our arithmetization of SHA-256 + ECDSA signature verification (\cref{tab:intro_table}) is an instance of $\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)$. We arithmetize SHA-256 as an instance of the CM relation, with $4.4\times$ smaller witnesses than Binius64 \cite{binius64} and Flock \cite{flock} ($3.6\times$ smaller than the AI-optimized arithmetization), see \cref{tab:sha_arithmetization}.  
% [AI-reworded (Claude Opus 5), 2026-09-16: previous sentence, was "we propose a proof system that can handle simultaneously constraints over integers (or other rings) and binary fields, and which uses \ftwoz\ as its PCS"; please review]
\fi


\begin{lncstable}{ShaArith}
\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{l|c}
        SHA-256 arithmetization type & Witness bit-size per compression  \\ 
        \toprule
        $\mathsf{CM}$ & $7144$ bits \\
        \hline
        R1CS over $\comfield$ &  $31744$ bits
         \\
        \hline
        R1CS over $\comfield$, AI optimized &  $25470$ bits
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Witness bit-size per SHA-256 compression as per two different arithmetizations. The $\mathsf{CM}$ row indicates the bit-size when arithmetizing SHA-256 via an instance of the $\mathsf{CM}$ relation (see \eqref{e:CM_rel}) using the methods from \cref{e:CM_rel,r:CM_is_NP_complete}\iflncs\else. The full arithmetization  is available in \url{https://github.com/reilabs/freigen/blob/324935e/Freigen/Circuit/Examples/Sha256.lean}\fi. The second row displays the witness bit-size in the  arithmetization from Binius64 \cite{binius64}, which is an R1CS constraint over a binary field. Flock's \cite{flock} arithmetization has 31400 bits per compression. The AI row refers to an optimized arithmetization found by an AI working against a Lean specification in the zkGolf competition \url{https://zk.golf/}.  }
    \label{tab:sha_arithmetization}
\end{table}
\end{lncstable}



Despite this small arithmetization, when it comes to proving a large amount of classic hash compressions, we observe that arithmetizing over $\comfield$ and using native binary field proof systems such as Flock \cite{flock} and Binius64 \cite{binius64} perform better than proving the $\mathsf{CM}$ constraint with \ftwoz-SNARK. Because of this, in \cref{s:hybrid_snark} we describe how the \ftwoz-SNARK can be combined with such a binary-field SNARK, on a common commitment, to prove computations that mix constraints over the integers (or other rings) and over binary fields. We provide a short overview of this SNARK below.

We use this simple combination to prove SHA-256 compressions together with multiplications modulo $2^{32}$ (see \cref{tab:intro_table}).

% [AI-reworded (Claude Opus 5), 2026-09-16: tones down "we describe a proof system ... which uses BitZ as its PCS" (the hybrid scheme is a simple combination; its batched opening uses WHIR or a similar PCS); please review]
% [AI edit (Claude Opus 5), 2026-09-18, at the author's request: this paragraph and the next sentence are shown in both versions (they were submission-only); "MLE evaluation claim(s)" replaced by "inner product claim(s)", as in \cref{s:hybrid_snark}; please review]
\paragraph{Polynomial constraints in integers and binary fields} For computations mixing constraints over the integers (or other rings) with constraints over binary fields, we construct a SNARK that works, intuitively, as follows: the witness of both constraints is committed together over a binary field. Then we run  \ftwoz-SNARK  for the integer constraints, and  a binary-field SNARK such as Binius64 \cite{binius64}, both using the committed witness. Both of these  reduce the initial constraints to an inner product claim of the witness over a binary field. Now we batch the two resulting inner product claims together and prove them over the binary field using a PCS such as WHIR \cite{whir} or Ligerito \cite{Ligerito}. We provide more details in \cref{s:hybrid_snark}. 





\subsection{Related work}\label{s:related_work}


\iflncs
% [AI-drafted (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-15, revised by the author: LNCS-only compression of the five paragraphs below, which are unchanged in the full version]
\paragraph{Proof systems over integers and other rings.} Campanelli and Hall-Andersen \cite{zaratan} propose proving polynomial constraints over the integers by projecting them onto a random prime field sampled by the verifier, a technique they call \emph{fingerprinting}. Subsequent works have adopted the same technique \cite{zinc,qaren,zincplus,limber}. \cite{zaratan} also proposes a hidden-order group based PCS suitable for their PIOP, similar to the ones in \cite{DARK,BlockDARK}. Zinc \cite{zinc} combines fingerprinting with a hash-based PCS over the rationals $\QQ$, providing the first relatively practical proof system over the integers, and addresses the subtle issues that arise when committing to \emph{integer} vectors with error correcting codes over rationals. Zinc+ \cite{zincplus} extends \cite{zinc} in two directions: it proposes a constraint system with polynomial constraints simultaneously over many types of rings\footnote{In the terminology of \cite{zincplus}, the constraints are over \emph{polynomial rings with ideal membership constraints}, for certain types of ideals (generated by a monic and non-constant polynomial, or zero). This is equivalent to saying the constraints are over different types of rings (within a certain class).} and fields, together with a method for constructing PIOPs for it that projects  all such rings and fields onto a random prime field. Zinc+ further improves the soundness analysis of the PCS of \cite{zinc}, and proposes the Integer Pseudo Reed-Solomon (IPRS) codes over $\ZZ$ or $\QQ$, obtained by ``lifting'' an FFT encoding algorithm over a small finite field to the integers. $\QQ$aren \cite{qaren} is a compiler that turns any PCS over a finite field of sufficiently large characteristic into a PCS for integer polynomials (more precisely, for rational polynomials close to the target integer polynomials, as in Zinc and Zinc+), yielding the first transparent and fully succinct PCS over integers/rationals. No implementation or performance reports are provided in \cite{qaren}. The concurrent work Limber \cite{limber} is a compiler of the same kind which only requires characteristic $q=\Omega(\lambda^2 \log^2 N)$, $N$ being the witness size (versus $\Omega(2^\lambda)$ in \cite{qaren}), compiles into strictly integer PCS (so that the ``almost integers'' issue does not arise), and is instantiated with the Hyrax and the Brakedown PCS. In \cref{s:experiments} we compare \ftwoz \ against Limber.
\else % LNCS: the five paragraphs below are replaced by the compressed one above
\paragraph{Proof systems over integers and other rings.} In \cite{zaratan}, Campanelli and Hall-Andersen propose proving polynomial constraints over integers, by projecting them onto a random prime field (sampled by the verifier), a technique which the authors call \emph{fingerprinting}. Subsequent works have adopted this idea \cite{zinc,qaren,zincplus,limber}. \cite{zaratan} also proposes a hidden-order group based PCS suitable for their PIOP, similar to the ones in \cite{DARK,BlockDARK}. The PCS is not implemented and so we do not compare against it.

The Zinc \cite{zinc} work adopts \emph{fingerprinting} and designs a hash-based PCS over the rationals $\QQ$, suitable for the fingerprinting approach, providing the first relatively practical proof system over the integers. \cite{zinc} addresses subtle technical issues that arise when aiming to commit to \emph{integer} vectors with error correcting codes (namely, malicious provers can potentially use rational numbers that are very close to being integers of a target magnitude).

The Zinc+ \cite{zincplus} work extends \cite{zinc} in two directions: 1) It proposes a constraint system that includes polynomial constraints simultaneously over many types of rings\footnote{In the terminology of \cite{zincplus}, the constraints are over \emph{polynomial rings with ideal membership constraints}, for certain types of ideals (generated by a monic and non-constant polynomial, or zero). However this is equivalent to saying the constraints are over different types of rings (within a certain class).} and fields, and provides a method for constructing PIOPs for those. This method extends the fingerprinting approach by not only projecting $\ZZ$ onto a random prime field, but projecting all sorts of rings and fields onto a random prime field. 2) It improves the soundness analysis of the PCS in \cite{zinc}, plus it proposes a new linear code over $\ZZ$ or $\QQ$, called Integer Pseudo Reed-Solomon (IPRS) code, obtained by ``lifting'' (in a suitable way) an FFT encoding algorithm over a small finite field, to the integers. 



In \cite{qaren} the authors propose $\QQ$aren, a compiler that takes any PCS over a finite field of sufficiently large characteristic and creates a PCS for integer polynomials (or, more precisely, rational polynomials close to the target integer polynomials, see the overview of \cite{zinc} above). The authors instantiate their compiler to obtain the first transparent and fully succinct PCS over integers/rationals. The prior work \cite{zinc,zincplus} is not succinct on the bit-size of the integers being committed. No implementation or performance reports are provided in \cite{qaren}.

The concurrent work Limber \cite{limber} proposes a new compiler that transforms any PCS over fields of sufficiently large characteristic $q$ (in this case, $q$ is $\Omega(\lambda^2 \log^2 N)$, $N$ being the witness size, while in \cite{qaren} it is $\Omega(2^\lambda)$) into a PCS for integer polynomials. Additionally Limber compiles into strictly integer PCS, and does not present the issue that a malicious prover could use rationals that are ``almost integers''. Limber is instantiated in practice with the Hyrax and the Brakedown PCS. In \cref{s:experiments} we compare \ftwoz \ against Limber.
\fi

Compared to all prior work, \ftwoz\ proposes a new  concrete PCS over rings, and a new paradigm  for building such. As already demonstrated in the present work for $\ZZ$ and Zaratan, the PCS \ftwoz\ can be combined with the arithmetizations and PIOPs from Zaratan, Zinc, and Zinc+, and any other PIOP for constraints over rings, to build efficient SNARKs over finitely generated rings.


\paragraph{Proof systems over binary fields.} Hash-based SNARKs over binary fields were pioneered by the line of work of Ben-Sasson et al.~\cite{fri,stark,linearSizedIOPs,cryptoeprint:2022/1542}, but did not see widespread adoption until the introduction of \textit{packed} polynomial commitment schemes by Diamond and Posen~\cite{binius,binius2}; see also \cite{binius64}. Such schemes allow one to commit to a bit-valued witness over an extension field without incurring embedding overhead, while still providing evaluation proofs for its original ``uncompressed'' representation. % The \emph{ring-switching} technique of Diamond and Posen~\cite{binius2} is generic, enabling the use of codes over larger alphabets without introducing representational redundancy.\footnotemark 
%A particularly attractive choice is Reed--Solomon codes over $GF(2^k)$ \cite{binius64,hashcaster:repo}, which offer an excellent trade-off between distance and encoding cost, although several works~\cite{Blaze,tensorswitch,Bolt} explore this design space using more efficient codes.

%\footnotetext{%
%Alternative approaches based on Galois conjugates have been proposed thereafter~\cite{hashcaster:repo,FRIBinius:Galois}.
%Remarkably, the Galois approach is present in prior work \cite[Appendix F]{stark}, although not explicitly framed in full generality.
%}

At the time of writing, two further works push the performance envelope of binary proof systems. Bünz, Rothblum, and Wang~\cite{flock} combine \cite{binius64} with the batched R1CS strategy inspired by~\cite{linearSizedIOPs}, achieving a striking proving throughput of several hundred thousand hashes per second on an M4 Max CPU. BinarySpartan~\cite{binaryspartan}, in turn, incorporates the optimizations of \cite{binius64}-Flock into Spartan, resulting in a proof system with again high hash-proving throughput.
% \ulrich{Not sure about Flock and Binary Spartan, since nothing conceptually new is happening there.
% The main improvement over binius64 stems from batching R1CS instances as in \cite{linearSizedIOPs}, and to be frank I do not know what Spartan is about, since binius64 and Flock are essentially Spartan.
% I have the impression that naming them requires also to provide a more complete list of binary provers.
% }

\paragraph{Commitments in binary fields with openings in rings $R$ of characteristic different than $2$.} To our knowledge this primitive is studied only in Soukhanov's note \cite{lev} and in the present work. The note \cite{lev}  proposes a scheme, called \emph{Fields-Witch} for committing over the binary field $\FF_{2^{127}}$ and then executing a PCS opening over $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$ (a prime field), by running the PCS in the exponent of a generator of   $\FF_{2^{127}}^*$. The approach, substantially different to ours, is limited to $R=\FF_{2^{127}-1}$ (or analogous configurations where $2^n-1$ is prime) and does not seem to be able to leverage our aforementioned ``low-entropy'' based optimizations.  We discuss this further in \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev} and compare \ftwoz\ with \cite{lev} in \cref{s:exp_fields_witch}. %Soukhanov has since released an implementation of the note (\texttt{fields-witch}, \url{https://github.com/morgana-proofs/fields-witch}); we compare against it in \cref{s:exp_fields_witch} and discuss the approach further in \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev}

%$\QQ$aren and Limber \cite{qaren,limber} provide a related primitive when $R=\ZZ$, but can only support commitments over fields of relatively large characteristic (compared to $2$).

\subsection{\iflncs Warm-up: \else\fi The prime field case}\label{s:to_simple_bitz}

We next provide a high-level overview of how \ftwoz\ works in a  simple setting.  We let $\FF_q$ be a prime field with $q$ elements, and assume the prover claims it knows a vector $\bff\in \FF_q^{\codedim_0}$  such that
 \begin{equation}\label{e:new_to_0}\langle \vv, \bff\rangle =\mu \qquad \text{in  } \FF_q,
\end{equation}
where $\vv\in \FF_q^{\codedim_0}, \mu\in \FF_q$ are public.  
%
%This is a core setting, in that many finite-field based SNARKs require such primitive. However, on several occasions, they require $\FF_q$ to be a field extension, instead of just a prime field. In \cref{s:to_general_rings} we treat this important case, as well as others.
%
%Additionally, the present setting is required when using the fingerprinting PIOP approach of \cite{zaratan} for proving integer constraints. In that approach, the integer constraint is projected to a random prime field $\FF_q$, and a PIOP is run over $\FF_q$, outputting a claim of the form \eqref{e:new_to_0}. 
%
%We  present a protocol (a simple instantiation of \ftwoz) for proving \eqref{e:new_to_0}.
We assume $\vv$ admits a tensor decomposition
%
\lncsmath{
\vv= \vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)} \in \FF_q^{\codedim_1 \times \codedim_2},
}
where we assume there is freedom to choose the lengths of $\vv^{(1)},\vv^{(2)}$ (whose product is $\codedim_0$).  This is typically the case in practice since $\vv$ is usually an ``eq'' vector, i.e.\ $\vv=(\eq(\bb,\rr))_{\bb\in \bits^{\log \codedim_0}}$ for some $\rr\in \FF_q^{\log\codedim_0}$. Tensor decompositions play a crucial role in the efficiency of \ftwoz, as we discuss in \cref{s:why_the_tensor_split}.

 \paragraph{Step 1: Commitment in $\comfield$.} Let $B\defeq \lceil \log q\rceil$ and let $\codedim\defeq B\cdot \codedim_0$. The prover creates a vector $\bitstext(\bff) \in \bits^{\codedim} \subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim}$ of integer bits, obtained by bit-decomposing each entry $\bff_i$ of $\bff$ (a field element) into a vector of $B$ bits $(\bff_{i1}, \ldots, \bff_{iB})$ so that $\bff_i = \sum_{j\in [B]} \bff_{ij}\cdot 2^{j-1}$, and concatenating all such $B$-length vectors.

 Formally, we look at $\bitstext(\bff)$ as a vector of \emph{integer} bits. We write $\pi_q(\bitstext(\bff))$ and $\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$ for the same vector seen in $\FF_q$ and $\FF_2$, respectively (in general, $\pi_n$ denotes reduction modulo $n$).  By $\pi^{-1}$ we denote the canonical representation of elements from $\FF_q$ as integers in the range $\range{0}{q-1}$.

     Next, the prover packs the vector  $\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)) \subseteq  \FF_2^{\codedim}$ as a vector from $\comfield^{\codedim/\comfieldexp}$ (see below), and encodes the packed vector with a Reed-Solomon code $\cC$ (or any other code) over $\comfield$, and sends a Merkle root of the resulting codeword to the verifier.   To \emph{pack} $\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$, every consecutive $\comfieldexp$ bits $b_1, \ldots, b_\comfieldexp$ of $\bitstext(\bff)$ are joined together as a single field element $\sum_{i\in [\comfieldexp]} X^{i-1}\cdot \pi_2(b_i)$ from $\comfield$ (one can also use a different $\FF_2$-basis than the monomial basis $(X^i)_i$). We denote the encoded word by $f\in \comfield^{\codelength}$, with $\codelength$ being the length of the code.
     
    From here on, the prover and verifier set out to prove an instance of the following relation, which we call LinBitsRings, and which takes a more general form in \cref{s:to_general_rings}:
%
    \begin{align*}
 &\LinModFieldBin(\FF_q) = \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\mu, \vv)\in \FF_q  \times \FF_q^{ \codedim_0}, \\
 &\oracle = f \in \comfield^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit= \bff\in \FF_q^{\codedim_0}\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned}  
 &f = \Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)),\\
 &\langle \bff, \vv \rangle = \mu  \qquad  \text{in } \FF_q
 \end{aligned} \right\},
\end{align*}
    %
    where $\Enc_\cC$ denotes encoding with the code $\cC$; $\pack$ is the packing operation. When proving such relation, the verifier receives $\inp$ and oracle access to $\oracle=f$, and the prover receives additionally $\wit$.

    If using a proximity parameter beyond the unique decoding radius, the prover and verifier perform an out of domain sample, see Round 0 of \cref{c:core_iop}.


\paragraph{Step 2: Bitification of the linear claim.}  Observe that $$\langle \vv,\bff\rangle =  \sum_{i\in [\codedim_0]} \sum_{j\in [B]}\vv_i \cdot 2^{j-1}\cdot \pi_q(\bitstext(\bff)_{ij}) = \langle \uu, \pi_q(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle \qquad \text{in } \FF_q,$$ for $\uu\defeq \vv\otimes(1,2,\ldots, 2^{B-1})$, where we index the entries of $\bitstext(\bff)$ with tuples $(i,j)\in [\codedim_0]\times[B]$.  Now, instead of having the prover prove it knows $\bff$ satisfying \eqref{e:new_to_0}, the prover can instead prove it knows a vector of bits $\bfg\in \bits^\codedim \subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ satisfying \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_2}\langle \uu, \pi_q(\bfg)\rangle =\mu \ \text{in  } \FF_q, \qquad f = \Enc_\cC\circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bfg).
\end{equation} 
Abusing the notation, we denote $\bfg$ by $\bitstext(\bff)$ (the bit vector used by the honest prover).

%
If $\vv$ is an ``eq vector'' or a vector admitting flexible tensor decompositions, then $\uu$ can be tensor decomposed as $\uu= \uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)} \in \FF_q^{\codedim_1 \cdot \codedim_2}$ with  $\codedim_1\approx \codedim_2\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$ (our instantiation takes $\codedim_1\in [\codedim^{0.5},\codedim^{0.6}]$, see \cref{s:instantiation}). 

%Other choices of decompositions may lead to better efficiency later, especially if $\bff$ contains entries with heterogeneous bit-sizes.  % assume for the sake of efficiency later on that $\uu$ also admits a tensor decomposition $\uu=\uu_1\otimes \uu_2 \in \FF_q^{\codedim_1 \cdot \codedim_2}$. This is in general a free assumption, see \cref{r:tensor_dec_u} 


 \paragraph{Step 3 (Optional):  Resampling of $\FF_q$.}  For soundness later, we need that $|\FF_q|< \fieldboundsizeexponent$ (because we will prove a collection of integer linear claims ``in the exponent'' of a generator of $\comfield^*$). If this condition is not met, we replace now $\FF_q$ by a smaller prime field via a randomized procedure. For now, we assume the condition holds, and we explain this procedure below in \cref{r:large_prime_field_in_BitZ_PCS}. 


 \paragraph{Step 4: Transformation of \eqref{e:intro_eq_2} into a batch of low-magnitude integer claims.} The prover organizes $\bitstext(\bff)$ into a $\codedim_1\times \codedim_2$ matrix with columns $\bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\in \bits^{\codedim_1}\subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim_1}$ ($j\in [\codedim_2]$). Then it computes and publishes   $\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2}\in  \ZZ$ defined as 
         $\mu_j\defeq\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle$  in $\ZZ$, for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$.  
         Then the verifier checks that $\mu_j\in \range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$ for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$, and that 
         %
         \lncsmath{
        \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi_q(\mu_j) \cdot \uu_j^{(2)} = \mu \qquad \text{in  } \FF_q,
         }
         and \eqref{e:intro_eq_2} is replaced by the  claim that the prover knows a vector of bits $\bitstext(\bff)\in\bits^\codedim\subseteq\ZZ^\codedim$ such that \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_3}\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle =\mu_j \ \text{in  } \ZZ, \text{ for all } j\in [\codedim_2], \qquad f = \Enc_\cC\circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)).
        \end{equation} 
       
        
        
        
        \paragraph{Step 5: Exponentiation in $\comfield$ and grand product in $\comfield$.}  Let $g$ be a generator of the multiplicative subgroup of $\comfield$. Since we assumed $|\FF_q|< \fieldboundsizeexponent$, and since both sides of the first part of \eqref{e:intro_eq_3} lie in $\range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$ (recall that $\bitstext(\bff)$ has bit entries, and that the verifier checked the range of each $\mu_j$), the first part of \eqref{e:intro_eq_3} is true if and only if 
          \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_4}
          \begin{aligned}
          g^{\mu_j}  &= g^{\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle}\\ &= g^{\sum_{i\in[\codedim_1]} \pi_q^{-1}(\uu_i^{(1)}) \cdot \bitstext(\bff)_{ij} } = \prod_{i\in [\codedim_1]} g^{ \pi_q^{-1}(\uu_i^{(1)}) \cdot \bitstext(\bff)_{ij} } 
          \\ &= \prod_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \left(\left(g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu_i^{(1)})} -1\right)\cdot \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)_{ij})  +1\right) \qquad \text{in  } \comfield \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2].
          \end{aligned}
        \end{equation} 
       %, and in the last expression, $\bitstext(\bff)_{ij}$ denotes the $i,j$-th entry (a bit) in $\bitstext(\bff)$ seen as an element of $\comfield$.

        The prover and verifier run  a batched grand product PIOP over the binary field $\comfield$ for the claim  \eqref{e:intro_eq_4} (see \cref{s:to_low_entropy_gkr}), obtaining as a result the claim that the prover knows some vector of bits $\bitstext(\bff)$ satisfying
        \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_5} \langle \zz, \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)_{*j}) \rangle = c_j \ \text{in  } \comfield \ \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2], \qquad f = \Enc_\cC\circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)),
        \end{equation} 
        for some $\zz\in \comfield^{\codedim_1}$ and $c_j\in \comfield$. 


        This is by far the most expensive step of \ftwoz. Our grand product PIOP is a simple GKR protocol for batched grand products, for which we design prover optimizations, which do not modify the GKR  transcripts, i.e.\ they only optimize the GKR prover's algorithm, without changing the standard GKR protocol, see \cref{s:to_low_entropy_gkr} below and \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}. These optimizations exploit the ``low entropy'' of the factors $(g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}_i)}-1)\cdot \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)_{ij}) +1$ across $(i,j)\in [\codedim_1]\times [\codedim_2]$, in the sense that among $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$ factors, there are only $\codedim_1+1=O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ possible values taken by these factors.  This, among others, is why it is important to have the vector $\uu$ admit a tensor split into $\approx$square-root sized factors (see \cref{s:why_the_tensor_split} for more reasons). % See \cref{?,s:gkr_low_entropy} for more details.
\begin{comment}
\begin{remark}[Other usages of the ``prove-in-the-exponent'' approach]
       There are other projects (e.g.\ Binius64 \cite{binius64} and Soukhanov's note \cite{lev}) that use exponentiation to prove integer (or mod $|\comfield|-1$) claims in binary fields, none of these (to our knowledge) is used or is usable in a context that results in the low entropy grand products of ours, where our optimizations apply.  We discuss this further in \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev}.
      \end{remark}  
      \end{comment}

 The  $\codedim_2$ claims from \cref{e:intro_eq_5} can optionally be batched into  a single claim \begin{equation} \label{e:intro_eq_5_batched}\langle \zz', \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)) \rangle = c \ \text{in  } \comfield, \qquad f = \Enc_\cC\circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)),\end{equation} for  $\zz'\in \comfield^{\codedim}, c\in \comfield$ with, for example, $\zz'=(\alpha_1,\ldots, \alpha_{\codedim_2}) \otimes \zz$ for $\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_{\codedim_2}$ verifier's randomness, and $c=\sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \alpha_j\cdot c_j$. 
        However, in most instantiations this step may not be necessary, especially if using an interleaved code to commit to $\bitstext(\bff)$. 
 \paragraph{Step 6: Proving linear claims on $\bitstext(\bff)$ over $\comfield$.} At this point, since $\bitstext(\bff)$ was committed over $\comfield$ (in packed form), one can prove \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} (or the batched version of it \eqref{e:intro_eq_5_batched}) via known methods such as those in \cite{binius,binius2,flock}. Concretely, we use the so-called ring switch from \cite{binius,binius2} to turn \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} into linear claims about the packed binary field elements $\pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$ we encoded, and then use WHIR or Ligerito \cite{whir,Ligerito} over $\comfield$ to prove these claims. 


\bigskip       

 
    In \cref{s:instantiation} we discuss how to efficiently instantiate \ftwoz \ with $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$, and attain 100 or 128 bits of security.





\begin{remark}[Description of Step 3]\label{r:large_prime_field_in_BitZ_PCS}
    Assume the condition $|\FF_q|< \fieldboundsizeexponent$ does not hold. Then, in Step 3, the prover sends an integer $\mu'\in \ZZ$ such that
    %
    \lncsmath{\langle  \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})\otimes \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}), \bitstext(\bff)\rangle = \mu' \qquad \text{in } \ZZ.}    
    The verifier checks that  $\pi_q(\mu')=\mu$, and responds with a random prime $q'$ from a public set $\cP$ of primes, all smaller than $\fieldboundsizeexponent$. Then, using the fingerprinting technique \cite{zaratan},  the prover and verifier project the above claim  to the field $\FF_{q'}$ (i.e.\ they apply $\pi_{q'}$ on both sides), so that it becomes
        \begin{equation}\label{e:to_projected_claim}\langle  \pi_{q'}\circ \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})\otimes  \pi_{q'}\circ\pi^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}),  \pi_{q'}(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle = \pi_{q'}(\mu') \ \text{in } \FF_{q'}, \qquad f = \Enc_\cC\circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)).\end{equation}  
        Then the prover and verifier proceed to Step 4 after updating $\FF_{q} \gets \FF_{q'}$, $\pi\gets \pi_{q'}$, $\mu \gets \pi_{q'}(\mu')$, and $\uu\gets \pi_{q'}\circ \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \pi_{q'}\circ \pi^{-1}(\uu^{(2)})$.
        
    To ensure soundness of the projection step, the verifier checks that $\mu'\in \range{0}{\codedim\cdot (q-1)^2}$  (see \cref{s:to_soundness_rings}).
\end{remark}


\begin{remark}[Succinctness of the verifier and the proof size]
Throughout the above construction, the verifier handles only vectors of size $\codedim_1$ and $\codedim_2$, hence, by setting $\codedim_1$ and $\codedim_2$ approximately of size $\sqrt{\codedim}$, we manage to keep the verifier and proof size succinct. We leave as an open problem making these asymptotically polylogarithmic, instead of square root sized. 
\end{remark}
%\paragraph{Costs.} Concrete costs are provided in \cref{?}. In short, the prover operations are comprised of: encoding  the vector $\pack\circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))\in \comfield^{\codedim/\comfieldexp}$, and computing a Merkle tree for the resulting word (Step 1); the operations required in the GKR grand product protocol and its subsequent possible batching steps (which we optimize in \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}) (half Step 5); the operations used by the ring-switch \cite{binius2} and the PCS, WHIR or Ligerito in our instantiation (Step 6); and the operations from Steps 2 to half of Step 5. The latter involve a linear amount of operations over $\ZZ$ and over $\comfield$.

\end{lncsmove}
\begin{lncsmove}{WhyTensor}
\iflncs\begin{remark}[Why the tensor split]\label{s:why_the_tensor_split}\else\subsubsection{Why the tensor split}\label{s:why_the_tensor_split}\fi{} The claim $\langle \uu, \pi_q(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle = \mu$ (cf.\  \iflncs\eqref{e:core_bitz_eq_new_0}\else\eqref{e:intro_eq_2}\fi) over $\FF_q$ is split, in \iflncs Round 2\else Step 4\fi, into $\codedim_2$ claims over $\ZZ$ of the form 
$
\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle =\mu_j$ for all  $j\in [\codedim_2]$ (cf.\ \iflncs\eqref{e:tensored_equality}\else\eqref{e:intro_eq_3}\fi).  This pays off in three ways:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
    \item All $\codedim_2$ grand products in \iflncs Phase 3 of \cref{c:core_iop}\else\eqref{e:intro_eq_4}\fi{} share the same $\codedim_1$ public values $g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu_i^{(1)})}$. In total, there are $\codedim$ factors, but at most $\codedim_1+1\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$ distinct values. This is crucial for performance when proving the grand products (see \cref{s:to_low_entropy_gkr}).
    \item The verifier only handles vectors of length $\codedim_1 \approx \sqrt{\codedim}$ and $\codedim_2\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$, instead of vectors of length $\codedim$, allowing for succinctness. 
    \item Each $\mu_j$ belongs to the range $\range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$ instead of $\range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim}$. Hence prime fields of size up to $(|\comfield|-1)/\codedim_1$, instead of $(|\comfield|-1)/\codedim$, fit in the exponent of $g$ without overflow. This difference is important in practice when seeking to instantiate \ftwoz\ at 128 bits of security while using $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$, and not paying too much on grinding, cf.\ \cref{s:instantiation}. % 
\end{itemize}

% [AI-drafted (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-15: sentence explaining $D$ and $N_R$, at the author's request; please review]
\Cref{t:core_iop_complexity} in the appendix records the concrete costs of this protocol\iflncs\else, stated for the general setting of \cref{s:to_general_rings}\fi.
\iflncs\end{remark}\fi



\end{lncsmove}
\begin{lncsmove}{Rings}
\subsection{Generalization to pairs of arbitrary finitely generated rings}\label{s:to_general_rings}






\paragraph{Finitely generated rings.} A ring $S$ is \emph{finitely generated} if  there exist elements $1, \eta_1, \ldots, \eta_k\in S$ such that every element $s\in S$ can be written in the form \begin{equation}\label{e:f_g_ring_element}s =  \sum_{a_1,\ldots, a_k\geq 0} s_{a_1,\ldots, a_k} \cdot \eta_1^{a_1}\cdots \eta_k^{a_k}\end{equation}  for some elements $s_{a_1,\ldots, a_k} \in \range{0}{\Char(S)-1}_S$, where $\Char(S)$ is the characteristic of $S$\footnote{The characteristic of a ring  is the minimum $n$ such that $1$ added together $n$ times equals $0$; or $\Char(S)=0$ if such $n$ does not exist.}, and for an integer $N\geq 0$, $\range{0}{N}_S$  is the set of elements from $S$ that can be obtained by adding $1$ together at most $N$ times.  If $N=0$ we use the convention that $\range{0}{N}_S=\ZZ$. If $S$ is infinite then \eqref{e:f_g_ring_element} must have finite support (i.e.\ only a finite number of summands can be nonzero).


For example, if $S$ is a field extension of size $q^e$ with $e> 1$, then $k_S=1$ and $\eta_1 = X$ (where we look at elements from the extension field as polynomials). If $S$ is a prime field or the integers $\ZZ$, then $k_S=0$ and the only generator is $1$. 

\paragraph{\ftwoz, general construction.} Let $S$ be a finitely generated ring. For example, $S$ could be a finite field extension, or the ring of integers $\ZZ$, or a cyclotomic ring. Let $R$ be another finitely generated ring, and let \lncsmath{\psi: S\to R} be a ring homomorphism. For example, we could simply have $S=R$ be a finite field, and $\psi$ be the identity (this is the setting in the previous \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}).  Or, in the fingerprinting approach to proving integer constraints, one would take $S=\ZZ$, $R$ a prime field, and $\psi$ reduction modulo $q$. In Zinc+ \cite{zincplus}, $S$ is a polynomial ring, $R$ is a prime field, and $\psi$ is a certain projection. 

Assume now that the prover claims knowledge of a vector $\bff\in S^{\codedim_0}$ such that
 \begin{equation}\label{e:new_to_0_rings}\langle \vv, \psi(\bff)\rangle =\mu \qquad \text{in  } R,
\end{equation}
where $\vv\in R^{\codedim_0}, \mu\in R$ are public.  



\paragraph{Size bounds.} For soundness reasons\footnote{There are also formalization difficulties when allowing arbitrary witness elements in an infinite ring. For example, no polynomial time extractor can output an exponentially sized witness.}, when $S$ is infinite, we cannot allow a prover to use arbitrarily large witnesses, and we  restrict the set of allowable witnesses to a publicly specified finite subset of $S$ (this is standard practice, see e.g.\ \cite{zaratan,zinc,qaren,limber,zincplus}). We explain how next. 

Let $B,D>0$ be public size parameters.  Intuitively, we want the prover to prove knowledge of a witness that can be written as polynomial expressions on $\eta_1, \ldots, \eta_{k_S}$ of total degree less than $D$, with coefficients in $\range{0}{2^B-1}_S$. We denote the set of such elements by $S_{\leq B}^{<D}$. Formally,
 %
 $$
S_{\leq B}^{<D}\defeq \left\{\left.\sum_{\substack{\aa\in \ZZ_{\geq 0}^k,\\  \aa_1+\ldots+\aa_k<D} } s_{\aa}\cdot \eta_1^{\aa_1}\cdots\eta_k^{\aa_k} \ \right| \ s_{\aa}\in \range{0}{2^B-1}_S\right\}
 $$
 % 
  If $S$ is finite to begin with, we can simply take $B,D$ so that $S_{\leq B}^{<D}=S$, or we may prefer to  restrict witnesses to a subset of $S$ anyway.


 \paragraph{Step 1: Commitment in $\comfield$.}  As before, the prover creates a vector $\bitstext(\bff) \in \bits^{\codedim} \subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim}$ of integer bits, for some $\codedim$, with $\bitstext(\bff)$ being linearly related to $\bff$ in a manner we explain next. Given $s\in S_{\leq B}^{<D}$ with $s = \sum_{\aa} s_\aa \cdot \eta_1^{a_1}\cdots \eta_{k}^{a_k}$, where the sum runs over the set $I$ of tuples $\aa\in \ZZ_{\geq 0}^k$ with $a_1+\ldots+a_k<D$, write $s_\aa =\sum_{i\in [B]} 2^{i-1}\cdot s_{\aa,i}$ for $s_{\aa,i}\in \{0,1\}$, so that
%
\lncsmath{
s= \sum_{\aa\in I} \sum_{i\in [B]} s_{\aa,i}\cdot 2^{i-1}\cdot  \eta_1^{a_1}\cdots \eta_{k}^{a_k}.
}
%
Then define $\bitstext(s)=(s_{\aa,i})_{i \in [B], \aa\in I}$, and we let $\bitstext(\bff)\in\bits^{\codedim}\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ be the vector obtained by concatenating $\bitstext(\bff_i)$ for $i\in [\codedim_0]$. We have $\codedim=\codedim_0\cdot B\cdot k$.  Note that 
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:new_to_linear_bits_to_f}
\bff = L \cdot \pi_{\Char(S)}(\bitstext(\bff))
\end{equation}
for certain matrix $L\in S^{\codedim_0 \times \codedim}$, where $\pi_{\Char(S)}(\bitstext(\bff))$ means that we look at the bits in $\bitstext(\bff)$ as elements in $S$. Concretely, $L= \mathsf{Id}_{\codedim_0} \otimes (1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1}) \otimes (\eta_1^{a_1}\cdots \eta_k^{a_k})_{\aa\in I}$. Note that by definition of $S_{\leq B}^{<D}$ and $L$,  \begin{equation}\label{e:closure_bitification}L\cdot \bfg \in S_{\leq B}^{<D} \qquad \text{for all } \bfg \in \bits^{\codedim_0}.\end{equation} This is required to guarantee that extractors output vectors with entries in  $S_{\leq B}^{<D}$.

We remark that this is not the only way one can transform $\bff$ into a vector of bits. Especially if $\bff$ contains elements with heterogeneous bit-sizes, a different bit-representation strategy is preferable, to avoid $\bitstext(\bff)$ being unnecessarily large, see \cref{r: non_uniform_bit_decomposition} for more comments on this bitification step. In any case, one always needs to make sure that a closure property such as \eqref{e:closure_bitification} is satisfied.
 


\bigskip

     \noindent Next, the prover commits to $\bitstext(\bff)$ in packed form exactly as in Step 1 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}. From here on, the prover and verifier set out to prove an instance of the following relation, which we call LinBitsRings:
%
    \begin{align*}
 &\LinModFieldBin(S, R, \psi) = \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\mu, \vv)\in R  \times R^{ \codedim_0}, \\
 &\oracle = f \in \comfield^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit= \bff\in (S_{\leq B}^{<D})^{\codedim_0}\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned}  
 &f = \Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)),\\
 &\langle \psi(\bff), \vv \rangle = \mu  \qquad  \text{in } R
 \end{aligned} \right\}.
\end{align*}

\begin{remark}[Integrated range checks]\label{r:integrated_range_checks}
    Note that $\LinModFieldBin(S, R, \psi) $ includes, in its definition, a restriction of $\bff$ into a finite subset of $S$, namely $S_{\leq B}^{<D}$. This means that a proof for $\LinModFieldBin(S, R, \psi)$ ensures that $\bff$ belongs to such a subset. Thus the \ftwoz\ scheme described below also proves such membership constraint. When $S$ is, say, a finite field or the integers, membership to $S_{\leq B}^{<D}$ is simply a range check (the $<D$ is vacuous in this case). 

\iflncs\else % LNCS: hidden
    Moreover, it is possible to add other heterogeneous subset membership checks by using a non-standard bitification matrix, see \cref{r: non_uniform_bit_decomposition}. One, however, needs to make sure that $L$ admits sufficient tensor structure, for efficiency purposes.

    The cost of these membership checks comes for free once one is willing to commit separately to the bits comprising the witness, as we do. See \cref{s:to_soundness_rings} for an explanation of how the extractor outputs witnesses satisfying the membership check.
\fi
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Step 2: Bitification of the linear claim.} Similarly as in Step 2 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}, the claim \eqref{e:new_to_0_rings} is replaced by a claim of knowledge of a bit vector  $\bitstext(\bff)$ such that \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_2_rings}\langle \uu, \psi(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle =\mu \ \text{in  } R, \qquad f = \Enc_\cC\circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)),
\end{equation} 
for a new $\uu\in R^{\codedim}$. This transformation is done using the linear expression from \eqref{e:new_to_linear_bits_to_f}. Concretely, we have $$\begin{aligned}\langle \vv, \psi(\bff)\rangle &= \langle \vv, \psi( L \cdot \bitstext(\bff)) \rangle = \langle \psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv, \psi(\bitstext(\bff)) \rangle = \langle \uu, \psi(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle, \\ \uu&\defeq \psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv \in R^{\codedim}.\end{aligned}$$ 
As before, it is important that $L$ is such that the new vector $\uu$ still admits a tensor decomposition $\uu=\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}$ with, say, $\uu^{(1)}\in R^{\codedim_1}$ and $\uu^{(2)}\in R^{\codedim_2}$ and $\codedim_1\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$, $\codedim_2\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$. This is the case for the example $L$ given above. For such matrix $L$ we have
%
$$\begin{aligned} \uu&=\vv\otimes (1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1})\otimes (\psi(\eta_1^{a_1}\cdots \eta_k^{a_k}))_{\aa\in I},\end{aligned}$$ 
%
which admits flexible tensor decompositions if $\vv$ does.
%


 \paragraph{Step 3 (Optional):  Replacing $R$ by a random ``small'' field $\FF_q$.}  Our next goal is to lift the claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_2_rings}, i.e.\ $\langle \uu, \psi(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle =\mu$ over $R$, to a claim ``in the exponent'' of a generator $g$ of $\comfield^*$. This time, however, there is a priori no natural lifting procedure, since, in intuitive terms, the exponent of $g$ can only accommodate integers or integers modulo $|\comfield|-1$ (the order of $g$), while $\langle \uu, \psi(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle$ is a sum of elements from an arbitrary ring $R$.

 Because of this, the prover and verifier undergo a randomization step where the ring $R$ is replaced by a random ``small'' prime field $\FF_q$. This step can be skipped if:
 \begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
     \item $R=\FF_q$ is a prime field with $q<\fieldboundsizeexponent$, i.e.\ $R$ is a ``small'' field,
     \item $R=\ZZ$ and all entries of $\uu^{(1)}$ are nonnegative and smaller than $\fieldboundsizeexponent$ (cf.\ \cref{r:skip_round_1_integers}),
 \end{itemize}
since in that case there is already a natural and sound lift of the claim $\langle \uu, \psi(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle =\mu$ to the exponent of $g$. The bound $\fieldboundsizeexponent$ ensures that the integer claims of Step 4 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz} cannot overflow $|\comfield|-1$, even if the claim is false (see \cref{s:to_soundness_prime}).

\bigskip 

\noindent The step proceeds as follows: 
suppose $R$ is generated by $1, \gamma_1, \ldots, \gamma_{k'}$. The prover computes and sends $\mu' \in \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k'}]$ (the ring of polynomials with integer coefficients and on variables $X_1,\ldots, X_{k'}$) such that
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim}
\langle \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}) , \bitstext(\bff) \rangle = \mu' \qquad \text{in } \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k'}],
\end{equation}
where $\picanon: \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k'}] \to R$ is the canonical ring homomorphism sending $X_i$ to $\gamma_i$ for all $i\in [k']$, and sending $1\in \ZZ$ to the multiplicative identity of $R$ (also denoted $1$). The inverse $\picanon^{-1}$ is a natural inverse of $\picanon$ (there are several ways to choose it), see \cref{e:presentations}.


%of an element $r\in R$ is obtained as follows: write $r= p(\gamma_1,\ldots, \gamma_{k'})$ for some polynomial $p(X_1,\ldots, X_{k'})$ with coefficients in $\range{0}{\Char(R)-1}$. Then $\picanon^{-1}(r)= \pi^{-1}(p)(X_1,\ldots, X_{k'})$, where $\pi^{-1}(p)$ is obtained by lifting all coefficients of $p$ to the integers. 

%
The verifier checks that $\mu'$ has total degree at most $D_\ZZ$ and coefficients of absolute value at most $2^{B_\ZZ}$, where $D_\ZZ$ and $2^{B_\ZZ}$ are the largest total degree and the largest absolute value of a coefficient attainable by the left-hand side of \eqref{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim}, among all bit vectors $\bitstext(\bff)$ (see \cref{s:to_soundness_rings}, and Round 1 of \cref{c:core_iop} for concrete expressions).  Then the verifier checks that $\picanon(\mu')=\mu$.



Now the idea is that the claim \eqref{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim} implies the claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_2_rings}, since $\picanon(\mu')=\mu$. Hence, the prover and the verifier  proceed to certify  \eqref{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim}. The constraint \eqref{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim} is a polynomial constraint (in fact, a linear constraint) over the ring $\ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k'}]$. We now generalize the fingerprinting technique for polynomial rings from Zinc+ \cite{zincplus} (which in turn generalizes that of Zaratan \cite{zaratan}), so as to project \eqref{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim} to a random ``small'' prime field.  

Concretely,  the verifier samples a random prime $q$ and a random morphism
%
$
\pirand : \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k'}] \to \FF_q
$. The protocol specifies a public set $\cP$ of samplable primes, and for each $q\in \cP$ a public set of samplable morphisms $\cM_q$. We require that all samplable primes $q$ satisfy $q< \fieldboundsizeexponent$. In our instantiation we let $\cM_q$ be the set of homomorphisms of the form $\pi(h(X_1,\ldots, X_{k'})) = \pi_q(h)(\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_{k'})$ for $\alpha_1,\ldots, \alpha_{k'}\in \FF_q$.

Now, the claim \eqref{e:new_to_lifted_polring_claim}  is replaced by the claim
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:new_to_projected_lifted_polring_claim}
\begin{aligned}
&\langle \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}) , \pirand(\bitstext(\bff)) \rangle = \pirand(\mu') \ \text{in } \FF_q,\\ &f = \Enc_\cC\circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
(Note that since $\bitstext(\bff)$ is full of bits, $\pirand(\bitstext(\bff))$ is simply an interpretation of these bits as the elements $0,1$ of $\FF_q$). 

Now the prover and verifier set out to prove \eqref{e:new_to_projected_lifted_polring_claim}. Recall that the prover has also sent a commitment to $\Enc_\cC\circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$. Hence we are exactly in the same setting as in Step 4 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}. Accordingly, at this point, the prover and verifier apply Steps 4, 5 and 6 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}, taking 
\begin{align*}
\uu^{(1)}{} &\gets \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}\left(\uu^{(1)}\right)\in \FF_{q}^{\codedim_1},\\
\uu^{(2)}{} &\gets \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}\left(\uu^{(2)}\right)\in \FF_{q}^{\codedim_2},\\
\mu&\gets \pirand(\mu')\in \FF_q.
\end{align*}


\end{lncsmove}
\begin{lncsmove}{GKR}
\subsection{GKR for low-entropy batched grand products}\label{s:to_low_entropy_gkr}
  In \iflncs Phase 3 of \cref{c:core_iop}\else Step 5 of \ftwoz \ (\cref{s:to_simple_bitz})\fi{} the prover must prove that \iflncs, for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$,\fi
                    \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_4_repeated}\iflncs\else g^{\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bitstext(\bff)_{*j}\rangle} =\fi \prod_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \left(\left(g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}_i)} -1\right)\cdot \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))_{ij}  +1\right) =g^{\mu_j} \qquad \text{in  } \comfield \iflncs\else\qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2].\fi
        \end{equation} 
        %
        As we mentioned, this is by far the most expensive step in \ftwoz. We next explain how we prove \eqref{e:intro_eq_4_repeated} in an efficient manner. We make the crucial  assumption that  both $\codedim_1$ and $\codedim_2$ are $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ (see \cref{s:instantiation} for exact parameter instantiations). This assumption is relatively simple to attain, as we have discussed throughout \cref{s:intro,s:tech_overview}. At a protocol level, we simply prove \eqref{e:intro_eq_4_repeated} with a standard GKR protocol for batched grand products \cite{thaler13}. Assume $\codedim_1=2^{r_1}$ and $\codedim_2=2^{r_2}$ are powers of two. We set up multilinear polynomials $L_{0}, L_1,\ldots, L_{r_1}$ on $r_2 + i$ variables for $i\in \range{0}{r_1}$, respectively, such that 
        %
\iflncs\begingroup\small\fi
                $$
        L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc) = \left(g^{\pi^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}_{\mathsf{int}(\bb)})} -1\right)\cdot \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))_{\mathsf{int}(\bb),\mathsf{int}(\cc)}  +1  \quad  \text{ for all } (\bb,\cc) \in \bits^{r_1} \times \bits^{r_2},
        $$
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
        and, for $i=0,\ldots, r_1-1$
      \begin{equation}\label{e:to_gkr_polys}
  L_{i}(\bb,\cc)  =  L_{i+1}(\bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{i+1}(\bb,1, \cc)  \quad \text{for all } (\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{i} \times \bits^{r_2}.
    \end{equation}
    We then use a series of sumchecks to certify \cref{e:to_gkr_polys} for all $i=0,\ldots, r_1-1$, and $L_{0}(\cc) = g^{\mu_{\mathsf{int}(\cc)}}$ for all $\cc\in \bits^{r_2}$.  At the end of the $i$-th sumcheck we are left with two evaluation claims for $L_{r_1-i}$ on different points. These two claims are transformed into a single claim via a ($1$-round) sumcheck.


    We emphasize again that, at a protocol level, this GKR scheme is completely standard. It is described in full detail in  \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}. 
    
    
    At the end of the GKR grand product, the prover and verifier are left with MLE evaluation claims of the form:
    %
    \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_grand_product_eq_1}
    \mle{g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})}}(\rr) = a,\qquad \mle{\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)_{*j})}(\rr)=a_j \qquad \text{for all } j \in [\codedim_2] \quad \text{over } \comfield,
    \end{equation}
    for some $\rr\in \comfield^{r_1}$, where $\mle{*}$ denotes MLE, and $g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})}$ is the vector with entries $g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}_i)}$. 
    
    Since $\uu$ is public, the verifier checks the first claim of \eqref{e:intro_grand_product_eq_1} on its own in time $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$, leveraging its tensor decomposition. \iflncs\else \fi


    
    We design prover optimizations for the GKR grand product (without altering the protocol itself), see \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy} for details. These adapt known techniques (fixed-base windowed exponentiation, and sumcheck optimizations from \cite{sumcheck_opt_1,sumcheck_opt_2}) to the following property, which is enabled, again, by the tensor split of $\uu=\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}$ and splitting the original claim into a batch of $\codedim_2$ claims:
    \medskip

    \begin{quote}\emph{\textbf{Low entropy grand products:}} There are at most $\codedim_1=O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ values that $L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)$ can take on the hypercube of size $\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2 = \codedim$. \end{quote}

     

    This property trickles down to different stages of the GKR protocol. For example, the above property implies that $L_{r_1-1}(\bb',\cc)$ as well can take only an $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ amount of values (with worse constants now) across all $(\bb',\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-1}\times \bits^{r_2}$. Similarly, the coefficients of the first messages sent by the prover at the last stages of the GKR protocol (which involve the most expensive sumchecks), also benefit from a low entropy property. 


            This allows the prover to compute small lookup tables that are kept in cache and reused across all values of $\cc\in [\codedim_2]$. We build different lookup tables, which are used at different stages of the GKR protocol.  For example, in the last sumcheck of GKR (the most expensive one, involving $r_1-1+r_2$ variables) the prover uses $4$ lookup tables to compute its first message, $5$ more tables to compute its second message, and one last table to compute its third message (see \cref{tab:gkr_tables}). Similarly, we build lookup tables for the first rounds of the second and third last sumchecks of the GKR protocol. This  configuration was found through an optimization process that was run with AI.

\paragraph{Example.} To give an idea of the type of optimizations we use, we explain how the prover computes its first message in the last sumcheck of the GKR protocol (the most expensive one). Further details are in \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}. This sumcheck certifies a claim of the form
\[
C=\sum_{(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-1}\times \bits^{r_2}} L_{r_1}(\bb,0,\cc)\cdot L_{r_1}(\bb,1,\cc)\cdot \eq(\bb,\cc;\bm{\alpha}),
\]
where $\bm{\alpha}\in \comfield^{r_1-1+r_2}$ contains verifier challenges. 
%
The first prover message is the degree-$3$ polynomial
$
h_1(X)=\sum_{\cc\in \bits^{r_2}} h_{1,\cc}(X)\cdot \eq(X,\cc;\bm{\alpha}_2)$ where  \[h_{1,\cc}(X)\defeq \sum_{\bb\in \bits^{r_1-2}} L_{r_1}(X,\bb,0,\cc)\cdot L_{r_1}(X,\bb,1,\cc)\cdot \eq(\bb;\bm{\alpha}_1),
\]
for some split of the verifier challenges $\bm{\alpha}=(\bm{\alpha}_1,\bm{\alpha}_2)\in \comfield^{r_1-2} \times \comfield^{r_2+1}$. Now we explain how the prover computes the constant coefficient of $h_{1,\cc}(X)$ (the other coefficients are computed similarly). This coefficient is
\[
