h_{1,\cc}(0)=\sum_{\bb\in \bits^{r_1-2}} A_0[\bb,\cc], \quad \text{where} \quad A_0[\bb,\cc]\defeq L_{r_1}(0,\bb,0,\cc)\cdot L_{r_1}(0,\bb,1,\cc)\cdot \eq(\bb;\bm{\alpha}_1).
\]
Now, write $y_{\bb'}\defeq g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}_{\mathsf{int}(\bb')})}$ for $\bb'\in \bits^{r_1}$. Since $\bitstext(\bff)$ is full of bits, each factor $L_{r_1}(0,\bb,\eps,\cc)$ is either $y_{(0,\bb,\eps)}$ (if the bit $\bitstext(\bff)_{\mathsf{int}(0,\bb,\eps),\mathsf{int}(\cc)}$ is $1$) or simply $1$ (if the bit is $0$). This means that, for a fixed $\bb\in \bits^{r_1-2}$, $A_0[\bb,\cc]$ takes only $4$ different values as $\cc$ ranges over $\bits^{r_2}$, one for each combination of the two bits $x=\bitstext(\bff)_{\mathsf{int}(0,\bb,0),\mathsf{int}(\cc)}$ and $x'=\bitstext(\bff)_{\mathsf{int}(0,\bb,1),\mathsf{int}(\cc)}$. Because of this, the prover precomputes a small table $T_{A_0}\in (\comfield^{4})^{2^{r_1-2}}$ with these $4$ values for each $\bb$, namely
\[
T_{A_0}[\bb]\defeq \Big( y_{(0,\bb,0)}^{\,x}\cdot y_{(0,\bb,1)}^{\,x'}\cdot \eq(\bb;\bm{\alpha}_1)\Big)_{(x,x')\in \bits^2} \in \comfield^4 \qquad \text{for } \bb\in \bits^{r_1-2},
\]
so that $A_0[\bb,\cc]$ is simply the entry $T_{A_0}[\bb][x,x']$. Building $T_{A_0}$ takes $\approx \codedim_1 \approx O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ multiplications in $\comfield$, once for all $\cc$. After that, the prover obtains the constant coefficients of all the $h_{1,\cc}(X)$ with $\codedim/4$ table reads and $\codedim/4$ additions in $\comfield$, and no multiplications at all. See \cref{t:gkr_step1_costs} for the cost of the rest of coefficients of $h_1(X)$. The rest of prover messages in this sumcheck, and the first messages of the second and third last sumchecks, are computed similarly with larger tables (cf.\ \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}).
\lncsplace[\lncsfittables\lncsfloattables]{GkrRound1Costs}
%\fi
      





\end{lncsmove}
\begin{lncsappendix}{gkr-comparison}
\iflncs\section{Efficiency and comparison to other ``prove-in-the-exponent'' usages}\else\subsubsection{Efficiency and comparison to other ``prove-in-the-exponent'' usages}\fi\label{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev}


We next discuss other existing approaches to proving foreign field statements in the exponent of a generator of $\comfield^*$ (the multiplicative subgroup of a binary field), in the context of hash-based SNARKs. Notice that elliptic curve-based PCSs usually perform commitments and opening proofs in the exponent of a generator of a large elliptic curve subgroup.  

   
        
        
        
        %The tensor split allows us to replace the claim into $\codedim_2=|\vv^{(2)}|$ claims $\langle \vv^{(1)}, \ww_{*j}\rangle = \eta_j$ in $\ZZ$ for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$. These claims are then each proved ``in the exponent''. Since $\vv^{(1)}$ is common across all claims, and $\ww$ is filled with bits, the resulting grand products are low entropy. 
        
      
        \cite{binius64} on the other hand multiplies 64-bit integer witness in the exponent directly. More precisely, \cite{binius64} proves the identity $g^{\ww_{1i}\cdot \ww_{2i}} = g^{2^{64}\cdot \ww_{3i}} \cdot g^{\ww_{4i}}$ for $\ww_1, \ldots, \ww_4$ vectors of 64-bit integers (together with a small additional constraint to address possible overflow). This is done through a combination of LogUp* \cite{logupstar}, a GKR grand product (for relatively small products of $128$ factors), and the Frobenius automorphism trick. It does not seem possible to follow a similar optimization strategy to ours.
       

        We  emphasize again that \ftwoz \ supports proving operations of all kinds of ring elements (small and large integers, field elements, lattice ring elements, extension field elements), as opposed to \cite{binius64} where multiplication is limited to 64-bit integers.

        In \cref{s:experiments} we compare the performance of \ftwoz \ and Binius64 when multiplying integers.  






        
              \paragraph{Binius64} \cite{binius64} includes a protocol for proving statements of the form $\bff \circ \bfg = \bfh^{(1)} + 2^{64}\cdot \bfh^{(2)}$ for $\bff,\bfg,\bfh^{(1)}, \bfh^{(2)}$ being \emph{witness} vectors of $64$-bit integers, and $\circ$ is the Hadamard product. This is proved  in the exponent of $g\in \comfield^*$ (together with a small additional constraint to address possible overflow). This is done through a combination of LogUp* \cite{logupstar}, a GKR grand product (for relatively small products of $128$ factors), and the Frobenius automorphism trick. 
        
        On the other hand, \ftwoz \ only proves, in the exponent of $g$, statements of the form ``(public values)$\cdot$ (witness elements)'', i.e.\ $\langle \vv, \bff\rangle = \mu$ in $\ZZ$, with the public values $\vv$ admitting a tensor split $\vv=\vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)}$. In simple terms, Binius64 proves quadratic constraints in the exponent, while \ftwoz\ only proves linear constraints (with structured public values). Hence, \ftwoz\ allows to prove a much narrower set of integer constraints in the exponent of $\comfield$ as compared to Binius64 (though note Binius64 only natively handles 64-bit integers, which is narrow, but on a different axis), but with it gains substantial optimization opportunities, as we have already described. 
        
        A key observation of this work is that, \textbf{by initially running a PIOP over $\ZZ$ (or other rings), one can reduce any nonlinear constraint (over any ring) into a linear constraint over $\FF_q$ for a ``small'' $q$, which can then be proved in the exponent of $g$, using the optimizations described above.} This way we avoid  proving quadratic constraints in the exponent of $g$, while still being able to use binary fields when proving arbitrary constraints over arbitrary (f.g.) rings.
        \begin{comment}
        

        This simpler setting is what allows us to use the low entropy optimizations designed in this work.  Crucially, \textbf{the reason why we only need to address this setting is because we start off with constraints and a PIOP over integers/fields/rings (Steps 2 to 4 inclusive), and only ever move to work on binary fields when we need to prove an MLE evaluation of the witness (a linear claim) over a ``small'' prime field (Step 5)}. 

        
        
        %The tensor split allows us to replace the claim into $\codedim_2=|\vv^{(2)}|$ claims $\langle \vv^{(1)}, \ww_{*j}\rangle = \eta_j$ in $\ZZ$ for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$. These claims are then each proved ``in the exponent''. Since $\vv^{(1)}$ is common across all claims, and $\ww$ is filled with bits, the resulting grand products are low entropy. 
        
      
        \cite{binius64} on the other hand multiplies 64-bit integer witness in the exponent directly. More precisely, \cite{binius64} proves the identity $g^{\ww_{1i}\cdot \ww_{2i}} = g^{2^{64}\cdot \ww_{3i}} \cdot g^{\ww_{4i}}$ for $\ww_1, \ldots, \ww_4$ vectors of 64-bit integers (together with a small additional constraint to address possible overflow). This is done through a combination of LogUp* \cite{logupstar}, a GKR grand product (for relatively small products of $128$ factors), and the Frobenius automorphism trick. It does not seem possible to follow a similar optimization strategy to ours.
       

        We  emphasize again that \ftwoz \ supports proving operations of all kinds of ring elements (small and large integers, field elements, lattice ring elements, extension field elements), as opposed to \cite{binius64} where multiplication is limited to 64-bit integers.
\end{comment}
        
        In \cref{s:experiments} we compare the performance of \ftwoz \ and Binius64 when multiplying integers and on other computations.  


        
        \paragraph{The Fields-Witch note \cite{lev}.} In the note \cite{lev}, Soukhanov proposes a scheme, called Fields-Witch, for committing to a vector of bits $\bff$ over the binary field $\FF_{2^{127}}$ (in packed form), and proving MLE evaluation claims over the prime field  $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$, for a vector of elements from   $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$ obtained by packing bits from $\bff$ into elements from $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$. To do so, at a high level, the MLE evaluation claims are proved ``in the exponent'' in the binary field $\FF_{2^{127}}$, leveraging that  the multiplicative subgroup of  $\FF_{2^{127}}$ has prime order $|\FF_{2^{127}}^*| = 2^{127}-1$. The proposed approach is substantially different from ours: \cite{lev} essentially takes a polylogarithmic PCS over the prime field $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$, such as Basefold \cite{basefold}, WHIR \cite{whir}, etc.,  and executes it ``in the exponent of $\FF_{2^{127}}$''. To do so, it relies on similar tools as Binius64, namely the lookup argument LogUp* \cite{logupstar}, grand product arguments (for small products), the Frobenius trick, and additional commitments, for each of the $\log\ell$ rounds.  
        
        %In its execution,  \cite{lev} and Binius64 \cite{binius64} seem to us to be more similar than any of these two to \ftwoz. Indeed, they both use, mostly, the same collection of subprotocols, in a similar manner. 
        In \cref{tab:fields-witch} we compare \cite{lev} with \ftwoz. In \cref{s:experiments} we perform extensive comparisons between \ftwoz\ and Binius64.  % Because of this we expect both approaches to have comparable performance profiles, though we do not validate this hypothesis due to \cite{lev} not reporting performance nor providing an implementation.
        
        Conceptually, Fields-Witch \cite{lev} shares with \ftwoz \ the aim of committing to bits in a binary field, while proving MLE claims of those bits in some other structure $R$.  \cite{lev} is limited to $R=\FF_{2^{127}-1}$, while \ftwoz \ can handle arbitrary finitely generated rings. Even with this greater generality capacity,  we observe in \cref{s:exp_fields_witch}  that \ftwoz\ performs slightly better overall (and much better on proof size)  than \cite{lev} on the latter's native operation, i.e.\ proving MLE openings over the prime field $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$. However it is unclear to us whether the two implementations are at a comparable level of optimization. 
       %It is not clear to us how or if \cite{lev} can leverage  our low-entropy oriented optimizations without substantially modifying the approach, i.e.\ without running Basefold ``in-the-exponent''. 

        
\end{lncsappendix}
\begin{lncsappendix}{virtual}
\iflncs\subsection{Virtual $\FF_2$-linear transforms, NP-complete multi-domain linear relations, and hybrid proof systems}\label{s:to_f2_virtual}\else\subsection{Virtual $\FF_2$-linear transforms, NP-complete multi-domain linear relations, and hybrid proof systems}\label{s:to_f2_virtual}\fi

\iflncs We provide further details on how to handle virtual $\FF_2$-linear combinations of integer witness, expanding the corresponding part of \cref{s:intro_contributions}. \else\fi


Observe that \iflncs Phase 3 of \cref{c:core_iop}\else Step 5 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}\fi{} outputs a linear claim of the form\iflncs\else\footnote{We start with the batched version of the claim, but the ideas in this section can be adapted to \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} as well.}\fi
\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_5_repeated} \langle \zz, \pi_2(\bitstext(\ww)) \rangle = c \qquad \text{in  } \comfield,
\end{equation} 
for some $\zz\in \comfield^{\codedim}$ and $c\in \comfield$. This is a standard linear claim over $\comfield$ for the vector $\bitstext(\ww)$ which we committed to previously (in this subsection we denote the witness by $\ww$, to free the letter $\bff$). 


Now, say that we want to impose that $\bitstext(\ww)$ is a publicly specified linear transformation of a vector $\bff\in  \FF_2^{\codedim'} \subseteq  \comfield^{\codedim'}$, so that
%
\lncsmath{
\pi_2(\bitstext(\ww)) = M \cdot \bff \qquad \text{ in } \comfield,
}
for some matrix $M\in \FF_2^{\codedim\times \codedim'}$.
Then 
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_5_virtual}
 \langle \zz, \pi_2(\bitstext(\ww)) \rangle  =  \langle M^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \zz, \bff \rangle = c \qquad \text{in  } \comfield.
\end{equation}
Hence, in this scenario, one can commit to $\bff$ instead of $\bitstext(\ww)$ (again, in packed form if necessary),  replace the claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_5_repeated} by \eqref{e:intro_eq_5_virtual}, and prove the latter in \iflncs Phase 4 of \cref{c:core_iop}\else Step 6 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}\fi. 



Thus, \ftwoz\ can be expanded as a PCS in a larger proof system (like the one we describe in \cref{s:to_snark}) to prove claims where we are given a public vector $\inp=\xx\in R^\inplen$, and the prover claims  it knows $\wit=\bff\in \FF_2^{\codedim'}$ such that 

\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_0_virtual}
\begin{cases}
&\pp_i(\xx,\ww)=0 \qquad \text{in  } R \qquad \text{ for all } i\in [m], \\ &\bitstext(\ww)=\pi_2^{-1}(M\cdot \bff)
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
%
where  $\pp=(\pp_i)_{i\in [m]}$ is a collection of polynomials over $R$, and $\bitstext(\ww)$ and $\ww$ are related through a bitification matrix, as the witness in \iflncs\cref{d:general_linbitsrings}\else\cref{s:to_general_rings}\fi. 

A particularly interesting instantiation of this relation is the following R1CS-like relation over the integers, where $\ww$ is a vector of bits (hence $\ww=\bitstext(\ww)$) and $\ww= \pi_2^{-1}(M\cdot \bff)$:
%
\begin{align*}
&\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)\\ &\iflncs\else\qquad \qquad\qquad\fi{} \defeq \left\{ \begin{aligned}
\iflncs
    &\inp= (\xx, A,B,C, M)\\ &\qquad \in \FF_2^{\inplen} \times (\ZZ^{m\times \codedim})^3 \times \FF_2^{\codedim \times (\inplen+\codedim')}\\
\else
    &\inp= (\xx, A,B,C, M)\in \FF_2^{\inplen} \times (\ZZ^{m\times \codedim})^3 \times \FF_2^{\codedim \times (\inplen+\codedim')}\\
\fi
    & \wit = \bff \in \bits^{\codedim'} \subseteq \FF_2^{\codedim'}
\end{aligned}\  \left| \  \begin{aligned} %&f=\Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bff)\\ 
&(A\cdot \ww) \circ (B\cdot \ww) = C \cdot \ww\ \text{in } \ZZ \\
&\ww= \pi_2^{-1}\left(  M\cdot (\xx, \bff)\right)   \end{aligned} \right.\right\}
\end{align*}
%
An interesting subset  of $\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)$
 is that where $A$ and $B$ are zero matrices. We call the resulting relation the \emph{$\mathsf{CM}$ relation} because of the matrices $C$ and $M$:
 \begin{equation}\label{e:CM_rel}
\Relation_{\mathsf{CM}}(\ZZ) \defeq \left\{ \begin{aligned}
    &\inp= (\xx, C, M)\in \FF_2^{\inplen} \times \ZZ^{m\times \codedim} \times \FF_2^{\codedim \times (\inplen+\codedim')}\\
    & \wit = \bff \in \bits^{\codedim'} \subseteq \FF_2^{\codedim'}
\end{aligned}\  \left| \  \begin{aligned} %&f=\Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bff)\\ 
&C \cdot \ww = 0\ \text{in } \ZZ \\
&\ww= \pi_2^{-1}\left(  M\cdot (\xx, \bff)\right) \end{aligned} \right.\right\}
\end{equation}
%
In a sense, the CM relation places only linear constraints over its witness, with some of these  constraints being over $\comfield$, and others being over $\ZZ$. Yet, the CM relation is NP-complete, as shown below in \cref{r:CM_is_NP_complete}. 



\textbf{We arithmetize SHA-256 as a CM instance}. Our arithmetization leads to witnesses of $7144$ bits per compression, $4.4\times$ smaller than those of Binius64 \cite{binius64} and Flock \cite{flock} (see \cref{tab:sha_arithmetization}). However, as we observe in \cref{s:intro} and in \cref{s:experiments}, when one only aims to prove a large amount of hashes, staying purely on binary fields seems preferable. We prove SHA+ECDSA on a few KB message (requiring $\leq 2^8$ SHA compressions) by arithmetizing the computation in $\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)$, with SHA-256 being arithmetized over $\Relation_{\mathsf{CM}}(\ZZ)$. 

\lncsplace{ShaArith}
\begin{lemma}[The $\RELCM$ relation is NP-complete]\label{r:CM_is_NP_complete}
Any Boolean circuit can be arithmetized as an instance of $\RELCM$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Indeed, since any Boolean circuit can be written as a system of equations using the operations XOR and AND, it suffices to prove that, given \emph{integers} $x,y,z\in \bits\subseteq \ZZ$, we can express both of the following constraints as an instance of $\RELCM$:
%
\lncsmath{    x \xor y = z,\qquad \qquad 
    x \band y = z.
}
Now, $x \xor y = z$ if and only if $\pi_2^{-1}(\pi_2(x) + \pi_2(y))=z$, where here $+$ means addition modulo $2$. Further, $x \band y = z$  if and only if  
%
\lncsmath{
2\cdot z =  x+y - \pi_2^{-1}(\pi_2(x) + \pi_2(y)) \qquad \text{in } \ZZ.
}    
One can verify this last equivalence  by directly inspecting that it holds for the four possible combinations of $x,y\in \bits$.
\end{proof}





%\subsection{The $\mathsf{CM}$ constraint system: composition of  $\ZZ$-linear  with $\FF_2$-linear constraints}

%\begin{remark}
%
%    $\RELCM$ is related to PIMSAT and $\LinBitsDual^{\mathsf{virtual}}$ in the sense that $\RELCM$ is the result of taking  $\PIMSAT(\bits, \pp_{\mathsf{CM}}, \bot)$ (i.e.\ the PIMSAT relation with $R=\ZZ, \moduledegree=0, B=1$ and no ideals) and replacing the vector input-witness vector in PIMSAT by a vector of the form $\mathbf{h}= \pi_2^{-1}\left(\Mmatrix\cdot \pi_2(\bff)\right)$.
    %
%\end{remark}



\iflncs\subsection{\ftwoz \ for fully hybrid constraints over $\ZZ$ and $\comfield$}\label{s:hybrid_snark}\else\subsubsection{\ftwoz \ for fully hybrid constraints over $\ZZ$ and $\comfield$}\label{s:hybrid_snark}\fi



Say one wants to prove a computation that involves both  $\FF_2$ operations and other more integer-like operations, like integer multiplication or multiplication mod $2^{32}$. This is the case, for example, with Virtual Machines running RISC-V instructions. To prove these computations we use an arithmetization with constraints both in $\ZZ$ and in $\FF_2$. We build a SNARK for such constraints as follows (and we use it in our benchmarks for proving SHA-256 compressions and multiplication modulo $2^{32}$). Consider a witness $(\bff_\ZZ, \bff_{\FF_2})$ split into an integer vector $\bff_\ZZ\in \ZZ^{\codedim_\ZZ}$ used in the integer constraints, and an $\FF_2$ vector $\bff_{\FF_2}\in \FF_2^{\codedim_{\FF_2}}$ used in the $\FF_2$ constraints. We can also assume that the $\FF_2$ constraints  use some entries from $\bff_{\ZZ}$ which are integer bits (looking at those integer bits as elements from $\FF_2$), otherwise we would have two unrelated constraints. Accordingly we have $\bff_\ZZ=(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(1)}, \bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)})$, where $\bff_{\ZZ}^{(1)}$ is a vector of arbitrary integers, and $\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}$ is a vector of bits.

We want to prove that $(\inp_\ZZ, \bff_\ZZ)\in \Relation_\ZZ$ and $(\inp_{\FF_2}, \pi_2(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}), \bff_{\FF_2})\in \Relation_{\FF_2}$, where $\Relation_\ZZ$ and $\Relation_{\FF_2}$ are some relations over $\ZZ$ and $\FF_2$, respectively, and where $\pi_2(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)})$ denotes the vector of bits $\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}$ seen in $\FF_2$. The overall proof system proceeds as follows:  
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
        \item Commit to the concatenation of $\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff_{\ZZ}))=\pi_2((\bitstext(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(1)}), \bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}))$ and $\bff_{\FF_2}$ over a binary field $\comfield$ (in packed form).
        \item  Partially run the previously described \ftwoz-SNARK for the statement $(\inp_\ZZ, \bff_\ZZ)\in \Relation_\ZZ$. Run it until we have an inner product claim $\langle\zz, \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff_\ZZ))\rangle= \eta$  over $\comfield$. 
        \item Then partially run any SNARK for binary fields such as \cite{binius64}, for the claim $(\inp_{\FF_2}, (\pi_2(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}), \bff_{\FF_2}))\in \Relation_{\FF_2}$. Run it until we have an inner product claim $\langle\zz', (\pi_2(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}), \bff_{\FF_2})\rangle= \eta'$  over $\comfield$.
        \item At this point we have two inner product claims over $\comfield$ for $\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff_\ZZ))$ and $(\pi_2(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}), \bff_{\FF_2})$, and we committed to the vector  $(\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff_\ZZ^{(1)})),\pi_2(\bff_{\ZZ}^{(2)}), \bff_{\FF_2})$ (in packed form). Now we prove the two inner product claims  both in a batched manner using WHIR \cite{whir} or any other similar PCS over $\comfield$. 
        
        If necessary, the inner product claims can be reduced to MLE evaluation claims before, via a batched sumcheck.
    \end{enumerate}
\bigskip
\noindent We demonstrate the practicality of this approach by proving a large amount of chained SHA-256 compressions, together with a large amount of multiplications modulo $2^{32}$. The results can be found in \cref{tab:intro_table} and in \cref{s:experiments_sha_modmul}. 




\end{lncsappendix}
\iflncs\else\subsection{Round-by-round knowledge soundness}\label{s:to_soundness}\fi % LNCS: the label is re-attached to the soundness subsection placed as 3.4
\iflncs\else
 We provide an overview of why the constructions from \cref{s:to_simple_bitz,s:to_general_rings} are round-by-round knowledge sound.  
\fi



\begin{lncsmove}{SoundnessPrime}
\iflncs\section{Soundness of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}}\else\subsubsection{Soundness of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}}\fi\label{s:to_soundness_prime}




In \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP} of \cref{s:soundness_core_iop} we prove that the IOP $\Pi$ from \cref{s:to_simple_bitz} is perfectly complete and round-by-round knowledge sound. To do so we look at $\Pi$ as the composition of three Interactive Oracle Reductions (IORs, cf.\ \cite{BCFW25} and \cref{s:IORs}), which we call $\Pi_1,\Pi_2,\Pi_3$:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
\item $\Pi_1$ consists of Steps 2, 3, 4 and the first half of Step 5. It reduces the initial claim, i.e.\ knowledge of a witness for $(\inp,\oracle)=((\mu,\vv),f)\in \LANG(\LinBitsDual(\FF_q))$, to the grand product claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_4}. The latter is the claim that the prover knows $\bfg\in \FF_2^{\codedim_1\times \codedim_2}$ with $f=\Enc_\cC\circ \pack(\bfg)$ and $\prod_{i\in [\codedim_1]}\bigl((g^{\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}_i)}-1)\cdot \bfg_{ij}+1\bigr)=g^{\mu_j}$ for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$, which is an instance of the relation $\PESAT$ from \cref{d:pesat} (see \cref{s:interleaved_setting}).
\item $\Pi_2$ consists in applying the second half of Step 5, i.e.\ a standard GKR protocol for the $\codedim_2$ grand products\footnote{Our optimizations for the GKR prover (\cref{c:gkr_grand_product}) do not modify the transcripts, and so they require no soundness analysis.}, which reduces them to the linear claims \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} over $\comfield$ on the vectors $\bitstext(\bff)_{*j}$.
\item $\Pi_3$ consists in applying Step 6, which proves the latter claims with the ring switch of \cite{binius2} followed by WHIR or Ligerito \cite{whir,Ligerito}, as done in Flock \cite{flock}, possibly after a batched sumcheck that reduces the claims \eqref{e:intro_eq_5} to a single MLE evaluation claim on $\bitstext(\bff)$ over $\comfield$.
\end{itemize}
It is well known that $\Pi_2$ and $\Pi_3$ are perfectly complete and round-by-round knowledge sound\footnote{This holds in a relaxed sense: a malicious prover may convince the verifier with an oracle $f$ that is not exactly $\Enc_\cC\circ \pack(\bfg)$ for a valid witness $\bfg$, but is \emph{close} to it in Hamming distance. This is a well-studied technicality \cite{stark,BCFW25,whir,zook}, and we deal with it in \cref{s:IORs,s:soundness_core_iop} using the formalism of round-by-round knowledge soundness \emph{with relaxation} from \cite{zook}. We ignore it in this overview.}, and that composing round-by-round knowledge sound IORs gives again a round-by-round knowledge sound IOR (\cref{r:ior_compositoin}). Because of this, below we only discuss $\Pi_1$. 


\begin{remark}[The oracle serving as a commitment. Unique Decoding Radius and Johnson bound]\label{r:oracle_udr}For simplicity, we assume below that WHIR is configured with a proximity parameter within the unique decoding radius. In intuitive terms, this means that when the prover sends the oracle $f$, it is committing to a unique bit vector $\bitstext(\bff) \in \bits^{\codedim}$ determined by $f$.  One can alternatively configure WHIR up to the Johnson bound, as long as one performs an Out Of Domain sample in Step 1. 
\end{remark}


\bigskip

\noindent \emph{\underline{Round-by-round knowledge soundness of $\Pi_1$}}. The (possibly malicious) prover and verifier start with the public input $(\vv,\mu)$ and the oracle $f$.  Recall that the prover claims knowledge of $\bff\in \FF_q^{\codedim_0}$ such that $\langle \bff,\vv\rangle=\mu$ in $\FF_q$.  

 \emph{Step 1:} In Step 1 the prover commits to $\pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$ the witness and hence there is nothing to prove.
 
   \emph{Step 2 (Bitification of the linear claim):} After Step 2, the claim is  that the prover knows $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ such that
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:to_soundness_1}
\langle \uu, \pi_q(\bfg)\rangle = \mu \quad \text{in } \FF_q, \qquad \text{and} \qquad f= \Enc_\cC \circ \pack\circ \pi_2(\bfg).
\end{equation}
%
This step has no  knowledge soundness loss. Indeed, from any valid witness $\bfg$ for \eqref{e:to_soundness_1} one can efficiently obtain a witness $\bff\in \FF_q^{\codedim_0}$ with $((\vv,\mu), f, \bff)\in \LinBitsDual(\FF_q)$: it suffices to take $\bff_i=\sum_{j\in [B]} 2^{j-1}\cdot \pi_q(\bfg_{ij})$ for $i\in [\codedim_0]$ (with these operations taking place in $\FF_q$). By definition of $\uu$, such $\bff$ satisfies $\langle \bff,\vv\rangle=\mu$ over $\FF_q$.

\emph{Step 3.} Assume first that Step 3 does not trigger, so that $|\FF_q|<(|\comfield|-1)/\codedim_1$. Later we will discuss the case where this is otherwise.

\emph{Step 4 (transforming the single inner product claim to $\codedim_2$ many of them):} At the end of Step 4 the  claim has become that the prover knows $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ such that
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:to_soundness_2}
\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bfg_{*j}\rangle =\mu_j \quad \text{in  } \ZZ \quad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2], \qquad \text{and} \qquad f= \Enc_\cC \circ \pack\circ \pi_2(\bfg).
\end{equation}
%
Again, we justify that given a valid witness $\bfg$ for \eqref{e:to_soundness_2}, the exact same witness $\bfg$ satisfies \eqref{e:to_soundness_1}, and hence this step also incurs no knowledge soundness loss. Indeed, 
 if \eqref{e:to_soundness_2} holds and the verifier's checks pass, then 
$$
\langle \uu,\pi_q(\bfg)\rangle = \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi_q\bigl(\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bfg_{*j}\rangle\bigr)\cdot \uu^{(2)}_j = \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi_q(\mu_j)\cdot \uu_j^{(2)}=\mu.
$$
%
Hence \eqref{e:to_soundness_1} holds for the same  witness $\bfg$.


\emph{First half of Step 5:} Finally, the first half of Step 5 replaces \eqref{e:to_soundness_2} by the grand product claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_4}. If the latter holds for some $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$, then $\langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}), \bfg_{*j}\rangle \equiv \mu_j \pmod{\mathsf{ord}(g)}$, where $\mathsf{ord}(g)=|\comfield|-1$. Since $\bfg$ has bit entries and $\mu_j$ passed the range check of the verifier, both sides lie in $\range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$. Further,  $(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1<|\comfield|-1$ by our assumption on $q$, so the congruence is an equality over $\ZZ$ and so \eqref{e:to_soundness_2} holds. 

Hence, if Step 3 does not trigger, then $\Pi_1$ is a one-round IOR with  round-by-round knowledge soundness error $0$ (the verifier sends no message during this round, hence the round can be glued together with the next one in \cref{s:soundness_core_iop}).


\emph{Step 3.} Assume Step 3 triggers (\cref{r:large_prime_field_in_BitZ_PCS}). Then the protocol has one extra round with a nonzero round-by-round knowledge soundness error. The prover sends $\mu'\in \range{0}{\codedim\cdot (q-1)^2}$ with $\pi_q(\mu')=\mu$, and the verifier samples a random prime $q'\in\cP$.

In this case, at the end of Step 3 the claim \eqref{e:to_soundness_1} has been replaced by a similar claim over the randomly sampled prime field $\FF_{q'}$, namely the claim that the prover knows $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim} \subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$  such that $$\langle \pi_{q'}\circ\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})\otimes \pi_{q'}\circ\pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}), \pi_{q'}(\bfg)\rangle=\pi_{q'}(\mu')$$ in $\FF_{q'}$, and such that $f = \Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ\pi_2(\bfg)$.

Let $\cE$ be the event that this latter claim holds for some $\bfg$, but \eqref{e:to_soundness_1} does not. Note that $\bfg$ is fixed before $q'$ is sampled: indeed, the oracle $f$ is the same in \eqref{e:to_soundness_1} and in \eqref{e:to_projected_claim}, and by \cref{r:oracle_udr} it determines $\bfg$. Now, if $\cE$ occurs, then the integer $h\defeq \langle \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(1)})\otimes \pi_q^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}), \bfg\rangle - \mu' \in \ZZ$ satisfies $\pi_q(h)=\langle \uu, \pi_q(\bfg)\rangle-\mu\neq 0$ (because \eqref{e:to_soundness_1} does not hold), and in particular $h\neq 0$. On the other hand $\pi_{q'}(h)=0$, i.e.\ $q'$ divides $h$. Since $\bfg$ has bit entries and $\mu'$ passed the verifier's range check, we have $|h|\leq 2\cdot \codedim\cdot (q-1)^2$. A nonzero integer of this size has at most $\log(2\codedim(q-1)^2)/\lfloor\log q_{\min}\rfloor$ prime divisors in $\cP$, where $q_{\min}$ is the smallest prime in $\cP$. Hence $\cE$ occurs with probability at most 
% 
\begin{equation}\label{e:to_kappa_0}
\kappa_0 \defeq \frac{\log\left(2\cdot \codedim\cdot (q-1)^2\right)}{\lfloor\log q_{\min}\rfloor\cdot |\cP|}
\end{equation}
%
over the choice of $q'$. 


After Step 3, we are exactly in the same scenario as at the beginning of Step 4 in the case that Step 3 did not trigger. Hence $\Pi_1$ is round-by-round knowledge sound with errors $(\kappa_0, 0)$ (or with error $0$ if Step 3 is skipped). 

Overall,  $\Pi$ has errors $\kappa_0$ followed by those of the GKR protocol and of the binary-field PCS (with prior possible batching sumcheck, followed by  ring switch).



\end{lncsmove}
\begin{lncsmove}{SoundnessRings}
\subsubsection{Soundness of \cref{s:to_general_rings}}\label{s:to_soundness_rings}\iflncs\label{s:to_soundness}\fi

The full soundness statement of \cref{s:to_general_rings} can be found in \cref{t:thm_LinModFieldBits}, and is proved in \cref{a:LinModFieldBin}. We provide an overview of it next.

As in \cref{s:to_soundness_prime}, we view the protocol as a composition of three IORs $\Pi_1, \Pi_2, \Pi_3$, with $\Pi_2$ and $\Pi_3$ being exactly the same as in \cref{s:to_soundness_prime}, i.e.\ $\Pi_2$ is the grand product GKR from Step 5, and $\Pi_3$ is the ring switch followed by a PCS  such as WHIR or Ligerito.  The IOR $\Pi_1$ consists of Steps 2 and 3 of \cref{s:to_general_rings} followed by Step 4 and the first half of Step 5 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}.

After Step 3, the prover and verifier are exactly in the same setting as in Step 4 of \cref{s:to_simple_bitz}. Hence the analysis of the construction after Step 3 is exactly the same as in \cref{s:to_soundness_prime}.  Because of this, below we only discuss Steps 1, 2 and 3.

\bigskip

\noindent\emph{\underline{Steps 1 and 2 (commitment and bitification)}.} The (possibly malicious) prover and the verifier receive $\inp=(\vv,\mu)\in R^{\codedim_0}\times R$ as input, and the prover sends an oracle $f\in \comfield^{\codelength}$. The initial claim is that the prover knows $\bff\in (S_{\leq B}^{<D})^{\codedim_0}$ such that $\langle \psi(\bff), \vv\rangle = \mu$ in $R$. As before, in Step 1, the prover commits to $\pack\circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$ and there is nothing to argue. In Step 2, the initial claim has been replaced by the claim that the prover knows $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ such that
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:to_soundness_1_rings}
\langle \uu, \psi(\bfg)\rangle = \mu \quad \text{in } R, \qquad \text{and} \qquad f= \Enc_\cC \circ \pack\circ \pi_2(\bfg),
\end{equation}
where $\uu=L^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv$, where $L$ is the public fixed bitification matrix. We argue that for any $\bfg$ satisfying \eqref{e:to_soundness_1_rings} the vector  $\bff\defeq L \cdot \bfg$ satisfies the initial claim. Indeed, we have $\langle \vv, \psi(\bff)\rangle = \langle \vv, \psi(L)\cdot \psi(\bfg)\rangle = \langle \uu, \psi(\bfg)\rangle  = \mu$. Moreover, we required when defining $L$ that $L\cdot \bfg$ has all entries in $S_{\leq B}^{<D}$  (as opposed to a different, possibly larger subset of $S$)  whenever $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}$. Hence  $\bff\in (S_{\leq B}^{<D})^{\codedim_0}$, as needed.  This shows that Step 2 incurs no knowledge soundness loss.

\bigskip 

\noindent\emph{\underline{Step 3}.}  In Step 3 the claim \eqref{e:to_soundness_1_rings} is replaced by
\begin{equation}\label{e:soundness_new_to_projected_lifted_polring_claim}
\begin{aligned}
&\langle \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}) , \pirand(\bitstext(\bff)) \rangle = \pirand(\mu'),\quad f = \Enc_\cC\circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Let $\cE$ be the event that \eqref{e:soundness_new_to_projected_lifted_polring_claim} holds for some $\bfg\in \bits^\codedim$ with $f= \Enc_\cC \circ \pack\circ \pi_2(\bfg)$, but \eqref{e:to_soundness_1_rings} does not hold. As in \cref{s:to_soundness_prime}, $\bfg$ is fixed by $f$ before $q,\pirand$ are sampled. Consider the polynomial
%
\lncsmath{
h\defeq \langle \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}) , \bfg \rangle-\mu' \ \in \ \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k'}].
}
%
Since $\picanon$ is a ring homomorphism with $\picanon\circ\picanon^{-1}=\mathsf{id}$ and $\picanon(\mu')=\mu$, we have $\picanon(h)=\langle \uu, \psi(\bfg)\rangle-\mu\neq 0$, and so $h\neq 0$. Since $\picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(2)})$ is public and $\bfg$ has bit entries, the verifier can compute an upper bound $D_\ZZ$ on the total degree of the polynomial $\langle \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(1)}) \otimes \picanon^{-1}(\uu^{(2)}) , \bfg \rangle$, and an upper bound $2^{B_\ZZ}$ on the absolute value of its coefficients (see Round 1 of \cref{c:core_iop} and \eqref{e:ring_theta_params} for concrete expressions). These are precisely the bounds that the verifier checks on $\mu'$ in Step 3 of \cref{s:to_general_rings}. Hence $h$ has total degree at most $D_\ZZ$, and coefficients of absolute value at most $2^{B_\ZZ+1}$. 

Now, since $\pirand$ is a ring homomorphism, \eqref{e:soundness_new_to_projected_lifted_polring_claim} yields  $\pirand(h)=0$. Say, for example, that $\cM_q$ consists of the maps $h\mapsto \pi_q(h)(\bm{\alpha})$ for $\bm{\alpha}\in \FF_q^{k'}$, as in our instantiation. Then  either $q$ divides every coefficient of $h$, which as in \cref{s:to_soundness_prime} occurs with probability at most $(B_\ZZ+1)/(\lfloor \log q_{\min}\rfloor\cdot |\cP|)$, or $\pi_q(h)$ is a nonzero polynomial of total degree at most $D_\ZZ$ that vanishes at the random point $\bm{\alpha}$, which occurs with probability at most $D_\ZZ/q_{\min}$ by the Schwartz--Zippel lemma. Overall we have
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:to_kappa_0_rings}
\Pr[\cE]\leq \kappa_0\defeq \frac{D_\ZZ}{q_{\min}} + \frac{B_\ZZ+1}{\lfloor\log q_{\min}\rfloor\cdot |\cP|}.
\end{equation}
%
%cf.\ \eqref{e:to_kappa_0} for the case $k'=0$, and \cref{l:examples_morphisms} for the concrete bounds $D_\ZZ, B_\ZZ$ of \cref{c:core_iop}.

We conclude that $\Pi_1$ is  round-by-round knowledge sound with error $\kappa_0$. The errors of the whole protocol construction from \cref{s:to_general_rings} are $\kappa_0$ followed by those of $\Pi_2$ and $\Pi_3$.







\end{lncsmove}
\begin{lncsmove}{FurtherWork} % not placed anywhere: dropped in the LNCS build
\begin{comment}
\subsection{Further work and open problems}\label{s:open_problems}
We list some ongoing work of ours and questions that we leave open:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
 \item \ftwoz \ is not a zero-knowledge scheme as of now. Enabling zero-knowledge for it is ongoing work. 
 \item As mentioned throughout the paper, \ftwoz\ can be used in combination of the Zinc+ arithmetization system and PIOP in order to prove lattice computations. Ongoing work is to  integrate (in our implementation) the two schemes in order to prove Falcon signature verification.
\item  \ftwoz's verifier and proof sizes have asymptotic square root costs. Making them polylogarithmic is an intriguing problem which we leave open.
\item \ftwoz\ works with finitely generated rings. This excludes the field or rational numbers. Ongoing work is to extend \ftwoz\ to such field.
\end{itemize}
\end{comment}

\end{lncsmove}
\section{Preliminaries}

We next present preliminary concepts and notation used throughout the paper.
\iflncs In \cref{a:prelims} we provide an extended preliminaries section, which includes, among others, concepts on Interactive Oracle Reductions (IORs) and their round-by-round knowledge soundness.\fi

\paragraph{General notation.} Given an integer $n\geq 1$, we write $[n]$ to denote the set $\{1,\ldots, n\}$. Given integers $m\leq n$, we let $\range{m}{n}$ denote the set of integers $\{m, m+1, \ldots, n\}$.

We use $\circ$ to denote composition of maps, so that for maps $g: S_1\to S_2$ and $f:S_2\to S_3$, we have $f\circ g: S_1 \to S_3$ defined as $f\circ g(x) = f(g(x))$. 

Any map $f:S_1 \to S_2$ applies  entry-wise to vectors from $S_1^n$, for any $n$, and similarly for matrices. The map $f$ also applies coefficient-wise to polynomials with coefficients in $S_1$. A multiplication of a vector  or a matrix by a scalar means that we multiply each entry of the vector or matrix by that scalar. 

We denote vectors with boldface letters, e.g.\ $\uu,\vv$. By $\uu_i$ we denote the $i$-th position of $\uu$. Sometimes we index the entries of a vector $\uu$ with elements of a boolean hypercube $\bits^{t}$. 

We look at vectors both as row vectors and column vectors. When performing matrix-vector multiplications, it will always be clear which one we intend. Given a ring $R$, a  matrix $M\in R^{n\times m}$ and  vectors $\uu\in R^m$, $\vv\in R^n$, one has
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:matrix_transpose_inner_product}
\langle M\cdot \uu, \vv \rangle = \langle \uu, M^{\mathsf{T}} \cdot \vv\rangle,
\end{equation}
%
where $M^{\mathsf{T}}$ denotes transposition and $\langle \cdot, \cdot \rangle$ denotes inner product.

\subsection{Algebra}\label{s:prelims_algebra}
\paragraph{Concrete fields and rings.} We write $\FF_q$ for the finite field with $q$ elements, $\QQ$ for the field of rational numbers, and $\ZZ$ for the ring of integers. For an integer $n\geq 1$, $\ZZ_n$ denotes the ring of integers modulo $n$, with the convention $\ZZ_0\defeq \ZZ$. When $n$ is prime, $\ZZ_n=\FF_n$. Let \lncsmath{\pi_n :\ZZ\to \ZZ_n} denote reduction modulo $n$. For $n\geq 1$, we fix the inverse $\pi_n^{-1} : \ZZ_n \to \range{0}{n-1}\subseteq \ZZ$ mapping each element $a$ in $\ZZ_n$ to the integer in the range  $\range{0}{n-1}$ representing $a$. 



\paragraph{Rings in general.} All rings in this work are associative, commutative, and have a multiplicative identity. Given a ring $R$, we write $R[X_1, \ldots, X_n]$ for the ring of polynomials with coefficients in $R$ and variables $X_1, \ldots, X_n$ (not necessarily multilinear). 

 The \emph{characteristic} of a ring $\ring$, denoted $\Char(R)$, is the smallest $c\geq 1$, if it exists, such that $1 + \overset{c}{\ldots} + 1 = 0$ in $\ring$. Otherwise, if $1 + \overset{c}{\ldots} + 1 \neq 0$ for all $c$, the characteristic is zero.
 By $\range{0}{n}_\ring$ we denote all elements in $\ring$ of the form $1 + \overset{i}{\ldots} + 1$ for $i=0,\ldots, n$.
\begin{lncsappendix}[Additional preliminaries\label{a:prelims}]{prelims-rings}% LNCS: appendix region starting mid-paragraph (the trailing % keeps the full version's spacing)
    \iflncs 
This section introduces further terminology, notation, and known results used in the paper. 
    \else\fi
\iflncs\paragraph{Integers in a ring.}\fi
For every ring $R$ we have a canonical embedding 
 %
 $$
    \ZZ_{\Char(R)} \subseteq R
 $$
of the ring of integers modulo $\Char(R)$ into $R$. Namely, the set $\{n \cdot 1 \mid n\in \ZZ\} \subseteq R$ for $1$ the identity element of $R$, forms the ring  $\ZZ_{\Char(R)}$. Further, $\pi_{\Char(R)}$ induces a natural homomorphism (not necessarily surjective)
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:Z_to_ring}
\pi_{\Char(R)} : \ZZ \to R
\end{equation}
sending $n\in \ZZ$ to $n\cdot 1$ for $1$ the identity of $R$. 

Given a set or vector of integers $\ww\in \ZZ^m$, we define \begin{equation}\label{e:integers_subscript_proj}\ww_R= \pi_{\Char(R)}(\ww).\end{equation}
\end{lncsappendix}
 
 \paragraph{Finitely generated rings.} All rings in this work are \emph{finitely generated} as rings. Equivalently, every ring $R$ in this work is isomorphic to a quotient \lncsmath{R=\ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_k]/I} of a polynomial ring over $\ZZ$ by an ideal $I$. This condition is satisfied by, for example:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
\item All finite fields and the rings $\ZZ_n$; 
\item $\ZZ$, and the polynomial rings $\ZZ[X]$ and $\FF_q[X]$;
\item Arbitrary quotients of the previous rings, such as $\ZZ_n[X]/(h(X))$ for an arbitrary polynomial $h(X)$ and an arbitrary integer $n\geq 0$, which includes  lattice rings. 
\end{enumerate}
It is not satisfied by, for example, $\QQ$ and $\QQ[X]$, which are not finitely generated as rings. %Some constructions in this work impose further requirements on the rings they use. We state these when  needed.


Given a finitely generated ring $R$ with fixed generators $1,\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_k$ (we always list $1$ among the generators, in particular $k=0$ for $\ZZ_n$) and bounds $B, D\geq 1$, we let $R_{\leq B}^{<D}$ denote the set of elements of $R$ of the form 
$$
h=\sum_{\aa\in \ZZ_{\geq 0}^k,\ a_1+\ldots+a_k<D} h_{\aa}\cdot \eta_1^{a_1}\cdots\eta_k^{a_k}, \qquad h_{\aa}\in \range{0}{2^B-1}_R,
$$
%\lncsmath{h=\sum_{\aa\in \ZZ_{\geq 0}^k,\ a_1+\ldots+a_k<D} h_{\aa}\cdot \eta_1^{a_1}\cdots\eta_k^{a_k}, \qquad h_{\aa}\in \range{0}{2^B-1}_R,}
 i.e.\  $R_{\leq B}^{<D}$ is formed by the elements of $R$ that can be written as a polynomial of total degree less than $D$ in $\eta_1,\ldots, \eta_k$, with coefficients obtained by adding $1$ together (in $R$) less than $2^B$ times. 


%--

%(One could bound the degree in each generator separately instead; nothing in this work depends on this choice.) We write $\gamma_1=1,\gamma_2,\ldots,\gamma_{\moduledegree}$, where $\moduledegree=\binom{D-1+k}{k}$, for the monomials $\eta_1^{a_1}\cdots\eta_k^{a_k}$ appearing above (in some fixed order), so that $R_{\leq B}^{<D}=\{\sum_{i\in[\moduledegree]} h_i\cdot\gamma_i \mid h_i\in\range{0}{2^B-1}_R\}$. For example, $\ZZ_{\leq B}^{<D}=\range{0}{2^B-1}$ for every $D$; for $R=\FF_q[X]/(h(X))$ or $R=\ZZ_n[X]/(h(X))$ with generator $x$ and $D=\deg h$, the $\gamma_i$ form the power basis $1,x,\ldots,x^{D-1}$ (this covers all finite fields); and for $R=\ZZ[X]$ with generator $X$ we obtain the polynomials of degree less than $D$ with coefficients in $\range{0}{2^B-1}$.



 
 %An ideal $I$ of a ring $R$ generated by a set $S\subseteq R$ is denoted $(S)$.

\begin{comment}

R_{\leq B}[X_1, \ldots, X_n]
$$
%
as the subset of $R[X_1, \ldots, X_n]$ formed by polynomials all whose coefficients are in the set $\range{0}{2^B-1}_R$.  


\bigskip

   
%Given a base $\alpha_1,\ldots, \alpha_\moduledegree$ for $M_\ring$, we let $\moduleonebound$ be the subset of $M_\ring$ with elements whose coordinates belong to $\ringbitbound{B}$ in this base.






\end{comment}


\subsection{\iflncs Relations\else Linear codes and relations\fi}\label{s:prelims_codes}

\begin{lncsappendix}{prelims-codes}
\iflncs\subsection{Linear codes}\label{a:prelims_codes}\else\paragraph{Linear codes.}\fi

Let $\cC \subseteq \FF^{\codelength}$ be a linear code over $\FF$ of dimension $\codedim$ and blocklength $\codelength$.  Given two words $w_1, w_2$ of length $n$, the relative Hamming distance between $w_1$ and $w_2$ (the fraction of positions in which they differ) is denoted 
%
\lncsmath{\hamming(w_1,w_2).}
%The \emph{relative minimum distance} of $\cC$, denoted $\hamming(\cC)$, is the minimum of $\hamming(c_1,c_2)$ over distinct codewords $c_1,c_2\in \cC$. 
Given $\delta\in (0,1)$ and an integer $L\geq 1$, we say $\cC$ is \emph{$(\delta, L)$-list-decodable} if for every word $w\in \FF^{\codelength}$ there are at most $L$ codewords $c\in \cC$ with $\hamming(w,c)<\delta$. Every code is $(\delta,1)$-list-decodable for $\delta\leq \hamming(\cC)/2$.  The value $\hamming(\cC)/2$ is called the \emph{unique decoding radius} of $\cC$.
\end{lncsappendix}

\paragraph{Relations.}
A \emph{relation} $\Relation$ consists of triples  $(\inp,\oracle;\wit)$, where $\inp$ is the explicit instance, $\oracle$ the implicit instance or oracle, and $\wit$ the witness.  The corresponding language  is 
\(
  \LANG(\Relation)\ \defeq\ \bigl\{(\inp,\oracle)\ \bigm|\ \exists\,\wit \text{ such that } (\inp,\oracle;\wit)\in\Relation\bigr\}.
\)

\paragraph{Polynomial Equation Satisfiability (PESAT) over finite fields.} We recall a relation from \cite{BCFW25}  over finite fields, which captures popular constraints such as R1CS, CCS, AIR (cf.\ \cite{BCFW25}), and lookup and permutation constraints (cf.\ \cite{zinc}).

Fix a finite field $\FF$, a witness length $\codedim$, an input length $\inplen$, a number of rows $n$, a collection of $n$ polynomials $\pp=(\mathbf{p}_{1}, \ldots, \pp_\numrows)$ on $\inplen+\codedim$ variables. 

\begin{definition}\label{d:pesat} The PESAT relation with respect to these parameters is defined as 
$$
\PESAT(\FF,\pp) = \left\{\left. 
\iflncs
\begin{aligned}
    \inp &= \xx \in \FF^{\inplen}, \quad \oracle = \bot ,\\
    \wit &=\bff \in \FF^\codedim
\end{aligned} 
\else
\begin{aligned}
    \inp &= \xx \in \FF^{\inplen},\\
    \oracle &= \bot ,\\
    \wit &=\bff \in \FF^\codedim
\end{aligned} 
\fi
\ \right| \ 
\begin{aligned}
    \mathbf{p}_{i}(\xx, \bff) = 0  \text{ for all } i\in [\numrows].
\end{aligned}
\right\}
$$
\end{definition}
%
% [LNCS pointer (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-16: to the codes paragraph moved to the extended preliminaries; please review]
Let $\cC\subseteq \FF^{\codelength}$ be a linear code of dimension $\codedim'$ and blocklength $\codelength$ over $\FF$\iflncs\ (cf.\ \cref{a:prelims_codes})\fi.    Let $W\subseteq \FF^{\codedim}$ be a subset and let $T:W \to \FF^{\codedim'}$ be an arbitrary map (looking ahead, for us $T$ will be a packing map that packs elements from $\FF_2$ into elements of a larger binary field, so that $W=\FF_2^{\codedim}\subseteq \FF^{\codedim}$).  We define the \emph{Encoded PESAT  relation} as: 
\iflncs
% [LNCS, 2026-09-17: the Encoded PESAT definition inlined in the LNCS build (the same formula on one line); please review]
$\PESAT(\FF, \pp, \cC, T) \defeq \{ (\inp=\xx\in \FF^\inplen,\ \oracle = f \in \FF^{\codelength},\ \wit= \bff\in W) \mid \Enc_{\cC}(T(\bff)) = f \ \wedge\ \pp_i(\xx, \bff) = 0 \text{ for all } i\in [\numrows]\}$.
\else
$$
\PESAT(\FF, \pp, \cC, T) \defeq \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=\xx\in \FF^\inplen, \\&\oracle = f \in \FF^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit= \bff\in W\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned}
&\Enc_{\cC}(T(\bff)) = f\\ \wedge \  &\pp_i(\xx, \bff) = 0  \text{ for all } i\in [\numrows]\end{aligned}\right\}
$$
\fi
We will drop the reference to $T$ whenever it is understood from the context. When $\numrows=1$ and $\pp_1(\xx,\ff)= \langle \ff, \vv\rangle -\mu$ where $\xx=(\vv,\mu)$, the relation $\PESAT(\FF,\pp, \cC, T)$ is called a \emph{constrained code} \cite{whir}.  We denote it \lncsmath{\LIN(\FF,\cC,T).}
%
\begin{definition}[Relaxation of $\PESAT$ and $\LIN$]Given $\delta \in (0,1)$, the \emph{relaxation} of $\PESAT$ and $\LIN$, denoted
%
\iflncs
$\PESAT^\delta$, $\LIN^{\delta}$,
\else
\lncsmath{
\PESAT^\delta, \qquad \qquad \LIN^{\delta}
}
\fi
%
are the relations defined exactly as $\PESAT$ and $\LIN$, respectively, except that the constraint $\Enc_{\cC}(T(\bff))=f$ is replaced by
%
\lncsmath{
\hamming(\Enc_{\cC}(T(\bff)), f) < \delta.
}
\end{definition}

\begin{lncsappendix}{prelims-mle}
\iflncs\subsection{Multilinear extensions}\label{a:prelims_mle}\else\paragraph{Multilinear extensions.}\fi{}  Given a field $\FF$ and $\uu\in \FF^{2^r}$ indexed by $\bits^r$, we denote by $\mle{\uu}\in \FF[X_1,\ldots,X_r]$  the unique multilinear polynomial with $\mle{\uu}(\bb)=\uu_\bb$ for all $\bb\in\bits^r$. For $\bm\alpha,\bm\beta\in\FF^r$ we let $\eq(\bm\alpha;\bm\beta)\defeq \prod_{i\in[r]}\bigl(\alpha_i\cdot \beta_i+(1-\alpha_i)\cdot (1-\beta_i)\bigr)$, so that $\mle{\uu}(\bm\alpha)=\sum_{\bb\in\bits^r}\uu_\bb\cdot \eq(\bb;\bm\alpha)$. We write $\eq(\bb,\cc;\bm\alpha)$ for $\eq((\bb,\cc);\bm\alpha)$.
\end{lncsappendix}


\begin{lncsappendix}{prelims-iors}
\subsection{Interactive Oracle Reductions (IOR) of proximity}\label{s:IORs}


A public-coin \emph{Interactive Oracle Reduction (IOR) of proximity} $\Pi=(\prover,\verifier)$ from a relation $\Relation$ to a relation $\Relation'$ is specified as follows. The prover $\prover$ receives $(\inp, \oracle,\wit)\in \Relation$. The verifier $\verifier$ receives $\inp$ and query access to $\oracle$. They interact for $k$ rounds. In round $j\in[k]$, the prover sends an oracle $\provermsg_j$ and the verifier sends a uniformly random message $\verifiermsg_j\in\{0,1\}^{r_j}$. After the $k$ rounds, the verifier makes  queries to $\oracle$ and to $\provermsg_1,\ldots,\provermsg_k$. Then:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item $\verifier$ either rejects or outputs a new  pair $(\inp', \oracle')$, where $\oracle'$ is specified implicitly in terms of the oracles $\oracle$ and $\provermsg_1,\ldots,\provermsg_k$.
  \item $\prover$ outputs a witness $\wit'$ such that $(\inp', \oracle', \wit')\in \Relation'$.
\end{itemize}
We write $ \langle \prover, \verifier\rangle(\inp,\oracle,\wit)$ for the interaction of prover and verifier during the IOR.

\begin{definition}[Completeness]
\label{def:ior-completeness}
$\Pi$ has \emph{(perfect) completeness}  if for every $(\inp, \oracle,\wit)\in\Relation$,
\[
  \Pr_{\verifiermsg_1,\dots,\verifiermsg_k}\bigl[\,(\inp',\oracle';\wit')\in \Relation'\, \mid \, (\inp',\oracle';\wit') \gets \langle \prover, \verifier\rangle(\inp,\oracle,\wit)\bigr]=1.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    An IOP for a relation $\Relation$ is an IOR from $\Relation$ to the trivial relation $\Relation_{\mathsf{triv}}=\{(\inp=\bot,\oracle=\bot, \wit=\bot)\}$.
\end{remark}

We proceed to define round-by-round knowledge soundness of an IOR, following \cite{BCFW25} in the formulation with relaxed relations of \cite{zook}.



\begin{definition}[Knowledge state function with relaxation]
\label{def:knowledge-state-function}
Let $\Pi = (\prover, \verifier)$ be an IOR from a relation $\Relation$ to $\Relation'$. A \emph{knowledge state function with relaxation $(\widetilde{\Relation}, \widetilde{\Relation'})$} for $\Pi$ is a (possibly inefficient) function $\kstate$ that, on input  $(\inp, \oracle)$, interaction transcript $\tr$, and knowledge state witness $\kswit$, outputs a bit and has the following properties.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
  \item \textbf{Empty transcript.} If $\tr = \emptyset$ is the empty transcript, then $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak \oracle,\lncsbreak \tr, \kswit) = 1$ if and only if $(\inp, \oracle; \kswit) \in \widetilde{\Relation}$.
  \item \textbf{Prover moves.} If $\tr$ is a transcript where the prover is about to move and $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak \oracle,\lncsbreak \tr, \kswit) = 0$, then $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak \oracle,\lncsbreak \tr \Vert \provermsg, \kswit) = 0$ for every prover message $\provermsg$.
  \item \textbf{Full transcript.} If $\tr = (\provermsg_1,\lncsbreak \verifiermsg_1,\lncsbreak \ldots,\lncsbreak \provermsg_k,\lncsbreak \verifiermsg_k)$ is a full transcript, then $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak \oracle,\lncsbreak \tr, \kswit) = 1$ if and only if $\verifier$ on $\tr$ outputs $(\inp', \oracle')$ such that $(\inp', \oracle'; \kswit) \in \widetilde{\Relation'}$.
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Round-by-round knowledge soundness]
\label{def:rbr-knowledge-new}
A $k$-round IOR $\Pi$ has \emph{round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation} with errors $(\knowledgeerror_1,\ldots,\knowledgeerror_k)$ if there exists a knowledge state function $\kstate$ as in \cref{def:knowledge-state-function} and a deterministic extractor $\rbrextractor$ such that for every $(\inp,\oracle)$, every $j\in[k]$, and every transcript $\tr$ where the verifier is about to send $\verifiermsg_j$,
\[
  \Pr_{\verifiermsg_j}\!\left[
  \exists\,\kswit \ \text{such that} \ \begin{array}{l}
  \wedge\ \kstate\bigl(\inp,\oracle,\tr,\rbrextractor(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr\Vert\verifiermsg_j,\kswit)\bigr)=0\\
  \wedge\ \kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr\Vert\verifiermsg_j,\kswit)=1
  \end{array}
 \right]
  \ \le\ \knowledgeerror_j(\inp,\oracle).
\]
If $(\widetilde{\Relation}, \widetilde{\Relation'})=(\Relation, \Relation')$ then we say the round-by-round knowledge soundness of $\Pi$ is \emph{without relaxation}.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Composition of IORs {\cite[Theorem 4.5]{zook}}]\label{r:ior_compositoin}
Let $\Pi_1$ be a perfectly complete $k_1$-round IOR from $\Relation_1$ to $\Relation_2$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\widetilde{\Relation_1},\widetilde{\Relation_2})$ and errors $\kappa_{11},\ldots,\kappa_{1k_1}$, and let $\Pi_2$ be a perfectly complete $k_2$-round IOR from $\Relation_2$ to $\Relation_3$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\widetilde{\Relation_2},\widetilde{\Relation_3})$ and errors $\kappa_{21},\ldots,\kappa_{2k_2}$. Then the IOR from $\Relation_1$ to $\Relation_3$ that runs $\Pi_1$ and then $\Pi_2$ on the output of $\Pi_1$ is perfectly complete, has $k_1+k_2$ rounds, and has round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\widetilde{\Relation_1},\widetilde{\Relation_3})$ and errors $\kappa_{11},\ldots,\kappa_{1k_1},\kappa_{21},\ldots,\kappa_{2k_2}$.
\end{lemma}


\paragraph{Grinding.}
 Grinding \cite{ethstark} is a technique that, given an IOP $\Pi$ for a relation $\Relation$ with relaxation $\widetilde{\Relation}$ with round-by-round knowledge errors $\kappa_1,\ldots, \kappa_k$,  produces a new IOP $\Pi'$ for $\Relation$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $\widetilde{\Relation}$ with errors $$2^{-\alpha_1}\cdot \kappa_1,\ldots, 2^{-\alpha_k}\cdot \kappa_k.$$ The new IOP $\Pi'$ works exactly as $\Pi$, except that, at each round $i\in [k]$, the verifier samples a random string of $\alpha_i$ bits, called \emph{grinding string}, which it sends alongside its message, and rejects unless all bits are $0$. 

 Clearly $\Pi'$ is not  necessarily complete, even if $\Pi$ is. The goal is to use the BCS compilation of IOPs, and not the IOPs themselves (i.e.\ the compilation where oracles are replaced by Merkle roots and where interactiveness is removed through the Fiat-Shamir transform). To this end, one chooses $\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_k$ small enough that the prover of the BCS transform of $\Pi'$ can \emph{grind} transcripts until all of the grinding strings in the transcript are the zero string. This results in the prover having to compute  per round $i\in [k]$, an average of $2^{\alpha_i}$  hashes of its $i$-th message followed by a small string of bits (in practice, one has the prover send a small string of bits next to its message, so that during grinding, it does not need to produce fresh Merkle roots at each grinding iteration). In \cref{tab:grinding-cost}, we provide example grinding times for different values of $\alpha_i$.
 
 The round-by-round knowledge errors of $\Pi'$ are obtained by defining a state function which is exactly as the state function of $\Pi$, except that the function outputs zero if some of the aforementioned strings of bits are not the zero string. The extractor for $\Pi'$ is defined as the extractor for $\Pi$, ignoring the additional strings of bits in the transcript.

\iflncs\subsection{Notation glossary}\fi
\iflncs For convenience, we provide a summary of the notation used throughout the paper.  \else We begin with a summary of the notation we use throughout the paper. Most of the notation will be introduced subsequently, but nevertheless we record it here for convenience of the reader. \fi

\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{l|l}
    \toprule
        $\codedim$ & Bit-length of witness cf.\ \cref{s:linmodfieldbin}\\
        \hline
        $\bff$ & Witness, either over $\bits\subseteq \ZZ$, or $S_{\leq B}^{<D_S}$ (see below)\\
        \hline
        $\vv$, $\uu$ & Public vectors in inner product constraints, over $R$\\
        \hline
         $R$ & Ring where constraints live, cf.\ \cref{s:linmodfieldbin,s:general_LinBitsDual}\\
         \hline
    $\codedim_1,\codedim_2$ & Tensor decomposition dimensions, with $\codedim_1\cdot\codedim_2=\codedim$\\
         \hline
                  $\pi_n$ & Reduction modulo $n$\\
                          \hline $k+1$ & Number of generators of $R$, including $1$ \\
                  \hline
         $\picanon^R$ & Canonical morphism $\picanon^R: \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_k] \to R$ (see \cref{s:core_iop})\\
          \hline
        $\comfield$ & A binary field, typically with $\comfieldexp=128$ \\
                 \hline
         $\cC$ & Linear code over $\comfield$ of dimension $\codedim/\comfieldexp$, and length $\codelength$\\
         \hline
         $\cP$ & Set of primes\\
         \hline
         $\cM_q$ & Set of homomorphisms $\pirand:\ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_k] \to \FF_q$ for $q\in \cP$\\
         \hline
         
        $\codedim_0$ & Witness length in the general LinBitsRings relation, cf.\ \cref{s:general_LinBitsDual}  \\
        \hline
                  $S$ & Ring where witness lives, cf.\ \cref{s:general_LinBitsDual}\\
         \hline
         $S_{\leq B}^{<D_S}$ & \makecell[l]{Elements of $S$ expressible as polynomials of degree $<D_S$ in the generators of $S$\\ \quad  with coefficients of bit-size $\leq B$, cf.\ \cref{s:prelims_algebra}\\ \quad  (In the core LinBitsRings relation, $S=\ZZ$, $B=1$)}\\
         \hline
         $\psi$ & Ring homomorphism $\psi: S\to R$\\
         \hline
         $d$ & \makecell[l]{Number of monomials $\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_d$ of degree $<D_S$\\ \quad in the generators of $S$ (\cref{s:general_LinBitsDual})}\\
         \hline
         $L$ & Bitification matrix, $L\in S^{\codedim_0\times \codedim}$, cf.\ \cref{d:bitification}\\
         \bottomrule
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Summary of notation used throughout the paper. }
    \label{t:symbols}
\end{table}
\end{lncsappendix}

\subsection{The core LinBitsRings relation}\label{s:linmodfieldbin}




\renewcommand{\ringtwo}{\FF_q}
\renewcommand{\ringmorphism}{\pi_q}
In this section we define the main linear relation we work with. We start by fixing notation:
\iflncs\else\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]\fi
    \iflncs\else\item\fi{} Let $\comfield$ be a field with $2^{\comfieldexp}$ elements. Let
%
$
\beta_1, \ldots, \beta_\comfieldexp \in \comfield
$
%
be an $\FF_2$-basis of $\comfield$ seen as an $\FF_2$-vector space of dimension $\comfieldexp$. Given a vector $\uu\in \FF_2^\codedim$, with $\comfieldexp$ dividing $\codedim$, we define
%
\lncsmath{
\pack(\uu)\in \comfield^{\codedim/\comfieldexp}
}
as the vector whose $j$-th entry is
%
$$
\pack(\uu)_{j} = \uu_{(j-1)\cdot \comfieldexp +1 }\cdot \beta_1 + \ldots + \uu_{(j-1)\cdot \comfieldexp +\comfieldexp-1 }\cdot \beta_{\comfieldexp-1}+\uu_{j \cdot \comfieldexp} \cdot \beta_\comfieldexp \in \comfield.
$$
%
\iflncs\else\item \fi
 Let $\codedim\geq 1$ be a dimension parameter divisible by $\comfieldexp$, $\cC$ a code over $\comfield$ of dimension $\codedim/\comfieldexp$ and blocklength $\codelength$.  
 \iflncs\else\item\fi{}  Let $R$ be a finitely generated ring (typically $R$ is $\ZZ$, a finite field, not necessarily a prime field, or a quotient of a polynomial ring $\ZZ[X]$ or $\FF_q[X]$) and let $\pi:\ZZ \to R$  be the  homomorphism mapping an integer $a$ to $a\cdot 1\in R$ (i.e.\ reduction modulo the characteristic of $R$).
\iflncs\else\end{itemize}\fi

 We begin by defining  a particular subclass of our target relation.

\begin{definition} The \emph{Core LinBitsRings} relation  is defined as
%
\iflncs\begingroup\small\fi
$$
\LinBitsDual(\bits,\pi, R,\cC) \defeq \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\mu, \vv)\in R \times R^{\codedim}, \\&\oracle = f \in \comfield^\codelength, \\ &\wit= \bff\in \{0,1\}^\codedim \subseteq \ZZ^\codedim\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned} &f=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack \circ \picomfield(\bff) \\    & \langle \pi(\bff), \vv\rangle  = \mu \quad \text{in } R  \end{aligned} \right\}
$$
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
%
\end{definition}
%
%Again, when there is no risk of confusion, we write $\LinBitsDual(\bits,\ringmorphism, \moduletwo,\cC)$ simply as $\LinBitsDual$.
%
%
%
\iflncs\else For $\delta\in (0,1)$, we let $\LinBitsDual^\delta$  be the relation $\LinBitsDual$  after replacing the constraint $f=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bff)$ by
%
\lncsmath{
\hamming(f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bff))<\delta.
}\fi
%
%
%
%
%

\renewcommand{\moduleone}{M}
\renewcommand{\moduleonebound}{M_{\leq B}}
\renewcommand{\moduletwo}{R}
 %For example, if $\ringtwo=\ZZ$, $\modulemorphism$ can be $\mathsf{id}$, and if $\ringtwo=\FF$, then $\modulemorphism$ can be $\pi_q$, i.e.\ reduction modulo $q$.


%When $Q$ and $\yy$ are such that $\yy=(\rr,\mu)\in \FF^{\log\codedim}\times \FF$ and $$Q(\yy,\ff)= \mle{\bff}(\rr)-\mu,$$ we denote $\Relation_{\cC, \FF}^{Q}$  by $\Relation_{\cC, \FF}^{\mathsf{mle}}$. 


%Next let $\comfield$ be a finite field of characteristic $2$,  and let $\moduletwo$ be either the field of rationals $\QQ$ or a finite field of characteristic $q$, possibly an extension field. For intuition, $\comfield$ is the field over which we will commit our witness, and $\moduletwo$ is the field over which we prove linear relations on the witness. If $\moduletwo=\QQ$ then we define $\degevalfield=0$, and if $\moduletwo$ is a finite field of characteristic $q$, then $\degevalfield$ denotes the degree extension of $\moduletwo$ over $\FF_q$.

%Let 
%
%$$
%\pievalfield: \ZZ[X] \to \moduletwo
%$$
%
%Rbe the natural projection of $\ZZ[X]$ onto $\moduletwo$. Concretely, if $\moduletwo=\QQ$,  then  $\pievalfield$ is reduction modulo the ideal $(X)$ (i.e.\ $\pievalfield(\sum_{i=0}^{k} a_i\cdot X^i)  = a_0$);  if $\moduletwo$ is a prime field $\FF_q$, then $\pievalfield$ is reduction modulo $q$ and modulo the ideal $(X)$; and if $\moduletwo$ is an extension field, so that $\moduletwo=\FF_q[X]/(h(X))$ for some irreducible polynomial $h(X)\in \FF_q[X]$, then $\pievalfield$ is reduction modulo $q$ and modulo the ideal $(h(X))$.




\renewcommand{\modulemorphism}{\psi}
\renewcommand{\moduletwo}{R}


\subsection{Tensor decomposition and products of constrained codes}\label{s:interleaved_setting}
%
\renewcommand{\ringmorphism}{\pi}
%\renewcommand{\ringtwo}{}

As discussed in \iflncs\cref{s:intro_nutshell}\else\cref{s:to_simple_bitz}\fi, \ftwoz \ crucially demands and uses a tensor decomposition of $\vv$. We next establish notation and basic definitions around it.

 Let $(\inp,\oracle,\wit)=((\mu,\vv), f, \bff) \in \LinBitsDual(\bits,\ringmorphism, R,\cC)$. We fix a tensor decomposition of the vector $\vv\in \moduletwo^\codedim$ as $$\vv = \vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)} = \left(\vv_i^{(1)}\cdot \vv_j^{(2)}\right)_{i\in [\codedim_1], j\in [\codedim_2]}$$ for some $\vv^{(1)}\in \moduletwo^{\codedim_1}, \vv^{(2)}\in \moduletwo^{\codedim_2}$, with $\codedim_1 \cdot \codedim_2=\codedim$. Note that the trivial tensor decomposition is admitted, i.e.\ we may effectively not decompose $\vv$ by choosing $\codedim_1=\codedim$, $\codedim_2=1$, $\vv^{(1)}=\vv$, and $\vv^{(2)} = (1)$.

%We assume $\cC = \cC_1^{\equiv \codedim_2}$ is the $\codedim_2$-dimensional interleaving of a code $\cC_1\subseteq \comfield^{\codelength/\codedim_2}$ of dimension $\codedim_1/2^\comfieldexp$ and blocklength $\codelength/\codedim_2$.

\renewcommand{\modulemorphism}{\ringmorphism}

We  index the entries of $\bff$ as $\bff=(\bff_{ij})_{i\in [\codedim_1], j\in[\codedim_2]}$. Note that
%
$$
\langle \modulemorphism(\bff), \vv \rangle = \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\left(\sum_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \modulemorphism(\bff_{ij}) \cdot \vv_i^{(1)}\right) \cdot \vv_j^{(2)} = \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \langle \modulemorphism(\bff_{*j}) , \vv^{(1)} \rangle \cdot \vv_j^{(2)},
$$
where $\bff_{*j} = (\bff_{ij})_{i\in[\codedim_1]}$. 
%
% We use $0$ and $1$ indistinctly to denote the zero and one integer, and zero and one field elements. 
%
Consider the relation  (cf.\ \cref{d:pesat}) $\PESAT(\comfield,\lncsbreak Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\lncsbreak\cC,\lncsbreak \pack)$ for $\ell_2$ constraint polynomials $Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ}$ so that
%
\iflncs\begingroup\footnotesize\fi
\begin{align*}
&\PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack)\\ &\iflncs\else\qquad\fi{} = \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\yy,\bm{\mu})\in \comfield^{\codedim_1}\times \comfield^{\codedim_2}, \\&\oracle = f\in \comfield^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit=\bff= (\bff_{*j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\in \FF_2^{\codedim_1 \times \codedim_2}\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned} &f= \Enc_{\cC}\circ\pack(\bff) \\ \wedge \  &\prod_{i=1}^{\codedim_1} \left((\yy_i-1)\cdot \bff_{ij} +1\right) = \bm{\mu}_j  \text{ for all } j\in [\codedim_2]\end{aligned}  \right\}.
\end{align*}
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
%
%We omit an explicit description of the polynomials $Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ}$, since the fact that the above relation is a particular instance of the PESAT relation is not relevant in this paper.
%
Here $Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ}$ consists of the $\codedim_2$ polynomials $\prod_{i=1}^{\codedim_1}((\yy_i-1)\cdot \bff_{ij}+1)-\bm{\mu}_j$, $j\in[\codedim_2]$.



%
We assume prover and verifier have access to an IOR $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$  that reduces 
%
$
\PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack)$ to $\LIN(\comfield, \cC, \pack)$, where, recall, this latter relation has predicates of the form $\langle \bfg, \mathbf{z}\rangle = c$ with $\bfg\in \FF_2^{\codedim}$, $\mathbf{z}$ a vector over $\comfield$, $c\in \comfield$, and $f=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack(\bfg)$.   In our instantiation $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$ is an optimized GKR protocol for  grand products (cf.\ \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}), which reduces the initial grand product claims to MLE evaluation claims of $\yy$ and $\bff$ over $\comfield$.

%
%
We assume $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$ works so that the verifier never queries $\oracle$; and that, given an input
%
$$\left(\inp=(\yy,\bm{\mu}),\  \oracle= f, \ \wit=\picomfield(\bff)\right)\in \PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack),$$
%
$\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$ outputs
%
\lncsmath{
\left((\mathbf{z}, c), \ f, \ \picomfield(\bff)\right) \in \LIN( \comfield,\cC,\pack)} for some vector $\mathbf{z}\in \comfield^{\codedim}$ and field element $c\in \comfield$. In particular, $\Pi_{\red}$ outputs the same oracle $f$ and witness $\pi_2(\bff)$ as the oracle and witness it is given as inputs. 


%This is indeed the case with GKR: GKR reduces the initial circuit evaluation claim with a witness input, into an MLE evaluation claim of the same witness, without querying any oracle in-between. 



\iflncs
\else
This is indeed the case with GKR: GKR reduces the initial circuit evaluation claim with a witness input, into an MLE evaluation claim of the same witness, without querying any oracle in-between. Consequently an IOR between the versions of these relations with oracle $\bot$ (such as \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}, cf.\ \cref{l:gkr_grand_product})  can be turned, in a straightforward manner, into an IOR from $\PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack)$ to $\LIN(\comfield, \cC, \pack)$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\PESAT^\delta,\LIN^\delta)$ and the same errors, for every $\delta$ within the unique decoding radius of $\cC$ (cf.\ \cref{l:gkr_grand_product}). The input to the IOR now includes an oracle $f$, and this same oracle is output at the end of the IOR. The knowledge state function adds the condition $\hamming(f,\Enc_\cC\circ\pack(\ww))<\delta$. 
\fi

%
%\john{I actually don't like this name. There's too many things in the protocol that is linear and it's too vague}





\subsection{The {\ftwoz} IOP for the core LinBitsRings relation}\label{s:core_iop}


\paragraph{Algebraic set-up.}


 Recall (\cref{s:prelims_algebra}) that all rings in this work are finitely generated as rings. We fix generators $1, \eta_1, \ldots, \eta_{k}\in R$ and we let
%
$$
\picanon^{R} : \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k}] \to R
$$
be the surjective ring homomorphism mapping each integer $a$ to $a\cdot 1\in R$, and  $X_i$ to $\eta_{i}$ for $i\in [k]$. The choice of $\eta_1,\ldots, \eta_k$ is far from unique, see \cref{e:presentations} below. 

We also fix an inverse map  $\picanon^{R}{}^{-1}: R \to \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}]$ for $\picanon^{R}$, so that $\picanon^{R}\circ \picanon^{R}{}^{-1}=\mathsf{id}$. We refer to $\picanon^{R}{}^{-1}(a)$ as the \emph{lift} of $a\in R$. As before, $\picanon{}^{-1}$ is not unique.  In  \cref{e:presentations}  we provide example inverse maps.  

\iflncs \else
Recall (see \cref{s:prelims_algebra}) that $\pi_n$ denotes reduction modulo $n$ and that $\pi_n^{-1}$ maps each element from $\ZZ_n$ to its integer representative in $\range{0}{n-1}$.
\fi


%We also fix an inverse map $\picanon^{R}{}^{-1}: R \to \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{\dim(R)-1}]$ of $\picanon^{R}$, defined as follows. Given $a\in R$, write $a=\sum_{i\in [\dim(R)]} a_i \cdot \gamma_i$ with $a_i\in \ZZ_{\Char(R)}$ (cf.\ \cref{s:prelims_algebra}), then $\picanon^{R}{}^{-1}(a) = \pi_{\Char}^{-1}(a_1) + \sum_{i=2}^{\dim(R)} \pi_{\Char}^{-1}(a_i)\cdot X_{i-1}$.


\begin{example}\label{e:presentations}
We provide some examples of rings $R$ and choices for  projections $\picanon^R$ and their inverses: 
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,nosep]
    \item \textbf{Prime fields.} If  $R=\FF_q$ is a prime field with $q$ elements, then we can take $k=0$, so that $\picanon^{\FF_q}=\pi_q:\ZZ \to \FF_q$ is simply reduction modulo $q$, with its inverse being $\pi_q^{-1}$ (see \cref{s:prelims_algebra}). 
    \item \textbf{Integers.} Similarly, for $R=\ZZ$ we can take $k=0$, and then $\picanon^{\ZZ}$ and its lifts are the identity map on $\ZZ$.
\item \textbf{Field extensions.} If $R$ is a field extension of a prime field $\FF_q$ of degree $d>1$, then we can  take $k=1$ and $\eta_1 = x$, where $x$ is a root of an irreducible polynomial $h(X_1)\in \FF_{q}[X_1]$ of degree $d$ (so that $R=\FF_q(x)$). Then $\picanon^R:\ZZ[X_1]\to R$ consists in reducing coefficients modulo $q$, and then sending $X_1$ to $x$. Writing $a\in R$ as $a=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1} a_i\cdot x^{i}$ with $a_i\in \FF_q$, one can then define a natural lift of $a$ as $\sum_{i=0}^{d-1} \pi_q^{-1}(a_i)\cdot X_1^{i}$.
\item \textbf{Univariate integer polynomials.} If $R=\ZZ[X]$, then we can take $k=1$ and $\eta_1=X$, and then $\picanon^{\ZZ[X]}:\ZZ[X_1]\to \ZZ[X]$ is an isomorphism, with lifts given by its inverse.
\item \textbf{Lattices, Galois rings, etc.} More generally, if $R$ is finitely generated as a $\ZZ_{\Char(R)}$-module and it is free as a $\ZZ_{\Char(R)}$-module, with  $\ZZ_{\Char(R)}$-module-basis $\beta_1=1, \beta_2, \ldots, \beta_m$ (a condition satisfied by, e.g., all finite fields, $\ZZ$, and $\ZZ_n[X]/(h(X))$ for any monic $h(X)$ and any integer $n\geq 0$), then we may take $k=m-1$ and $\eta_i=\beta_{i+1}$ for $i\in [k]$. Then given $a\in R$ and writing $a=\sum_{i\in [m]} a_i\cdot \beta_i$ with $a_i\in \ZZ_{\Char(R)}$, we can define the lift of $a$ as $\pi_{\Char}^{-1}(a_1) + \sum_{i=2}^{m}\pi_{\Char}^{-1}(a_i)\cdot X_{i-1}$.
\end{itemize}
\end{example}


We assume without further comment that it is efficient to compute  values $\picanon^R(h)$  for $h\in \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_k]$, and to check whether  $\picanon^R(h)=0$.   This is the case in all examples above.


\paragraph{The \ftwoz\ IOP.} We provide an IOP for $\LinBitsDual$ next. We assume the notation from the previous two \cref{s:linmodfieldbin,s:interleaved_setting}, and from the preliminary \cref{s:prelims_algebra}, together with:
\renewcommand{\evalfield}{R}
\iflncs\else\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]\fi
%\item Given a finite field $\KK$ which is an extension of a prime field $\FF_p$ of degree $\degevalfield$, we let $\picanon^\KK: \ZZ[X] \to \KK$ be the canonical homomorphism from $\ZZ[X]$ to $\KK$: namely, if $\KK=\ZZ[X]/(p,h(X))$  for a polynomial $h(X)$ irreducible over $\FF_p[X]$, then $\picanon^\KK$ consists in modding $p$ out, and taking the quotient by $(h(X))$ (if $\KK$ is the prime field $\FF_p$, then $\picanon^\KK$ is just $\pi_p$). We fix an inverse for $\picanon^{\KK}$ of the form $$\picanon^{\KK}{}^{-1}: \KK \to \range{0}{p-1}^{<\degevalfield}[X] \subseteq \ZZ[X].
%$$ In words, $\picanon^{\KK}{}^{-1}$ simply takes an element from $\KK$ represented as a degree $< \deg(h(X))$ polynomial over $\FF_p$, and applies $\pi_{p}^{-1}$ to it. We remark that one could choose any other such ``inverse'' maps, e.g.\ for $\pi_{p}^{-1}$ one may prefer to use centered representatives. 





%\item Define $\picanon^\ZZ: \ZZ\to\ZZ$ as the identity map.  We define $\deg(\ZZ)=1$.



\iflncs\else\item\fi{} Let $\Pi_{\iopp}$ be an IOP for $\LIN(\comfield,\cC, \pack)$. In our instantiation, $\Pi_{\iopp}$ is  WHIR or Ligerito  \cite{whir,Ligerito} over binary fields as implemented in Flock \cite{flock}, with ring-switching \cite{binius2} and a preliminary sumcheck over $\comfield$ that transforms arbitrary inner products to MLE evaluation claims. We set the proximity parameter  to the Johnson bound when targeting $100$ bits of round-by-round knowledge soundness, and to the unique decoding radius for higher security regimes.   
\iflncs\else\item \fi
Let $\codedim =\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$ be dimension parameters,  and $R$ a ring with generators $1,\eta_1,\ldots, \eta_k$,  as in the previous sections. We let $\cP$ be a set of primes with $\max\cP< \fieldboundsizeexponent$. Given the tensor decomposition $\vv=\vv^{(1)}\otimes \vv^{(2)}$ from \cref{s:interleaved_setting}, we define 
\begin{equation}\label{e:ring_theta_params}
\begin{aligned}
\uu &\defeq (\vv^{(1)},\vv^{(2)})\in R^{\codedim_1+ \codedim_2}\\
D &\defeq \max_{i\in [|\uu|]} \deg\left(\picanon^{R}{}^{-1}(\uu_i)\right), \qquad\qquad N_R \defeq \max_{i\in [|\uu|]}\ \left\lVert \picanon^{R}{}^{-1}(\uu_i)\right\rVert_\infty,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
%
where $\deg$ denotes total degree, and $\lVert \cdot \rVert_\infty$ denotes the maximum absolute value of the coefficients. We also let $T\defeq \binom{D+k}{k}$ be the number of monomials in $X_1,\ldots, X_k$ of total degree at most $D$. Its role is the following: if $f,g\in \ZZ[X_1,\ldots,X_k]$ have total degree at most $D$, then $\lVert f\cdot g\rVert_\infty\leq T\cdot \lVert f\rVert_\infty\cdot \lVert g\rVert_\infty$, since each monomial of $f\cdot g$ is the sum of at most $T$ pairs of monomials of $f$ and $g$. 

For example, $T=1$ if $k=0$; $T= D+1$ if $k=1$; and in general $T\leq (D+1)^{k}$.  %In practice, for us, the bound $T\leq (D+1)^{k}$ is good enough.
%
%$$
%\Char^*(R) \defeq \begin{cases} \Char(R)-1 & \text{if } \Char(R)>0,\\ \max\left(\lVert \vv^{(1)}\rVert_\infty,\ \lVert \vv^{(2)}\rVert_\infty\right) & \text{if } R=\ZZ. \end{cases}
%$$

\iflncs\else\item\fi{} For every prime $q\in \cP$, let $\cM_q$ be a set of ring homomorphisms $$\pirand:\ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k}] \to \FF_q,$$ and let $\cM\defeq (\cM_q)_{q\in \cP}$. The verifier will sample primes and morphisms from $\cP$ and $\cM_q$. We let $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$ be the maximum probability that $\pirand(h)=0$, over random $q\gets \cP$, $\pirand\gets \cM_q$, and where the maximum is taken over nonzero polynomials $h\in \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}]$ of total degree at most $2\cdot D$, with $\lVert h\rVert_{\infty} \leq 2\cdot T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2$, and such that $\picanon^{R}(h)\neq 0$. Precisely:
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:theta_def}
\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim) \defeq  \max_{h}\left\{\Pr_{q\gets \cP, \pirand\gets \cM_q}[ \pirand(h)=0] \right\},
\end{equation}
%
where $h$ ranges over the polynomials just described. See \cref{l:examples_morphisms} for an example. Note that $D$, $N_R$, $T$, and hence $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$, depend on $R$ and on the instance vector $\vv$, but we do not make this explicit in the notation.
\iflncs\else\end{itemize}\fi


\begin{lemma}[Example definition of  $\cM_q$ and the corresponding $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$]\label{l:examples_morphisms}
    Let $\cP$ be a set of primes. For each $q\in \cP$ let $\cM_q$  be the set of all maps $\pi: \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k}]\to \FF_q$ of the form
     $$\pi(h(X_1, \ldots, X_{k})) = \pi_q(h)(\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_{k}), \qquad \text{for } h\in \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k}],$$
%
for some $\alpha_1,\ldots, \alpha_{k}\in \FF_q$, and where $\pi_q(h)$ is $h$ after reducing its coefficients modulo $q$. Then
         $$
    \theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim) \leq \frac{2\cdot D}{q_{\min}} + \frac{\log\left(2\cdot T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2\right)}{|\cP|\cdot \lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor},
    $$
    where $q_{\min}$ is the smallest prime in $\cP$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    We defer the proof to later in \cref{s:soundness_core_iop}.
\end{proof}


\renewcommand{\degevalfield}{\deg(\evalfield)}
%\renewcommand{\picanon}{{\pi_{\mathsf{canon}}^R}}




\begin{construction}[IOP for the core {$\LinBitsDual$ relation}]\label{c:core_iop}
\noindent \textbf{Parameters.} $(\codedim,\lncsbreak \codedim_1,\lncsbreak\codedim_2,\lncsbreak \Pi_\iopp,\lncsbreak \Pi_\red,\lncsbreak \cP,\lncsbreak (\cM_q)_{q\in \cP})$ \iflncs as above\else comprised of: a witness vector length $\codedim$; tensor decomposition dimensions $\codedim_1, \codedim_2$  satisfying $\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2 = \codedim$; the IOP $\Pi_\iopp$ described above, the IOR $\Pi_\red$ from \cref{s:interleaved_setting}; and the sets $\cP, \cM_q$ from above. \fi
\\

\noindent \textbf{Inputs.} The prover and verifier receive $\inp=(\mu, \vv)\in \evalfield \times \evalfield^{\codedim}$ and  $\oracle= f \in \comfield^\codelength$. The prover receives $\wit= \bff\in \bits^\codedim \subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ such that $(\inp,\oracle, \wit) \in \LinBitsDual(\bits,\lncsbreak\ringmorphism,\lncsbreak \evalfield,\lncsbreak\cC)$.  We assume the tensor split from the previous \cref{s:interleaved_setting}, and adopt its notation. 


Let $\basegenerator\in \comfield$ be a fixed generator of the multiplicative group $\comfield^*$. Denote $\picanon^R$ simply as $\picanon$.  %Let $q\in \ZZ$ denote the characteristic of $R$ if $R$ has nonzero characteristic, or if $R$ has zero characteristic, then


\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*] 
% [LNCS pointer (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-16: the multilinear-extension paragraph moved to the extended preliminaries is pointed to at its first use; please review]
% [LNCS, 2026-09-16: the description of Round 0 is hidden in the LNCS body (one-line pointer, AI-drafted) and copied verbatim into the OOD appendix a:OOD; please review]
% [LNCS pointer (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-16: the multilinear-extension paragraph moved to the extended preliminaries is pointed to at its first use; please review]
\item \textbf{Round 0 (optional): Out Of Domain (OOD) sample.} Optionally, the verifier sends a random point of a certain shape, and the prover replies with the MLE evaluation of $\pack\circ\pi_2(\bff)$ on the point.  This step is skipped if the proximity parameter $\delta$ is  set within the unique decoding radius of $\cC$ (cf.\ \iflncs\cref{a:prelims_codes}\else\cref{s:prelims_codes}\fi, and \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}). Otherwise the step is executed (see \cref{a:OOD})\iflncs.\else.
