In that case, the verifier samples and sends a random $\zeta\in \comfield$, and the prover responds with \iflncs(where ``tilde'' denotes multilinear extension, cf.\ \cref{a:prelims_mle}\fi$$y=\mle{\pack\circ\pi_2(\bff)}(\bm{\zeta}), \qquad \text{where } \bm{\zeta}\defeq \left(\zeta^{2^0}, \zeta^{2^1}, \ldots, \zeta^{2^{\log (\codedim/\comfieldexp) -1}}\right).$$\fi
\item \textbf{Round 1 (optional): replacing $R$ by a ``small'' prime field $\FF_q$.}
This step can be skipped (i.e.\ prover and verifier move to Round 2 directly) if $R$ is a prime field $\FF_p$ and $p < \fieldboundsizeexponent$; in that case we set $q\defeq p$ in Round 2 and Phase 3. Otherwise the step is executed. %, or if $R=\ZZ$ and $\lVert \vv \rVert < \fieldboundsizeexponent$.

The prover computes and sends $\mu' \in \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}]$ such that
%
$$
\langle \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}) \otimes \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}) , \bff \rangle = \mu' \qquad \text{in } \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}].
$$
%
The verifier checks that $\mu'$ has total degree at most $2\cdot D$ and $\lVert \mu'\rVert_\infty \leq T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2$, then it checks that $\picanon(\mu')=\mu$, and replies with a random prime $q\in \cP$ and a random morphism
%
$
\pirand : \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}] \to \FF_q
$ from $\cM_q$.
 The prover and verifier update:
 \iflncs
 $$
 \vv^{(1)}{} \gets \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}\left(\vv^{(1)}\right)\in \FF_{q}^{\codedim_1},\qquad
\vv^{(2)}{} \gets \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}\left(\vv^{(2)}\right)\in \FF_{q}^{\codedim_2},
$$
and $\mu\gets \pirand(\mu')\in \FF_q.$
 \else
\begin{align*}
\vv^{(1)}{} &\gets \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}\left(\vv^{(1)}\right)\in \FF_{q}^{\codedim_1},\\
\vv^{(2)}{} &\gets \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}\left(\vv^{(2)}\right)\in \FF_{q}^{\codedim_2},\\
\mu&\gets \pirand(\mu')\in \FF_q.
\end{align*}
\fi
The prover and verifier now set to prove the updated claim 
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:core_bitz_eq_new_0}
\langle \vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)}, \pi_q(\bff)\rangle = \mu \qquad \text{over } \FF_q.
\end{equation}

% [AI-drafted (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-16: LNCS-only one-line pointer replacing Remark r:skip_round_1_integers, which is copied verbatim into App. B; please review]
\iflncs Round 1 can also be skipped when $R=\ZZ$ and $\lVert \vv\rVert_{\infty}$ is ``small'', see \cref{r:skip_round_1_integers}.\else
\begin{remark}[Skipping Round 1 when $R=\ZZ$ and $\lVert \vv\rVert_{\infty}$ is ``small'']\label{r:skip_round_1_integers}
    This step can also be skipped when $R=\ZZ$ and all entries of $\vv^{(1)}$ are nonnegative and less than $\fieldboundsizeexponent$. In that case the claim to prove at the start of Round 2 is $\langle \vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)}, \bff\rangle = \mu $ over $\ZZ$. In this case the map $\pi_q$ in Round 2 is simply replaced by the identity map, $\FF_q$ is replaced by $\ZZ$, and $q$ in the range checks of Round 2 denotes $\max_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \vv^{(1)}_i+1$. In Phase 3, $\bm{\gamma}$ is  $\vv^{(1)}$. 
\end{remark}
\fi
\item \textbf{Round 2: transforming \eqref{e:core_bitz_eq_new_0} to $\codedim_2$ integer constraints with bounded integers.}
\iflncs\else\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]\fi
    \iflncs\else\item\fi{} The prover computes and sends integers $\mu_1, \ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2}\in \range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$ such that, over $\ZZ$, \begin{equation}\label{e:tensored_equality}\langle \bff_{*j}, \pi_q^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})\rangle = \mu_j \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2].\end{equation} 
\iflncs\else\item\fi{} The verifier checks that $\mu_j\in \range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$ for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$, and that \begin{equation}\label{e:tensor_decomposition_reconstruction}\sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi_q(\mu_j)\cdot \vv_j^{(2)}  = \mu\qquad \text{over } \FF_q.\end{equation} 
Next, prover and verifier will focus on certifying that \eqref{e:tensored_equality} holds for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$.

%In \cref{r:core_iop_optional_sumcheck} we describe a variation in which, instead of sending the elements $\mu_1, \ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2}$, the prover commits to their bit-decomposition over $\comfield$, and proves \eqref{e:tensor_decomposition_reconstruction} with \cref{c:LinModFieldBin}. This saves on proof size and verifier time when $\codedim$ is large.
%\item \textbf{Optional step: Projecting the integer claim to a prime field, and then lifting it back up to $\ZZ$.} The following step triggers in the following scenarios:
%\begin{itemize}
%    \item $\degevalfield>1$.
%    \item $\degevalfield=1$ and $q\geq (|\comfield|-1)/(\codedim_1+1)$.  
%\end{itemize} The verifier uniformly samples and sends a  prime $q'$ from $\cP$, and, if $\degevalfield>1$,  an element $\alpha\in \FF_{q'}$. Now update
%
%\begin{align*}
%\vv^{(1)}{} &\gets \pi_{q',\alpha}\circ \picanon^{-1}\left(\vv^{(1)}\right)\in \FF_{q'}^{\codedim_1},\\
%\mu_j &\gets \pi_{q'}^{-1}\circ \pi_{q',\alpha}(\mu_j) \in \range{0}{q'-1}\subseteq \ZZ, \qquad j\in [\codedim_2],\\
%q&\gets q',
%\end{align*}
%where, recall, $\pi_{q',\alpha}:\ZZ[X]\to \FF_{q'}$ is defined as coefficient-wise reduction modulo $q'$ and then evaluation of $X$ at $\alpha$.
%\end{itemize}
%
%\begin{remark}
%[Using any module homomorphism instead of $\pi_{q',\alpha}$]\label{r:otheSodule_morphisms}
%Instead of sampling $\pi_{q',\alpha}$, the verifier could sample other $\ZZ$-module homomorphisms from $\ZZ^{<\mathsf{deg}(R)}[X]$ to a prime field. For example it could sample a prime $q'$ and $\mathsf{deg}(R)$ random elements $\alpha_1,\ldots, \alpha_{\mathsf{deg}(R)}\in \FF_{q'}$, and then prover and verifier use the map $\pi_{q',\alpha_1,\ldots, \alpha_{\mathsf{deg}(R)}}(f(X)) = \sum_{i\in [\mathsf{deg}(R)]}\pi_{q'}(f_{i}) \cdot \alpha_i$, where $f(X)=\sum_{i\in [\mathsf{deg}(R)]} f_{i}\cdot X^{i-1}$. 
%$$
%For such a class of maps, the error term $(\mathsf{deg}(R)-1)/q_{\min}$ of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP} becomes $1/q_{\min}$.
%\end{remark}

%
%For ease of readability onwards, we use the same symbols $\vv^{(1)}$ and $q$ for the pre- and post-update vectors and primes in this step.
\iflncs\else\end{itemize}\fi
\item \textbf{Phase 3: Transforming the claim into a PESAT claim over {$\comfield$}, and invoking the IOR {$\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$}.}
\iflncs\else\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]\fi
\iflncs\else\item \fi
Prover and verifier compute and define \lncsmath{\bm{\gamma} = \pi_{q}^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}) \in \range{0}{q-1}^{\codedim_1}\subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim_1},}
%
and set to prove the following claim:
%
\lncsmath{
\prod_{i \in [\codedim_1]} g^{\bm{\gamma}_i\cdot \bff_{ij}}  = g^{\mu_j} \in \comfield, \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2].
}
This claim is equivalent to
\begin{equation}\label{e:exponent_tensored_equality}
\prod_{i \in [\codedim_1]} \left(\left(g^{\bm{\gamma}_i}-1\right)\cdot \pi_2(\bff_{ij}) +1\right)= g^{\mu_j}  \quad  \in \comfield, \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2].
\end{equation}
Notice that \iflncs\begingroup\small\fi\begin{equation}\label{e:Q_F2Z_intermediate}\left(\inp=((g^{\bm{\gamma}_i})_{i \in [\codedim_1]},\ (g^{\mu_j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}),\  \oracle= f, \ \wit=\picomfield(\bff)\right)\in \PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack).
\end{equation}
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
Let  $\bm{\mu}\defeq (\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2})$, $g^{\bm{\gamma}}\defeq (g^{\bm{\gamma}_i})_{i \in [\codedim_1]}$ and $g^{\bm{\mu}}\defeq (g^{\mu_j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}$.
% \ulrich{%
% Suggestion: 
% Maybe it is more clear, if we write in the above two equalities explicitly a $\bmod\: q$, or, add $\in \mathbb F_q$?
\iflncs\else\item\fi{} To prove \eqref{e:Q_F2Z_intermediate}, prover and verifier engage in the IOR $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$\footnote{Recall that, in our instantiation, $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$ is the GKR protocol applied to multiple grand products, cf.\ \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}.}   with input \iflncs$(\inp=(g^{\bm{\gamma}}, g^{\bm{\mu}}),\allowbreak\ \oracle= f,\allowbreak\ \wit=\picomfield(\bff))$.\else\lncsmath{\left(\inp=(g^{\bm{\gamma}}, g^{\bm{\mu}}),\  \oracle= f, \ \wit=\picomfield(\bff)\right).}\fi
%
%
% [AI-drafted (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-16: LNCS-only one-sentence gloss of the LIN relation, replacing its set-builder definition in the display below; please review]
 At the end of this IOR, they are left with the following claim to prove: 
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:iopp_mle_claims}
\left((\mathbf{z}, c), \ f, \ \picomfield(\bff)\right) \in \LIN( \comfield,\cC,\pack)\iflncs\else= \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\uu,\mu)\in \comfield^{\codedim}\times \comfield, \\&\oracle = g \in \comfield^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit= \bfg\in \FF_2^\codedim\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned} 
&g=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack (\bfg) \\    &\langle \uu, \bfg\rangle = \mu  \end{aligned}\right\}\fi
\end{equation} for some vector $\mathbf{z}\in \comfield^{\codedim}$ and field element $c\in \comfield$ output during the IOR $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$\iflncs; here $\LIN(\comfield,\cC,\pack)$ is the constrained-code relation of \cref{s:prelims_codes}, i.e.\ $f$ is the encoding of a packed bit vector $\bfg\in\FF_2^{\codedim}$ with $\langle \mathbf{z}, \bfg\rangle = c$\fi. 
\iflncs\else\end{itemize}\fi
\item \textbf{Phase 4: Using an IOP over {$\comfield$}.} Prover and  verifier engage in the IOP $\Pi_{\iopp}$  to certify the claim \eqref{e:iopp_mle_claims}.  If Round 0 (Out Of Domain sampling) triggered, they also use $\Pi_{\iopp}$ to certify the evaluation claim from Round 0.
\end{enumerate}
\end{construction}


\end{lncsmove}
\iflncs\paragraph{Costs of \cref{c:core_iop}.} \else In \cref{t:core_iop_complexity} we summarize the main costs of \cref{c:core_iop}.


\paragraph{Costs ($k\leq 1$).}\fi  In \Cref{t:core_iop_complexity} we summarize the costs of \cref{c:core_iop} beyond those of $\Pi_\red$ and $\Pi_\iopp$ (cf.\ \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy,s:instantiation}), for the specific case $k\leq 1$, corresponding to $R$ being of the form $\ZZ_n$ or $\ZZ_n[X]/(h(X))$ for some $n\geq 0$ and some $h(X)$. In our experiments we only use $k=0$, but future work of ours on proving lattice computations requires $k=1$. The number of rounds is  between $t_\red +t_\iopp$ and $2+t_\red +t_\iopp$, depending on whether Rounds 0 and 1 execute or not, where $t_\red, t_\iopp$ denote the number of rounds of $\Pi_\red,\Pi_\iopp$.

Note that in Rounds 0, 1 and 2 the prover sends no oracles, and so the only oracles and queries in \cref{c:core_iop} are those of $\Pi_\red$ and $\Pi_\iopp$. In the table, if Round 1 is skipped, then $q$ stands for $|R|$ when $R=\FF_q$, and for $\max_i \vv^{(1)}_i+1$ when $R=\ZZ$ (cf.\ \cref{r:skip_round_1_integers}). Regarding Phase 3, each of the exponentiations $g^{\bm{\gamma}_i}$ ($i\in [\codedim_1]$) and $g^{\mu_j}$ ($j\in [\codedim_2]$) takes at most $\lceil \log(q\cdot\codedim_1)\rceil \leq \comfieldexp$ multiplications.


In our instantiation, we set $\codedim_1\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$, $\codedim_2\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$, making the verifier succinct.  We assume implicitly that the instance is given to the verifier as $(\mu,\vv^{(1)},\vv^{(2)})$, so that it does not read $\vv$ in full.

When $k=0$ (so that $R=\ZZ_n$ for some $n$), we have $D=0$ and $T=1$.  When $k=1$ and $R=\ZZ_n[X]/(h(X))$ for some monic $h(X)$, then $D\leq \deg(h(X))-1$ and $T\leq D
+1$, cf.\ \eqref{e:ring_theta_params} for a definition of these quantities. If $n>0$, then $N_R\leq |\ZZ_n|=n$. Otherwise if $n=0$, $N_R \leq \lVert (\vv^{(1)}, \vv^{(2)})\rVert_\infty$.



\begin{table}[H]
  \centering
  \begin{tabular}{l|l|l}
    \toprule
    Round 0 & Prover & $O(\codedim/\comfieldexp)\cdot \comfield$ \\
     & Verifier & -- \\
     & Proof (bits) & $\comfieldexp$ \\
    \hline
    Round 1 & Prover & $O(\codedim\cdot (D+1))$ additions of $\log(\codedim_1 \cdot N_R)$-bit integers \\
     & & $O(\codedim_2\cdot (D+1)^2)$ additions of $\log(\codedim \cdot N_R^2)$-bit integers \\
     & & $O(\codedim_2\cdot (D+1)^2)$ multiplications of $\log(\codedim\cdot N_R^2)$-bit integers \\
     & & $O((\codedim_1+\codedim_2)\cdot (D+1))$ reductions mod $q$ of $\log(N_R)$-bit integers \\
     & & $O((\codedim_1+\codedim_2)\cdot (D+1))\cdot \FF_q$ \\
     & Verifier & $O(D+1)$ reductions mod $\Char(R)$ of $\log((D+1)\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2)$-bit integers \\
     &  & $O(D+1)\cdot R$ \\
     & & $O((\codedim_1+\codedim_2)\cdot (D+1))$ reductions mod $q$ of $\log(N_R)$-bit integers \\
     & & $O((\codedim_1+\codedim_2)\cdot (D+1))\cdot \FF_q$ \\
     & Proof (bits) & $(2 D + 1)\cdot \log((D+1)\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2)$ \\
    \hline
    Round 2 & Prover & $O(\codedim)$ additions of $\log(q\cdot \codedim_1)$-bit integers \\
     & Verifier & $O(\codedim_2)$ reductions mod $q$ of $\log(q\cdot \codedim_1)$-bit integers \\
     & & $O(\codedim_2)\cdot \FF_q$ \\
     & Proof (bits) & $\codedim_2\cdot \log(q\cdot \codedim_1)$ \\
    \hline
    Phase 3 & Prover & $O((\codedim_1+\codedim_2)\cdot \comfieldexp/w)\cdot \comfield$ \\
     & Verifier & same as prover \\
     & Proof (bits) & -- \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{Costs of \cref{c:core_iop} when $k\leq 1$, in addition to those of $\Pi_\red$ and $\Pi_\iopp$. By $N\cdot \comfield$, $N\cdot \FF_q$, $N\cdot R$ we indicate $N$ operations in the respective ring. Rounds 0 and 1 are optional.}
  \label{t:core_iop_complexity}
\end{table}

 We explain the costs of Round 1 in \cref{t:core_iop_complexity}, since these are arguably the less obvious ones. The first three prover costs correspond to computing $\mu'$. To obtain $\mu'$, the prover first computes, for each $j\in [\codedim_2]$, the polynomial $h_j\defeq \sum_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \bff_{ij}\cdot \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}_i)$. Since $\bff$ has bit entries, this requires no multiplications. Moreover, the coefficients of the $h_j$ have absolute value at most $\codedim_1\cdot N_R$. Hence all the $h_j$ can be computed with $O(\codedim\cdot (D+1))$ additions of $\log(\codedim_1\cdot N_R)$-bit integers (the first cost in the table). Next, the prover computes $\mu'=\sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}_j)\cdot h_j$. Each of the $\codedim_2$ products in this sum takes at most $(D+1)^{2}$ multiplications of $\log(\codedim_1\cdot N_R)$-bit integers (the third cost), and adding all the terms together gives the second cost.

The last two costs of both the prover and the verifier in Round 1 come from computing $\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(i)})$ for $i=1,2$. For each entry of $\vv^{(i)}$, this amounts to reducing $D+1$ integers in the range $\range{0}{N_R-1}$ modulo a prime, and then taking a linear combination of the results in $\FF_q$. 



 The first two verifier costs in Round 1 correspond to computing $\picanon(\mu')$. For that, it first reduces the at most $2D+1$ coefficients of $\mu'$ (which are integers of at most $\log(T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2)$ bits) modulo $\Char(R)$ and evaluates the resulting polynomial at $\eta_1$ in $R$ using Horner's rule, and then compares the result with $\mu$. 
 


\begin{comment}
\begin{remark}[Reducing proof size by committing to the elements $\mu_1, \ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2}$]  \label{r:core_iop_optional_sumcheck}
uation of $(g^{\mu_j})_j$ } When $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$ is such that $\codedim_2$ is large, the elements $\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2}\in \ZZ$ sent at Round 2 of \cref{c:core_iop} may start to dominate the proof size. For example, for $\codedim_2=2^{13}$ and $\mu_i$ of $128$ bits, the elements $\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2}$ amount to $131$ KB.  To mitigate this cost,  one can have the prover commit to the vector $(\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2})$ and then prove \eqref{e:tensor_decomposition_reconstruction}, i.e.\ 
%
$$
\langle \bff_{*j}, \pi_q^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})\rangle = \mu_j \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2].
$$
%
using an IOP for constrained codes. Concretely, the prover commits to the vector $\bitstext(\mu)$ of bits forming the elements $(\mu_1, \ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2})$ using \ftwoz, and then proves \eqref{e:tensor_decomposition_reconstruction} using \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} from \cref{s: general_bitz}.   %At the end of \cref{c:core_iop}, the prover is left with an inner product claim for the vector $\pi_2(\bitstext(\mu))$.  
%
\end{remark}
\end{comment}

\iflncs
% [LNCS-only verbatim copy of Remark r:skip_round_1_integers (hidden in the LNCS body), 2026-09-16; please review]
\begin{remark}[Skipping Round 1 when $R=\ZZ$ and $\lVert \vv\rVert_{\infty}$ is ``small'']\label{r:skip_round_1_integers}
    This step can also be skipped when $R=\ZZ$ and all entries of $\vv^{(1)}$ are nonnegative and less than $\fieldboundsizeexponent$. In that case the claim to prove at the start of Round 2 is $\langle \vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)}, \bff\rangle = \mu $ over $\ZZ$. In this case the map $\pi_q$ in Round 2 is simply replaced by the identity map, $\FF_q$ is replaced by $\ZZ$, and $q$ in the range checks of Round 2 denotes $\max_{i\in [\codedim_1]} \vv^{(1)}_i+1$. In Phase 3, $\bm{\gamma}$ is  $\vv^{(1)}$. 
\end{remark}
\fi
\begin{lncsmove}{IopB2}
\begin{remark}[Succinct verifier and proofs]\label{r:succinct_verifier}
   \iflncs In \cref{t:core_iop_complexity} we provide concrete costs of \cref{c:core_iop}. In particular, \else By \cref{t:core_iop_complexity},\fi choosing $\codedim_1 =O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ and $\codedim_2 =O(\sqrt{\codedim})$, the verifier and the proof size of \cref{c:core_iop} have asymptotic cost $O(\sqrt{\codedim})$ (where $\codedim$ is the bit-length of the witness). One also needs to make sure the verifier handles $\vv$ via the tensor factors $\vv^{(1)}, \vv^{(2)}$, so that it does not read $\vv$ entirely.
\end{remark}
\end{lncsmove}



\begin{lncsmove}{IopC}
\subsection{Soundness and completeness}\label{s:soundness_core_iop}


For readability, we temporarily denote $$\PESAT=\PESAT(\comfield, Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\cC, \pack),\qquad \LIN = \LIN(\comfield, \cC, \pack).$$




\begin{theorem}[Completeness and round-by-round knowledge soundness of \cref{c:core_iop}]\label{t:thm_core_IOPP} 
   Let $\delta \in (0,1)$ be a proximity parameter within the unique decoding radius of $\cC$ (cf.\ \iflncs\cref{a:prelims_codes}\else\cref{s:prelims_codes}\fi), and assume that Round 0 (OOD sampling) of \cref{c:core_iop} is skipped. We treat the general case in \cref{a:OOD}. Assume 
   %
   $$\max\cP < \fieldboundsizeexponent.$$ 
   %
   Assume $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$ is a perfectly complete $t_\red$-round IOR from $\PESAT$ to $\LIN$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness  with relaxation $(\PESAT^\delta,\LIN^{\delta})$, with errors  $(\ksred_1, \ldots, \ksred_{t_\red})$. Assume $\Pi_{\iopp}$ is a perfectly complete $t_{\iopp}$-round IOP  for $\LIN$ (i.e.\ an IOR from $\LIN$ to the trivial relation $\bot$) with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\LIN^{\delta}, \bot)$ (see \cref{s:IORs}), and errors \lncsmath{\ksiopp_1,\ldots, \ksiopp_{t_\iopp}.}
%
    Then \cref{c:core_iop} is a perfectly complete $(1+t_\red+t_\iopp)$-round IOP for $\LinBitsDual$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\LinBitsDual^{\delta}, \bot)$, with errors \lncsmath{\kappa_0, (\ksred_i)_{i\in [t_\red]}, (\ksiopp_i)_{i\in [t_\iopp]},} where  
    %
     \lncsmath{
    \kappa_0 = \theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)
     }
    is the quantity defined in \eqref{e:theta_def}\footnote{Note that $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$ depends on the public input $\inp$. In practice one can obtain a uniform bound on $\kappa_0$ by taking the maximum $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$ across all public inputs that are admitted by the protocol.}. In particular, if the sets $\cM_q$ are as in \cref{l:examples_morphisms}, then one can take
    $$
    \kappa_0 = \frac{2\cdot D}{q_{\min}} + \frac{\log\left(2\cdot T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2\right)}{|\cP|\cdot \lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor},
    $$
    where $q_{\min}$ is the smallest prime in $\cP$ and $D$, $T$, $N_R$ are as in \eqref{e:ring_theta_params}.
    If Round 1 is not executed, the error term $\kappa_0$ and its round are absent, and \cref{c:core_iop} has $t_\red+t_\iopp$ rounds. % The case where $\delta$ exceeds the unique decoding radius of $\cC$, so that Round 0 is executed, is treated in \cref{a:OOD} (\cref{t:thm_core_IOPP_OOD}) under additional hypotheses on $\Pi_\red$ and $\Pi_\iopp$; it adds one round and an error term $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}$.
    %Recall that $q$ is either the characteristic of $R$ if $R$ is a finite field, or $q=\codedim_1 \cdot \lVert \vv\rVert_{\infty} +1$ if $R=\ZZ$.
%
%
    %If  $\evalfield$ is a prime field, then \cref{c:core_iop} is a $t_\red+t_\iopp$-round IOP with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\LinBitsDual^\delta, \bot)$, with errors $(\ksred_i)_{i\in [t_\red]}, (\ksiopp_i)_{i\in [t_\iopp]}$.
\end{theorem}
\iflncs
% [AI-drafted (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-16, shortened at the author's request: LNCS-only proof sketch of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP} saying how \cref{l:examples_morphisms,l:reduction_lemma} enter; condensed from \cref{a:core_iop_proofs}; to be rewritten by the author and to be covered by the AI-usage paragraph; please review]
\begin{proof}  Deferred to \cref{a:core_iop_proofs}. In short, by the composition of IORs (\cref{r:ior_compositoin}) it suffices to analyze Rounds 1 and 2, which form an IOR from $\LinBitsDual$ to $\PESAT$ with errors $(\kappa_0,0)$.  In Round 1, a false claim $\langle\pi(\bff),\vv\rangle\neq\mu$ that passes the checks means that the nonzero polynomial $h\defeq \langle \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})\otimes\picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}),\bff\rangle-\mu'\in \ZZ[X_1,\ldots,X_k]$ satisfies $\pirand(h)=0$. But $h$ has total degree at most $2\cdot D$ and $\lVert h\rVert_\infty\leq 2\cdot T\cdot\codedim\cdot N_R^2$ because of the verifier's checks, the bound on $\picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(i)})$, and the fact that $\bff$ has bit entries (which, intuitively speaking, is guaranteed by the PCS, which is a PCS used to commit to bit vectors). Hence, the event $\pirand(h)=0$ happens with probability at most $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)=\kappa_0$ by \eqref{e:theta_def}. The specific expression of $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$ mentioned in the theorem follows from \cref{l:examples_morphisms}. 

    In Round 2 we only need to ensure that \eqref{e:tensored_equality} occurs if and only if \eqref{e:exponent_tensored_equality} occurs. Equivalently, we need to ensure that \eqref{e:tensored_equality} holds on the integers if and only if it holds modulo $|\comfield|-1$. This is the case because of the verifier checks on $\mu_j$,  $\max\cP<\fieldboundsizeexponent$,  and $\bff$ contains only bits. % exact because $\max\cP<\fieldboundsizeexponent$ keeps the exponents of $g$ below its order.
\end{proof}
\fi
\end{lncsmove}




\begin{lncsmove}{IopProofsA}
 To prove \cref{l:examples_morphisms} we will use the following result:
\begin{lemma}\label{l:reduction_lemma}
Let $h\in \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_m]$ be a nonzero polynomial of total degree $\leq d$ and with $\lVert h\rVert_\infty\leq M$ for some $M\geq 1$. Let $\cP$ be a set of primes, and let $q_{\min}$ be the smallest prime in $\cP$. Given a prime $q$ and $\bm{\alpha}\in \FF_q^m$, write $\pi_{q,\bm{\alpha}}(h)\defeq \pi_q(h)(\bm{\alpha})\in \FF_q$. Then 
%
$$
\Pr_{\substack{q\gets \cP,\\\bm{\alpha} \gets \FF_q^m}}[\pi_{q,\bm{\alpha}}(h)=0] \leq \frac{\log M}{\lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor\cdot |\cP|} + \frac{d}{q_{\min}}.
$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $\cE_{\mathsf{prime}}$ be the event that all coefficients of $h$ are  multiples of $q$. Then, by the Schwartz--Zippel lemma, \lncsmath{\Pr_{\substack{q\gets \cP,\\\bm{\alpha} \gets \FF_q^m}}[\pi_{q,\bm{\alpha}}(h)=0\mid \neg \cE_{\mathsf{prime}}] \leq  \frac{d}{q_{\min}}.}
%
Now, every nonzero coefficient $c$ of $h$ is divisible by at most $\log M/\log q_{\min}\leq \log M/\lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor$ primes from $\cP$, because the product of $m'$ distinct such primes divides $c$, hence $q_{\min}^{m'}\leq |c|\leq M$. Hence, the probability that $\cE_{\mathsf{prime}}$ occurs is at most $(\log M/\lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor)/|\cP|$, and the lemma follows.
%
\end{proof}



\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{l:examples_morphisms}]
Let $h\in \ZZ[X_1,\ldots, X_{k}]$ be a polynomial of total degree at most $2\cdot D$, with $\lVert h\rVert_\infty\leq 2\cdot T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2$, and with $\picanon^{R}(h)\neq 0$ (cf.\ the definition of $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$). In particular $h$ is nonzero, and $\lVert h\rVert_\infty\leq M\defeq 2\cdot T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2$. Since $h$ is nonzero and has total degree at most $2\cdot D$, \cref{l:reduction_lemma} (applied with $m=k$ variables and total degree $2\cdot D$) yields \iflncs the result.\else $$\Pr_{\substack{q\gets \cP,\\ \bm{\alpha} \gets \FF_q^{k}}}[\pi_q(h)(\bm{\alpha})=0] \leq \frac{\log M}{\lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor\cdot |\cP|} + \frac{2\cdot D}{q_{\min}},$$ as needed.\fi
%
\end{proof}
\end{lncsmove}
\begin{lncsmove}{IopProofsB}


%The following lemma bounds the probability, over the OOD challenge $\zeta$ of Round 0, that two distinct bit vectors close to the oracle share the same OOD evaluation. Throughout, for $\zeta\in \comfield$ we write, as in Round 0 of \cref{c:core_iop}, $$\bm{\zeta}= \left(\zeta^{2^0}, \zeta^{2^1}, \ldots, \zeta^{2^{\log(\codedim/\comfieldexp)-1}}\right)\in \comfield^{\log (\codedim/\comfieldexp)}$$ for the corresponding evaluation point.


\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}] 
%
The completeness of \cref{c:core_iop} is clear from its description.  Recall that, by hypothesis, Round 0 (OOD sampling) is skipped; the case where Round 0 is executed is treated in \cref{a:OOD}. 

We decompose  \cref{c:core_iop} into three IORs $\Pi_1, \Pi_2, \Pi_3$.  The first one, $\Pi_1$, is an IOR from $\LinBitsDual$ to $\PESAT$, and it consists of applying Rounds 1 and 2 of \cref{c:core_iop}, and outputting $$\left(\inp'=(g^{\bm{\gamma}}, g^{\bm{\mu}}),\  \oracle'= f, \ \wit'=\picomfield(\bff)\right),$$ as described in Phase 3 of \cref{c:core_iop}. Formally, $\Pi_1$ is a $2$-round IOR whose second verifier message is empty: the prover first sends $\mu'$, the verifier replies with $(q,\pirand)$, and the prover then sends $\bm{\mu}=(\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2})$. The second IOR, $\Pi_2$, is simply $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$, reducing $\PESAT$ to $\LIN$. Finally, the third IOR, $\Pi_3$, is simply the IOP $\Pi_{\iopp}$ for $\LIN$.


 Below we show that $\Pi_1$  has round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\LinBitsDual^{\delta}, \PESAT^{\delta})$, with errors $(\kappa_0, 0)$, assuming Round 1 is not skipped. The second verifier message of $\Pi_1$ is empty, hence the last prover message of $\Pi_1$ can be sent together with the first prover message of $\Pi_2$, and thus  \cref{c:core_iop} has $1+t_\red+t_\iopp$ rounds. The theorem then follows from the composition of IORs result (cf.\ \cref{r:ior_compositoin}). 




 At the end of the proof we discuss the case when Round 1 is skipped. 



\paragraph{Completeness and soundness of $\Pi_1$.}
We describe a knowledge state function $\kstate$. Let $\inp=(\mu,\vv)\in \evalfield \times \evalfield^{\codedim}$ and  $\oracle=f\in \comfield^{\codelength}$. Let $\tr$ be a partial interaction transcript for $\Pi_1$. We start by setting $\kstate$ so that the first  condition in \cref{def:knowledge-state-function} is met. Precisely, if   $\tr=\emptyset$, we set $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr,\ww) = 1$ if and only if $(\inp, \oracle, \ww)\in \LinBitsDual^{\delta}$. Further,  if $\tr=(\mu')$ consists of a single prover message, we set $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\mu',\ww)=\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\emptyset,\ww)$, so that the second condition in \cref{def:knowledge-state-function} holds for the first prover message round.

After the verifier's message, $\tr$ is as follows:
%
$$
\tr=(\mu', (q,\pirand)) \in \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}] \times \left(\cP \times \cM_q\right).
$$
%
We define $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr,\ww)=1$ if and only if  the verifier's degree and range checks on $\mu'$ in Round 1 pass,  $\ww=\bff\in \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$,  $\bff=(\bff_{*j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\in \left(\bits^{\codedim_1}\right)^{\codedim_2}$, and
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:core_iop_proof_eq1}
    \begin{aligned}
&\picanon(\mu')=\mu,\\
&\langle \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}) \otimes \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}), \pi_q(\bff)\rangle = \pirand(\mu') \quad \text{ over } \FF_q,\\
&\hamming(f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ\pack\circ \pi_2(\bff))< \delta.
    \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
%
We continue with our definition of $\kstate$.  Let $\tr=(\mu', (q,\pirand), \bm{\mu})$ for a prover message $\bm{\mu}=(\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2})\in \range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}^{\codedim_2}$, or let $\tr$ be the full transcript obtained by appending the empty second verifier message to $(\mu', (q,\pirand), \bm{\mu})$. We set $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr,\ww)=1$ if and only if the verifier accepts on $\tr$ (i.e.\ its checks in Rounds 1 and 2 pass) and $(\inp',\oracle',\ww)\in \PESAT^{\delta}$, where $(\inp',\oracle')$ is the output of the verifier, namely
%
\iflncs\begingroup\small\fi
$$
\inp'=(g^{\bm{\gamma}}, g^{\bm{\mu}}), \qquad \oracle'= f, \qquad \text{with } \bm{\gamma} = \pi_{q}^{-1}\left(\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})\right) \in \range{0}{q-1}^{\codedim_1}\subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim_1}.
$$
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
%
 Thus $\kstate$ satisfies the third condition in \cref{def:knowledge-state-function}.



We define an extractor as $\rbrextractor(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr,\ww)=\ww$. It remains to prove two statements: first, that
%
\[
  \Pr_{q,\pirand}\!\left[
  \exists\,\ww \ \left| \ \begin{aligned}
  &\kstate\bigl(\inp,\oracle,\mu',\rbrextractor(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak(\mu', (q,\pirand)),\ww)\bigr)=0\\
  \wedge\ &\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak(\mu', (q,\pirand)), \ww)=1
  \end{aligned}
  \right.\right]
  \ \le\ \kappa_0;
\]
and second, that $\kstate$ satisfies the second condition in \cref{def:knowledge-state-function} for transcripts where the prover is about to send $\bm{\mu}$ (its second message).

\bigskip

We start with the first statement. Assume $$\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak(\mu', (q,\pirand)), \ww)=1 \quad \text{and} \quad \kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\mu',  \ww)=\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\ww)=0.$$
The latter occurs if and only if  $(\inp,\oracle,\ww)\notin \LinBitsDual^{\delta}$. The former implies that $\ww=\bff=(\bff_{*j})_{j\in[\codedim_2]}\in \bits^\codedim$ satisfies \eqref{e:core_iop_proof_eq1}; in particular $\hamming(f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ\pack\circ \pi_2(\bff))< \delta$, and hence, since $(\inp,\oracle,\ww)\notin \LinBitsDual^{\delta}$, necessarily
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:break_soundness_2}
\langle \pi(\bff), \vv\rangle \neq \mu \qquad \text{over } R.
\end{equation}
%
Define the polynomial $$h\defeq  \langle \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}) \otimes \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}), \bff \rangle- \mu' \ \in \ \ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_{k}].
$$
Since $\picanon$ is a ring homomorphism satisfying $\picanon\circ \picanon^{-1}=\mathsf{id}$ and restricting to $\pi$ on $\ZZ$, the first condition in \eqref{e:core_iop_proof_eq1} gives $$\picanon(h) = \langle \pi(\bff), \vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)} \rangle- \picanon(\mu') = \langle \pi(\bff), \vv\rangle - \mu,$$ which is nonzero by \eqref{e:break_soundness_2}. In particular $h\neq 0$. On the other hand, since $\pirand$ is a ring homomorphism restricting to $\pi_q$ on $\ZZ$, the second condition in \eqref{e:core_iop_proof_eq1} states precisely that $\pirand(h)=0$. We claim that $h$ has total degree at most $2\cdot D$ and satisfies $$\lVert h \rVert_{\infty}\leq 2\cdot T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2.$$ Indeed, the lifts $\picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}_i)$ and $\picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}_j)$ have total degree at most $D$ and coefficients of absolute value at most $N_R$, so each product $\picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}_i)\cdot \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}_j)$ has total degree at most $2\cdot D$ and coefficients of absolute value at most $T\cdot N_R^2$ by the definition of $T$. Further, by the verifier's checks in Round 1, $$\lVert \mu'\rVert_{\infty} \leq T\cdot \codedim\cdot N_R^2$$ and the total degree of $\mu'$ is at most $2\cdot D$. The claim follows now from the fact that the entries of $\bff$ are bits and there are $\codedim$ of them.


Since additionally $\picanon(h)\neq 0$, the polynomial $h$ belongs to the class of polynomials over which $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$ is defined. Finally,  since $\delta$ is within the unique decoding radius of $\cC$, the code $\cC$ is $(\delta,1)$-list-decodable, and hence there is at most one $\ww$ such that \ $\hamming(f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ\pack\circ \pi_2(\ww))< \delta$.  Therefore  $$
\begin{aligned}
&\Pr_{q,\pirand}[\exists \ww  \text{ such that } (\inp,\oracle,\ww)\notin \LinBitsDual^\delta \text{ and } \kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak(\mu', (q,\pirand)), \ww)=1]\\ \leq &\Pr_{q\gets \cP,\ \pirand\gets \cM_q}[\pirand(h)=0] \leq \theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim) = \kappa_0,
\end{aligned}
$$
%
where the last inequality holds by the definition of $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$.

\bigskip

It remains to prove the second statement, namely: if $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak(\mu', (q,\pirand)),\ww)=0$, then $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak(\mu', (q,\pirand), \bm{\mu}),\ww)=0$ for every prover message $\bm{\mu}=(\mu_1,\ldots, \mu_{\codedim_2})\in \range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}^{\codedim_2}$. Suppose towards a contradiction that $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak(\mu', (q,\pirand), \bm{\mu}),\ww)=1$, i.e.\ that the verifier accepts and $(\inp',\oracle',\ww)\in \PESAT^{\delta}$. Then $\ww=\bff=(\bff_{*j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\in \bits^\codedim$, $\hamming( f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ \pi_2(\bff)) < \delta$, and, for all $j\in [\codedim_2]$, $$g^{\langle \bff_{*j}, \bm{\gamma}\rangle} = \prod_{i \in [\codedim_1]} \left(\left(g^{\bm{\gamma}_i}-1\right)\cdot \pi_2(\bff_{ij}) +1\right)= g^{\mu_j},$$ and hence $$\langle \bff_{*j}, \bm{\gamma}\rangle = \mu_j \mod \mathsf{ord}(g),$$ where $\mathsf{ord}(g)$ denotes the multiplicative order of $g$. Now, since $\bm{\gamma}_i\in \range{0}{q-1}$, $\mu_j\in \range{0}{(q-1)\cdot \codedim_1}$, and $\bff_{*j}$ contains bits, we have $|\langle \bff_{*j}, \bm{\gamma}\rangle - \mu_j| \leq \codedim_1 \cdot (q-1)$. Since $g$ is a generator of $\comfield^*$ and $q\leq \max\cP< \fieldboundsizeexponent$, we have $$\mathsf{ord}(g) = |\comfield|-1> \codedim_1\cdot \max\cP \geq \codedim_1\cdot q> \codedim_1 \cdot (q-1),$$ and hence
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:mu_j_integer_equalities}
\langle \bff_{*j}, \bm{\gamma}\rangle = \mu_j \qquad \text{over } \ZZ, \qquad \text{ for all } j\in [\codedim_2].
\end{equation}
%
Applying $\pi_q$ to \eqref{e:mu_j_integer_equalities}, and using that $\pi_q(\bm{\gamma})=\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})$, we obtain $$\langle \pi_q(\bff_{*j}), \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})\rangle = \pi_q(\mu_j) \quad \text{ over } \FF_q, \qquad \text{ for all } j\in [\codedim_2].$$ Since the verifier accepts, its check \eqref{e:tensor_decomposition_reconstruction} in Round 2 passes, i.e.\ $\sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi_q(\mu_j)\cdot \left(\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)})\right)_j = \pirand(\mu')$. Now
%
\begin{align*}
&\langle \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)}) \otimes \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)}), \pi_q(\bff)\rangle\\ =& \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \langle \pi_q(\bff_{*j}), \pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})\rangle \cdot \left(\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)})\right)_j \\ =& \sum_{j\in [\codedim_2]} \pi_q(\mu_j)\cdot \left(\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}(\vv^{(2)})\right)_j = \pirand(\mu').
\end{align*}
%
Hence the second condition in \eqref{e:core_iop_proof_eq1} holds. The first condition in \eqref{e:core_iop_proof_eq1}, i.e.\ $\picanon(\mu')=\mu$, holds because the verifier accepts, and the third one, i.e.\ $\hamming(f, \Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack \circ\pi_2(\bff))<\delta$, holds because $(\inp',\oracle',\ww)\in \PESAT^{\delta}$. Hence $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak(\mu', (q,\pirand)),\ww)=1$, a contradiction.




\paragraph{The case where Round 1 is skipped.}  In this case, the messages $\mu'$ and $(q,\pirand)$ and the error $\kappa_0$ disappear, and essentially the arguments above apply after replacing $\pirand\circ \picanon^{-1}$ with the identity map on $\FF_q$ or on $\ZZ$. In the latter case,  $\pi_q$ is also replaced by the identity map, cf.\ \cref{r:skip_round_1_integers}.

\end{proof}
\end{lncsmove}








\begin{lncsmove}{IopD}
\subsection{The general LinBitsRings relation}\label{s:general_LinBitsDual}


\renewcommand{\modulemorphism}{\psi}
\renewcommand{\ringmorphism}{\psi}
\renewcommand{\moduleonebound}{{S_{\leq B}^{<D_S}}}

We now proceed to describe the full relation we seek to work with in this section.  Compared to $\LinBitsDual(\bits, \pi, R, \cC)$,   the witness $\bff$ will now have entries in an arbitrary finitely generated ring $S$,  instead of in $\bits\subseteq \ZZ$.  These entries will be restricted to a bounded subset of $S$, so that each witness can be expressed as a pre-specified linear expression (over $S$) of a string of bits.


We set the following additional notation:
\iflncs\else\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]\fi
 \iflncs\else\item\fi{} Let $S$ be a ring, possibly different from $R$, and let $\psi:S \to R$ be a ring homomorphism. %Let $M$ be a $S$-module and  let $\psi: \moduleone \to R$ be a module homomorphism (with underlying 
\iflncs\else\item \fi
% [AI-drafted formula (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-16, at the author's request: the monomials written out; both versions; please review]
Let $D_S\geq 1$ be a degree bound, let $1,\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_{k_S}$ be a generating set of $S$ (\cref{s:prelims_algebra}), and let $\gamma_1, \gamma_2,\ldots, \gamma_\moduledegree\in S$ be the distinct elements of the form \lncsmath{\eta_1^{a_1}\cdots \eta_{k_S}^{a_{k_S}}}  with $a_1,\ldots,a_{k_S}\geq 0$ and $a_1+\cdots+a_{k_S}< D_S$, i.e.\ the monomials of total degree less than $D_S$ in the generators, so that $\moduledegree$ is the number of such monomials. Concretely $\moduledegree\leq \binom{D_S-1+k_S}{k_S}$.
\iflncs\else\item\fi{}  
%\lncsnote{Could be shortened} 

Recall from \cref{s:prelims_algebra} that $\moduleonebound$ consists of the sums $\sum_{i\in [\moduledegree]} h_i\cdot \gamma_i$ with $h_i \in \range{0}{2^B-1}_S$, and that $\ww_S$ denotes the projection of an integer vector $\ww$ to $S$ (see \eqref{e:integers_subscript_proj}).
     \iflncs\else\item\fi{} Let $\codedim_0$ be a vector witness length, so that our witnesses are $\bff\in S^{\codedim_0}$.    
     \iflncs\else\end{itemize}\fi

     We will use the natural homomorphism $\pi_{\Char(S)} : \ZZ \to S$ consisting in reducing an element modulo the characteristic of $S$. 

\begin{definition}[Bitification of the witness]\label{d:bitification}
Let $\bff \in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}$. Let $\codedim$ be a  vector length, called \emph{bitification  length}. Let $L\in S^{\codedim_0 \times \codedim}$ be a matrix, called  \emph{bitification matrix}. Let $\bfg\in \bits^\codedim\subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$ be a vector filled with integer bits. We say $\bfg$ is a
\emph{bitification} of $\bff$ (with respect to $L$)  if
%
\lncsmath{
\bff = L \cdot \bfg_S,
}
%
where for a vector $\ww$ of integer bits, $\ww_S$ denotes its natural projection to a vector with entries the elements $\bits_S \subseteq S$. 
%
Additionally, we require that\iflncs\else\footnote{This condition is required to guarantee that extractors obtain witnesses in  the specific subset $\moduleonebound$ of $S$. In some applications, only proving that the prover knows a witness in $S$ may suffice (even if the relation specifies witnesses with entries  $\moduleonebound^{\codedim_0}$), and in that case the condition can be dropped.}\fi $$L\cdot \bfg_S\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0} \qquad \text{for all } \bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}.$$  This  condition holds for the standard bitification matrix below. 


The choice of $L$ and a method for building a bitification of $\bff$  can be customized. Below, we describe what we call the \emph{standard bitification} procedure. This can always be used for arbitrary finitely generated rings $S$, and is suitable when $\bff$ has entries of more or less uniform bit size.  In some scenarios (see \cref{r: non_uniform_bit_decomposition}) one may choose different more convenient approaches.

For a fixed $L$ and $\bff$, bitifications of $\bff$ are not unique in general. Nevertheless, abusing the notation, we often denote them as \lncsmath{\bitstext(\bff).} 
%

A \emph{standard bitification} of $\bff\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}
$ is obtained as follows: write
%$\bff=(\bff_{*1}, \ldots, \bff_{*\moduledegree})$ 
$\bff=\sum_{j\in [\moduledegree]} \bff_{*j}\cdot \gamma_j$
for some $\bff_{*j}\in \left(\range{0}{2^B-1}_S\right)^{\codedim_0}$, $j\in [\moduledegree]$.  Then write $$\bff_{*j}= \sum_{k=1}^{B}\  \bff_{*jk}\cdot \pi_{\Char(S)}(2^{k-1}) $$ for some vectors \lncsmath{\bff_{*jk}\in \{0,1\}_S^{\codedim_0}\subseteq S^{\codedim_0},} for all $j,k$.  We  define  
\iflncs 
$\codedim\defeq \codedim_0\cdot \moduledegree\cdot B$ and let
\lncsmath{
\bitstext(\bff)\in \bits^{\codedim} \subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim}
}
\else
$$
\begin{aligned}
& \codedim\defeq \codedim_0\cdot \moduledegree\cdot B,\\
&\bitstext(\bff)\in \bits^{\codedim} \subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim}\end{aligned}$$
\fi be the vector with integer bit entries obtained by concatenating the vectors $\bff_{*jk}$ and lifting and reordering their entries to $\bits \subseteq \ZZ$ in a natural way, i.e.\ \begin{equation}\label{e:bits_of_f}\bitstext(\bff)\defeq \left(\mathsf{lift}(\bff_{ijk})\right)_{i\in [\codedim_0], k\in [B], j\in [\moduledegree]}\in \bits^{\codedim} \subseteq \ZZ^{\codedim},\end{equation}
where $\mathsf{lift}$ is the identity function if $\Char(S)=0$,  and  $\mathsf{lift} = \pi_{\Char(S)}^{-1}$ if $\Char(S)>0$.  Note that the vectors $\bff_{*j}$ are not uniquely defined, hence the expression ``a bitification'' instead of ``the bitification''. 

Defining 
$$L=\mathsf{Id}_{\codedim_0\times \codedim_0} \otimes (1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1})_S \otimes (\gamma_1, \ldots, \gamma_d) \in S^{\codedim_0\times \codedim},$$
we have
\lncsmath{
\bff = L \cdot \bitstext(\bff)_S.
}
\end{definition}
%
%
Again, let $\cC$ be a linear code over $\comfield$ of dimension $\codedim/\comfieldexp$ and blocklength $\codelength$. 


\begin{definition}\label{d:general_linbitsrings} The \emph{general LinBitsRings}  relation is defined as 
%
\iflncs\begingroup\small\fi
\begin{align*}
 &\LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism,R,  \cC,L)\\ &\iflncs\else\qquad\fi \defeq \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\mu, \vv)\in R  \times R^{ \codedim_0}, \\
 &\oracle = f \in \comfield^{\codelength}, \\ &\wit= \bff\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned}  
 &f = \Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)),\\
 &\langle \modulemorphism(\bff), \vv \rangle = \mu  \qquad  \text{in } R,\\
 &  \bff = L \cdot (\bitstext(\bff))_S \ \text{for some } \bitstext(\bff)\in \bits^{\codedim}\subseteq\ZZ^\codedim
 \end{aligned} \right\}.
\end{align*}
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
\end{definition}
%
When there is no risk of confusion, we denote $\LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism,\moduletwo,  \cC, L)$ simply by $\LinModFieldBin$. The Core LinBitsRings relation is precisely $\LinModFieldBin$ after taking $S=\ZZ$, $B=1$, $\modulemorphism = \pi_{\Char(R)}$, and $L=\mathsf{Id}_{\codedim\times \codedim}$ (the standard bitification matrix in this case, so that $\codedim_0=\codedim$).

%\renewcommand{\modulemorphism}

\iflncs\else For $\delta\in (0,1)$, we let $\LinModFieldBin^\delta$ be the relation $\LinModFieldBin$ after replacing the constraint $f=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$ by $\hamming(f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)))<\delta$, as in \cref{s:linmodfieldbin}.\fi

\begin{remark}[Non-uniform bit-decomposition sizes]\label{r: non_uniform_bit_decomposition} In practical settings, it is possible that not all entries of $\bff$ have the same bit size. For example, some chunks of $\bff$ may be comprised of elements from $\{0,1\}_S$, while others may be comprised of generic ring elements. However, the standard bitification procedure from \cref{d:bitification} allocates $\moduledegree\cdot B$ bits for every entry of $\bff$, no matter its size. To avoid this, one can find alternative approaches to defining $L$ and $\bitstext(\bff)$. 

For efficiency purposes, one needs to make sure that $L$ has enough structure to later support  tensor decomposing certain vectors, as discussed in \cref{r:tensor_dec_u}. In any case, one can always use the matrix $L$ above, which in general does possess enough structure as argued in \cref{r:tensor_dec_u}.
%
\end{remark}

\iflncs\else See \cref{r:integrated_range_checks} for comments on $\LinBitsDual$ having (generalized) range checks in its definition and about \ftwoz\ proving them, in a sense, ``for free''.\fi


\subsection{The \ftwoz\  IOP for the general LinBitsRings relation}\label{s: general_bitz}



\renewcommand{\modulemorphism}{\psi}
\renewcommand{\ringmorphism}{\psi}
\renewcommand{\moduleonebound}{{S_{\leq B}^{<D_S}}}

Next we describe our IOP for  the general relation $\LinModFieldBin$.  We assume the notation of \cref{s:general_LinBitsDual}. Let  $L$ be a \emph{bitification} matrix as in \cref{d:bitification}, with $L\in S^{\codedim_0 \times \codedim}$ so that $\codedim$ is the vector length of the vector $\bitstext(\bff)$. For the standard bitification matrix   $L=\mathsf{Id}_{\codedim_0 \times \codedim_0} \otimes (1,\ldots, 2^{B-1})_S\otimes (\gamma_1, \ldots, \gamma_d)$, we have $\codedim=\codedim_0\cdot \moduledegree\cdot B$. Let further $\codedim_1,\codedim_2$ be tensor decomposition parameters (to be used in \cref{c:core_iop}) such that $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$. We assume that $\cC$ is a linear code over $\comfield$ of dimension $\codedim/\comfieldexp$. %, which is a $\codedim_2$-interleaving of a code $\cC_1$ over $\comfield$ of dimension $\codedim_1/2^{\comfieldexp}$.
%
We will need the following result.


\begin{lemma}\label{l:bitify}
    Let $\bff\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}$,  let $\vv\in \moduletwo^{\codedim_0}$, and let $\bitstext(\bff)$ be a bitification of $\bff$ with respect to $L$. Then 
    \iflncs
    $$
\langle \psi(\bff), \vv \rangle = \langle \bitstext(\bff)_R,\uu\rangle \in R,\qquad
    \uu\defeq \psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv \in R^{\codedim},
    $$
    \else
       \begin{align*}
   & \langle \psi(\bff), \vv \rangle = \langle \bitstext(\bff)_R,\uu\rangle \in R \\ & 
    \uu\defeq \psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv \in R^{\codedim},
    \end{align*}
    \fi
    %
    where $\mathsf{T}$ denotes transposition.  In particular, if $L=\mathsf{Id}_{\codedim_0 \times \codedim_0} \otimes (1,\ldots, 2^{B-1})_S\otimes (\gamma_1, \ldots, \gamma_d) \in S^{\codedim_0 \times (\codedim_0\cdot B\cdot \moduledegree)} = S^{\codedim_0 \times \codedim}$ (cf.\ \cref{d:bitification}), then 
    \begin{equation}\label{e:u_def}
    \uu =   \left(\vv \otimes \left(1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1}\right)_R \otimes \left(\modulemorphism(\gamma_1), \ldots, \modulemorphism(\gamma_{\moduledegree})\right)\right) \in R^{\codedim}
    \end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Rewriting $\langle \psi(\bff), \vv \rangle$, we obtain  by \cref{d:bitification,e:matrix_transpose_inner_product}
$$
\langle \psi(\bff), \vv\rangle = \langle \psi(L\cdot \bitstext(\bff)_S), \vv\rangle =  \langle \psi(\bitstext(\bff)_S), \psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}} \cdot \vv\rangle 
$$
%
Now $\psi(\bitstext(\bff)_S) = \bitstext(\bff)_R$ because every entry of $\bitstext(\bff)$ consists of bits and $\psi$ is a ring homomorphism, so it sends the $0$ and $1$ elements of $S$ to the $0$ and $1$ elements of $R$. Further, $$\psi(L) = \mathsf{Id}_{\codedim_0\times \codedim_0} \otimes \psi( (1, \ldots, 2^{B-1})_S) \otimes (\psi(\gamma_1),\ldots, \psi(\gamma_\moduledegree)),  $$ 
and $\psi \circ \pi_{\Char(S)}(2^i) = \pi_{\Char(R)}(2^i)$ because $\psi \circ \pi_{\Char(S)}$ is a ring homomorphism,  $\psi\circ \pi_{\Char(S)}(2^i) = 2^i \cdot (\psi\circ \pi_{\Char(S)}(1))$, hence $\psi\circ \pi_{\Char(S)}(2^i)$ is $2^i$ times the identity element of $R$, and that is precisely the definition of $\pi_{\Char(R)}(2^i)$. 

The specific form of $\uu$ in \eqref{e:u_def} is now obtained by direct calculation of $\psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}} \cdot \vv$. 
%
\begin{comment}
\begin{align}
    \langle \modulemorphism(\bff),\vv\rangle &= \langle \modulemorphism\left(\sum_{\substack{k\in [B],\\ j\in [\moduledegree]}} \bff_{*jk} \cdot 2^{k-1} \cdot \gamma_j\right), \vv \rangle\label{e:iop_linear_claim_1}\\ &= \langle \sum_{\substack{k\in [B],\\ j\in [\moduledegree]}} \ringmorphism(\bff_{*jk}) \cdot \ringmorphism(2^{k-1}) \cdot \modulemorphism(\gamma_j),\vv \rangle,\label{e:iop_linear_claim_2}\\
    &= \sum_{\substack{k\in [B],\\ j\in [\moduledegree]}} \ringmorphism(2^{k-1}) \cdot \modulemorphism(\gamma_j)\cdot \langle \ringmorphism(\bff_{*jk}), \vv \rangle \notag\\ &=   \sum_{\substack{k\in [B],\\ j\in [\moduledegree]}} \sum_{i\in [\codedim_0]} \ringmorphism(2^{k-1}) \cdot \modulemorphism(\gamma_j)\cdot  \langle \ringmorphism(\bff_{ijk}), \vv_i \rangle= \langle \ringmorphism(\bitstext(\bff)), \uu \rangle \notag,
\end{align}
as needed.
\end{comment}
\end{proof}

We assume that the input vector $\vv$ for $\LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound, \psi, R,\cC,L)$, the matrix $L$, and the tensor decomposition dimensions are such that $\uu= \psi(L)^\mathsf{T}\cdot \vv$ admits a tensor decomposition $\uu=\uu^{(1)}\otimes \uu^{(2)}$ with $\uu^{(1)}\in R^{\codedim_1}$, $\uu^{(2)}\in R^{\codedim_2}$, see \cref{r:tensor_dec_u} for an explanation that this can be achieved easily in  applications, and what to do otherwise. 

\begin{construction}[IOP for $\LinModFieldBin$]\label{c:LinModFieldBin}

 \textbf{Parameters.} $(\codedim_0,\lncsbreak \codedim,\lncsbreak \codedim_1,\lncsbreak\codedim_2,\lncsbreak \Pi_\iopp,\lncsbreak \Pi_\red,\lncsbreak \cP,\lncsbreak (\cM_q)_{q\in \cP})$ \iflncs as above.\else comprised of: a witness vector length $\codedim_0$; a \emph{bitified witness} vector length $\codedim$; tensor decomposition dimensions $\codedim_1, \codedim_2$ for \cref{c:core_iop} satisfying $\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2 = \codedim$; IORs and IOPs $\Pi_\iopp$ and $\Pi_\red$ as in \cref{c:core_iop}; and sets of primes and homomorphisms $ \cP, (\cM_q)_{q\in \cP}$ again as in \cref{c:core_iop}.\\\fi


\noindent \textbf{Inputs.} The prover and verifier receive $\inp=(\mu, \vv)\in \moduletwo\times R^{\codedim_0}$ and  $\oracle= f \in \comfield^\codelength$. The prover receives  $\wit= \bff\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}$ such that $(\inp,\oracle, \wit) \in \LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\lncsbreak\modulemorphism,\lncsbreak \moduletwo,\lncsbreak \cC,\lncsbreak L)$, i.e., 
%
$$
f = \Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)),\qquad \langle \psi(\bff), \vv \rangle = \mu \quad \text{in } R,
$$
for some $\bitstext(\bff)$ such that $L\cdot \bitstext(\bff)_S =\bff$.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
     \item By \cref{l:bitify}, the   claim $(\inp,\oracle, \wit) \in \LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism, R,  \cC, L)$ is equivalent to the claim \begin{equation}\label{e:iop_final_linear_claim}
\iflncs
     \begin{aligned}\left(\inp'=(\mu, \uu),\ \oracle' = f=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)),\ \wit' = \bitstext(\bff)\right)\\ \in \LinBitsDual(\bits,\pi, R,\cC),\end{aligned}
\else
     \left(\inp'=(\mu, \uu), \oracle' = f=\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff)), \wit' = \bitstext(\bff)\right) \in \LinBitsDual(\bits,\pi, R,\cC),
\fi
\end{equation}
where $\uu = \psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv$. 

By assumption, $\uu=\uu^{(1)}\otimes \uu^{(2)}$ with $\uu^{(1)}\in R^{\codedim_1}$, $\uu^{(2)}\in R^{\codedim_2}$. Then the prover and the verifier use \cref{c:core_iop} to prove \eqref{e:iop_final_linear_claim}. They use parameters $(\codedim,\codedim_1,\codedim_2,\Pi_\iopp, \Pi_\red, \cP, (\cM_q)_{q\in \cP})$.  See \cref{r:tensor_dec_u} for comments on how to obtain tensor decompositions of $\uu$ in \cref{c:core_iop} assuming $\vv$ is appropriately tensor-decomposable.
 %  \left(\vv \otimes \left(1, \ringmorphism(2), \ldots, \ringmorphism(2^{B-1})\right) \otimes \left(\modulemorphism(\gamma_1), \ldots, \modulemorphism(\gamma_{\moduledegree})\right)\right)$. 
 \end{enumerate}
\end{construction}


\begin{remark}[Tensor decompositions of $\uu$]\label{r:tensor_dec_u}
Recall that \cref{c:core_iop} considers a (possibly trivial) tensor decomposition of its public input vector $\uu$ (denoted $\vv$ in \cref{c:core_iop}). Moreover, as argued in \cref{s:to_low_entropy_gkr,s:gkr_low_entropy}, this tensor decomposition is crucial for performance of \ftwoz\ (concretely, we seek $\codedim^{0.5}\leq \codedim_1 \leq \codedim^{0.6}$, cf.\ \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}).   

In many applications, the input vector $\vv$ for $\LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism, \moduletwo,  \cC, L)$ is an ``eq'' vector, i.e.\ a vector of the form $(\eq(\bb;\rr))_{\bb\in\bits^{\log\codedim_0}}$ for some $\rr\in \moduletwo^{\log \codedim_0}$. In this case  $\vv=\vv^{(1)}\otimes \vv^{(2)}$ with the dimensions of $\vv^{(1)}$, $\vv^{(2)}$ being arbitrary powers of two as long as $|\vv^{(1)}|\cdot |\vv^{(2)}|=\codedim_0$. In that case, if \begin{equation}\label{e:canonical_bitification_matrix}L=\mathsf{Id}_{\codedim_0\times \codedim_0} \otimes (1,\ldots, 2^{B-1})_S\otimes (\gamma_1,\ldots, \gamma_\moduledegree)\end{equation} (cf.\ \cref{d:bitification}), then $\uu$ has the form \eqref{e:u_def}, i.e.\ $$ \uu =   \left(\vv \otimes \left(1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1}\right)_R \otimes \left(\modulemorphism(\gamma_1), \ldots, \modulemorphism(\gamma_{\moduledegree})\right)\right) $$ and we may use a tensor decomposition for $\uu$ in \cref{c:core_iop} of the form, say, $\uu=\uu^{(1)}\otimes \uu^{(2)}$, where
%
$$
\uu^{(1)} \defeq \vv^{(1)},\quad \uu^{(2)}\defeq \vv^{(2)}\otimes \left(1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1}\right)_R \otimes \left(\modulemorphism(\gamma_1), \ldots, \modulemorphism(\gamma_{\moduledegree})\right).
$$
Other decompositions apply. For example one of the tensor factors in $\uu^{(2)}$ above could be placed in $\uu^{(1)}$, and the vector  $\left(1, 2, \ldots, 2^{B-1}\right)_R$ can admit further tensor decomposition, and some of the factors can be placed in $\uu^{(1)}$.

%
For more general matrices $L$, one needs to make sure that $L$ has enough structure to allow for a favorable tensor decomposition of $\uu$. If that is not possible, one  can either: 
\begin{itemize}
[leftmargin=*,nosep]
\item Fall back to using the matrix \eqref{e:canonical_bitification_matrix}, which is always available for arbitrary finitely generated rings $S$ with bit-bound $B$, or
\item Alternatively, if the ring $R$ supports it (e.g.\ if $R$ is a finite field or a ring with a large exceptional set), one can run a sumcheck to reduce the claim $\langle \bitstext(\bff)_R, \uu\rangle = \mu$ to an MLE evaluation claim over $R$, thereby ``replacing'' the vector $\uu$ by an ``eq vector'' that, as such, admits tensor decompositions of arbitrary dimensions.
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\end{lncsmove}
\paragraph{Costs of \cref{c:LinModFieldBin}.} We discuss the costs of \Cref{c:LinModFieldBin}. The rounds, oracles and queries of \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} are exactly those of \cref{c:core_iop} (see \cref{t:core_iop_complexity}), which is run on the bitified witness $\bitstext(\bff)$ of length $\codedim=\codedim_0\cdot \moduledegree\cdot B$ (if using the standard bitification matrix of \cref{d:bitification}), and with the public input vector $\uu=\uu^{(1)}\otimes \uu^{(2)}$ of \cref{r:tensor_dec_u}.   The only additional costs of \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} are those of computing $\bitstext(\bff)$ and of computing $\uu^{(1)}$ and $\uu^{(2)}$. When using the standard bitification matrix, the verifier does not need to compute $\uu$ entirely: it suffices to compute $\uu^{(1)}, \uu^{(2)}$, avoiding a linear cost.  In \Cref{t:LinModFieldBin_complexity} we summarize the costs of \cref{c:LinModFieldBin}. 


%For a general bitification matrix $L$, computing $\uu=\psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv$ costs $O(\mathsf{nnz}(L))$ operations in $R$, where $\mathsf{nnz}(L)$ denotes the number of nonzero entries of $L$.

\begin{table}[H]
  \centering
  \begin{tabular}{l|l|l|l}
    \toprule
     & Prover & Verifier & Proof (bits) \\
    \hline
    Bitification of $\bff$ (\cref{d:bitification}) & $O(\codedim)$ bit op. & -- & -- \\
    \hline
    Instance translation $(\mu,\vv)\mapsto (\mu, \uu^{(1)}, \uu^{(2)})$ & $O(\codedim_2)\cdot R$ & same as prover & -- \\
    \hline
    \Cref{c:core_iop} with length $\codedim=\codedim_0\cdot \moduledegree\cdot B$ & \cref{t:core_iop_complexity} & \cref{t:core_iop_complexity} & \cref{t:core_iop_complexity} \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{Costs of \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} for the standard bitification matrix and the tensor decomposition of \cref{r:tensor_dec_u}. By $N\cdot R$ we indicate $N$ operations in $R$.}
  \label{t:LinModFieldBin_complexity}
\end{table}

\begin{lncsmove}{IopE}
\begin{theorem}\label{t:thm_LinModFieldBits}
    Under the same hypotheses and notation as in \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP} for the parameters $(\codedim,\codedim_1,\codedim_2, \Pi_\iopp,\Pi_\red, \cP, (\cM_q)_{q\in \cP})$, the  ring $R$, the code $\cC$, and the binary field $\comfield$ with packing map $\pack$, \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} is a perfectly complete $(1+t_\red + t_\iopp)$-round IOP for $\LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism, \moduletwo, \cC,L)$ with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\LinModFieldBin^{\delta},\bot)$ and errors \lncsmath{\kappa_0, (\ksred_i)_{i\in [t_\red]}, (\ksiopp_i)_{i\in [t_\iopp]},} where $
    \kappa_0 = \theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim) %\frac{\degevalfield}{q_{\min}} + \frac{\log(\codedim_1 +1) + \log(q-1)}{|\cP|\cdot \lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor}.
    $ is as in \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}. % (as in \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}, including its explicit expression when the sets $\cM_q$ are as in \cref{l:examples_morphisms}; here $\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$ is computed with respect to the tensor decomposition $\uu=\uu^{(1)}\otimes \uu^{(2)}$ of \cref{r:tensor_dec_u} used in \cref{c:core_iop}, i.e.\ the quantities $D$, $T$, $N_R$ are determined by the lifts of the entries of $\uu^{(1)}$ and $\uu^{(2)}$).
    
    The error term $\kappa_0$ and its round are absent if Round 1 of \cref{c:core_iop} is not executed. 
    
    If Round 0 of \cref{c:core_iop} (OOD sampling) is executed, then \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} has an additional round and the additional OOD error term $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}$ of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP_OOD}.
    %If $\degevalfield=0$, i.e.\ $\evalfield$ is a prime field, then \cref{c:core_iop} is a $t_\red+t_\iopp$-round IOP with round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\LinBitsDual^\delta, \bot)$, with errors $\kappa_0, (\ksred_i)_{i\in [t_\red]}, (\ksiopp_i)_{i\in [t_\iopp]}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof} Deferred to \cref{a:LinModFieldBin}. In short, the result is a consequence of \cref{l:bitify}, which establishes a correspondence between  triples for $\LinModFieldBin\lncsbreak(\moduleonebound,\lncsbreak\modulemorphism,\lncsbreak \moduletwo,\lncsbreak \cC,\lncsbreak{}L)$ and $\LinBitsDual\lncsbreak(\bits,\lncsbreak\pi,\lncsbreak \moduletwo,\lncsbreak\cC)$. \cref{l:bitify} further provides a method for efficiently computing a corresponding triple  from the other. Using the assumption that $L\cdot \bfg \in (S_{\leq B}^{<D})^{\codedim_0}$ for all $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}$, this allows  to build a round-by-round knowledge soundness state and an extractor for \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} from those of \cref{c:core_iop}.
\end{proof}
\end{lncsmove}



\end{lncsappendix}
\begin{lncsappendix}[End-to-end SNARKs\label{a:end_to_end_snarks}]{snark}
\iflncs\subsection{An end-to-end \ftwoz-based SNARK over  any finitely generated ring}\label{s:to_snark}\else\section{An end-to-end \ftwoz-based SNARK over  any finitely generated ring}\label{s:to_snark}\fi




We present an example SNARK for constraints over finitely generated rings, using \ftwoz\ as PCS.  In our exposition we make several simplifying assumptions, for the sake of clarity. We discuss the general case in \cref{s:to_general_bitz}. 

We emphasize that the main novelty is the PCS (Step 4 below). The techniques surrounding the PCS, namely the PIOP, can be traced back to Zaratan and Zinc+ \cite{zincplus,zaratan}, though we allow for arbitrary finitely generated rings $S$ (which neither work does) by modifying Zinc+'s PIOP slightly.  Because of this, we adopt an explanatory and intuitive tone in our presentation, rather than delving into full formality.

    
Let  $S$ be a finitely generated ring, for example $S$ may be the integers $\ZZ$, a prime field, a lattice ring, a finite field extension, etc. For simplicity, for now let $S$ be either $\ZZ$ or a finite field $\FF$ (possibly an extension field).  If $S=\ZZ$, then we assume a public parameter $B$ specifying the maximum bit-size of a witness element. We let $S_{\leq B}$ denote either the finite field $\FF$ or the integer range $\range{0}{2^B-1}$.

In a proof system over a ring $S$, given a public vector $\inp=\xx\in S^\inplen$, the prover wants to prove it knows $\wit=\bff\in S_{\leq B}^{\codedim_0}$ such that \begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_0}\pp_i(\xx,\bff)=0 \qquad \text{in  } S \qquad \text{ for all } i\in [m], \end{equation} where $\pp=(\pp_i)_{i\in [m]}$ is a public collection of polynomials with coefficients in $S$.  For example, in an \emph{R1CS constraint}, \lncsmath{\pp_i(\xx,\bff) = (A\cdot (\xx,\bff))_i \cdot (B\cdot (\xx,\bff))_i  - (C\cdot (\xx,\bff))_i}
where $A,B,C$ are $m\times (\inplen + \codedim_0)$ matrices over $S$. 

%\bigskip




     \paragraph{Step 1: Commitment.} The prover commits to $\bff$ with \ftwoz, i.e.\ it sends a commitment to $\Enc_\cC\circ\pack\circ\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$ over $\comfield$, as in \iflncs\cref{d:general_linbitsrings}\else Step 1 of \cref{s:to_general_rings} (or of \cref{c:LinModFieldBin})\fi. 

     \paragraph{Step 2: Projection to a   finite field $\FF$.}  If $S=\ZZ$, we perform the fingerprinting step of \cite{zaratan}. Concretely, the verifier samples a random prime $q$  of $\approx \lambda$ bit size. Then the claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_0} is replaced by
\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_05}\pi_q(\pp_i)(\pi_q(\xx),\pi_q(\bff))=0 \qquad \text{in  } \FF_q \qquad \text{ for all } i,\end{equation} 
where $\pi_q:\ZZ\to \FF$ denotes reduction modulo $q$, and $\pi_q(\pp_i)$ is $\pp_i$ with coefficients reduced modulo $q$. 

If $S=\FF$ is a finite field, this step is skipped (unless we want to use the approach from \cref{r:to_reducing_ring}). 

Going forward,  we abuse the notation and use the same symbol $\FF$ to denote either the field $\FF_q$ above or $S$ if $S$ is a finite field. We also let $\pi$ denote either reduction modulo $q$, or the identity homomorphism, respectively. 

% In \cref{s:to_general_bitz} we discuss other rings and other projections.

%This step can be skipped if one already has an efficient PIOP for polynomial constraints over $R$; for example, if $R$ is a finite field of reasonable size. 

\renewcommand{\lift}{\mathsf{lift}}

\begin{remark}[Reducing the size of $S$, if necessary]\label{r:to_reducing_ring} If $S$ is a prime field of very large size, one may prefer to lift the initial polynomial constraints to $\ZZ$  first (between Step 1 and Step 2), and then project onto a random prime field $\FF$ of bit-size $\approx \lambda$ during the present Step 2. This can compress the bit-size of the witness in the next steps by a very large factor. 
    
    To \emph{lift the constraints to $\ZZ$}, the prover publishes the integer values $a_i\defeq \lift(\pp_i)(\lift(\xx),\lncsbreak\lift(\bff))$  for all $i$, where $\lift$ is the natural lift from a prime field to integers. Then the initial constraints over $S$ are replaced by $\lift(\pp_i)(\lift(\xx),\lncsbreak\lift(\bff))-a_i = 0$ in $\ZZ$.

If $S$ is a proper finite field extension (or any finitely generated ring), one can also lift constraints. This time, one follows an approach similar to \iflncs Round 1 of \cref{c:core_iop}\else Step 3 of \cref{s:to_general_rings}\fi.

A scenario where this can be desirable is when proving lattice-type computations, where rings are sometimes very large \cite{ckks}.
\end{remark}

     \paragraph{Step 3: PIOP over $\FF$.} As in \cite{zaratan,zincplus,limber}, a PIOP is run over $\FF$, reducing \eqref{e:intro_eq_05} to a linear claim over $\FF$ or, if $\FF$ is too small, to a field extension  $\FF_{\mathsf{ext}(e)}$ of $\FF$.  The claim is  of the form
\begin{equation}\label{e:intro_eq_1}
\langle \vv, \pi(\bff)\rangle =\mu \qquad \text{in } \FF_{\mathsf{ext}(e)},
\end{equation}
for some $\mu\in\FF_{\mathsf{ext}(e)}$, $\vv\in (\FF_{\mathsf{ext}(e)})^{\codedim_0}$, where it is understood that $\FF_{\mathsf{ext}(e)}=\FF$ if the PIOP did not need to use a field extension. 

In general, if $\FF$ is a prime field of $\approx \lambda$ bits (e.g.\ if we started with $S=\ZZ$), then $\FF_{\mathsf{ext}}=\FF$. The field $\FF_{\mathsf{ext}}$ may be a proper extension if, for example, we started with $S$ being a small finite field,  and the PIOP had to use challenges in an extension of $\FF$ to guarantee soundness.
 
We  assume $\vv$ admits a tensor decomposition $\vv=\vv^{(1)} \otimes \vv^{(2)} \in \FF^{n_1 \cdot n_2}$. This is typically the case since $\vv$ is usually an ``eq'' vector, i.e.\ $\vv=(\eq(\bb,\rr))_{\bb\in \bits^{\log \codedim_0}}$ for some $\rr\in \FFext^{\log\codedim_0}$. As explained throughout the paper, this is leveraged crucially by the \ftwoz\ PCS.


\paragraph{Step 4: Opening with the \ftwoz\ PCS.} The claim \eqref{e:intro_eq_1}, together with the commitment to $\Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$, is an instance of the relation $\LinModFieldBin(S,\FF,\pi)$ from \iflncs\cref{d:general_linbitsrings}\else\cref{s:to_general_rings}\fi{} (or of $\LinModFieldBin(\FF_q)$ from \iflncs\cref{s:linmodfieldbin}\else\cref{s:to_simple_bitz}\fi{} if $S=\FF=\FF_q$), with the commitment of Step 1 as oracle. Accordingly, prover and verifier prove it with the \ftwoz\ PCS.


\bigskip


\begin{remark}[Handling of arbitrary finitely generated rings]\label{r:arb_fg_ring}\label{s:to_general_bitz}
We briefly outline how to handle the case when $S$ is an arbitrary finitely generated ring. The idea is the same as used in \ftwoz\ (\iflncs\else Step 3 of \cref{s:to_general_rings}, or \fi{}Round 1 of \cref{s:core_iop}). In these steps, we lift a linear constraint over an arbitrary finitely generated ring, to the free ring $F=\ZZ[X_1, \ldots, X_k]$, and then sample a random morphism from $F$ to a finite field, projecting the lifted constraints onto $\FF$. The same method is valid if instead of starting with linear constraints, one starts with arbitrary polynomial constraints. One can use \cref{l:reduction_lemma} to derive the soundness of this step. 
\end{remark}






\end{lncsappendix}
\begin{lncsappendix}{gkr}
\section{A GKR protocol for low entropy batched grand products }\label{s:gkr_low_entropy}


We now describe our instantiation of the GKR protocol for proving batched grand products, and our prover optimizations when these have ``low entropy''.



The bulk of the work in \ftwoz \ (\cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin}) is to reduce the claim over the binary field $\comfield$
\begin{equation}\label{e:main_grand_product}
\prod_{i \in [\codedim_1]} \left(\left(g^{\bm{\gamma}_i}-1\right)\cdot \pi_2(\bff_{ij}) +1\right)= g^{\mu_j}  \quad  \in \comfield, \qquad \text{for all } j\in [\codedim_2]
\end{equation}
to an inner product claim  of the form $\langle \vv, \pi_2(\bff)\rangle =\mu$ over $\comfield$, for some $\vv\in \comfield^{\codedim}$, where here $\bff\in \bits^\codedim \subseteq \ZZ^\codedim$.  Recall that $$\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2.$$ To fix intuition,  in our instantiation (cf.\ \cref{s:instantiation}) we set $\codedim_1 \approx \codedim^{0.6}, \codedim_2=\codedim^{0.4}$. %For the purposes of this reduction, it is irrelevant that $\bff$ is defined over the integers: we will only consider the vector $\pi_2(\bff)$.


\renewcommand{\comfield}{\KK}


Cryptographically speaking, our protocol is simply a standard batched version of the GKR protocol applied to grand products from \cite{thaler13}. However, our implementation uses lookup tables (i.e.\ preprocessed data) to optimize the prover time of such protocol. In particular, our techniques leverage the fact that the factors in \eqref{e:main_grand_product} have \emph{low entropy}, in the sense that for every $i\in [\codedim_1]$ and $j\in [\codedim_2]$, the $i$-th factor in the $j$-th grand product of \eqref{e:main_grand_product} can take only two values, depending on whether $\bff_{ij}=1$ or $\bff_{ij}=0$.  



 The optimizations we introduce apply to any (batched) grand product with low entropy factors, and not only to the grand product in \eqref{e:main_grand_product}. They also work on any field. Because of this, we  describe a general protocol IOR  that, given a finite field $\KK$;  a bivariate polynomial $\gkrpol\in \KK[Y,X]$; %a code $\cC$ and map $T$ as in \cref{?}\footnote{The code $\cC$ and map $T$ are  not used throughout the entirety of the IOR.}; 
  and dimensions $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$, reduces triples from 
%
\iflncs\begingroup\footnotesize\fi
\begin{align*}
&\PESAT(\comfield, \gkrpol)\\ &\iflncs\else\qquad\fi{} = \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\yy,\bm{\mu})\in \comfield^{\codedim_1}\times \comfield^{\codedim_2}, \\
&\oracle=\bot,\\
%&\oracle = f\in \KK^{\codelength},\\ 
&\wit=\bfg= (\bfg_{*j})_{j\in [\codedim_2]}\in \KK^{\codedim_1 \times \codedim_2}\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned}   % &f =\Enc_{\cC}\circ T(\bfg)\\ 
&\prod_{i\in[\codedim_1]} \gkrpol(\yy_i, \bfg_{ij}) = \bm{\mu}_j \ \text{ for all } j\in [\codedim_2]\end{aligned}  \right\},
\end{align*}
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
%
to triples in 
%
$$
\LIN( \comfield)  = \left\{ \left(\begin{aligned} &\inp=(\uu,\mu)\in \comfield^\codedim \times \comfield, \\
&\oracle=\bot,\\
%&\oracle = f\in \comfield^{\codelength}, \\ 
&\wit= \bfg\in \KK^{\codedim_1 \times \codedim_2}\end{aligned}\right) \;\middle|\;  \begin{aligned} 
%&f=\Enc_{\cC}\circ T (\bfg) \\   
&\langle \uu, \bfg\rangle = \mu  \end{aligned}\right\}.
$$
%
One can additionally add an encoding $f=\Enc_\cC(\bfg)$ as an oracle  in the above relations (matching the context in which we use the IOR), but our IOR does not make any use of it whatsoever, hence we omit it. 

In our instantiation, $\yy_i=g^{\bm{\gamma}_i}$ and $\bm{\mu}_j=g^{\mu_j}$, where $\bm{\gamma}_i$ and $\mu_j$ are the integers in \eqref{e:main_grand_product}.

We  describe $\Pi_{\red}$ now. We will describe our implementation-level optimizations afterwards. We assume $\codedim_1,\codedim_2$ are powers of two:
%
$$
\codedim_1 = 2^{r_1}, \quad \codedim_2 = 2^{r_2}.
$$
We index $[\codedim_1],[\codedim_2]$ via elements of the hypercubes $\bits^{r_1}, \bits^{r_2}$, respectively.
%
\begin{construction}[An IOR $\Pi_\red$ from $\PESAT(\comfield, \gkrpol)$ to $\LIN( \comfield)$]\label{c:gkr_grand_product}
\noindent \textbf{Input.}   The prover and verifier receive $(\yy,\bm{\mu})\in \comfield^{\codedim_1} \times  \comfield^{\codedim_2}$. The prover receives also $\bfg\in \KK^{\codedim_1\times \codedim_2}$.


Let $L_{r_1}$ be a  \emph{multilinear} polynomial over $\comfield$ on $r_1 + r_2$ variables such that $$L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)= \gkrpol(\yy_{\bb}, \bfg_{\bb,\cc}) \quad  \text{ for all } (\bb,\cc) \in \bits^{r_1} \times \bits^{r_2}.$$ (Note that $\gkrpol$ may not be multilinear.)  
     For $i=0,\ldots, r_1-1$, let $L_i$ be a multilinear polynomial on $r_2 + i$ variables  such that 
      $$
  L_{i}(\bb,\cc)  =  L_{i+1}(\bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{i+1}(\bb,1, \cc)  \quad \text{for all } (\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{i} \times \bits^{r_2}.
    $$
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
    \item The verifier sends $\bm{\alpha}^{(0)}\in \comfield^{r_2}$. Both prover and verifier compute $C^{(0)}\defeq 
    L_0(\bm{\alpha}^{(0)})$ (note that $L_0$ is the multilinear extension of $\bm{\mu}$ over $\comfield$). 
    \item  For $i=0,\ldots, r_1-1$:
    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
        \item \textbf{$i$-th sumcheck.} The prover and verifier run the sumcheck protocol for the following claim: %\john{FYI: The current implementation constructs two sumchecks. One for b variables. A subsequent one for the c variables. It might be good to explore the difference between the two}
    %
    $$
    C^{(i)} =  \sum_{(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^i \times \bits^{r_2}}  L_{i+1}(\bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{i+1}(\bb,1,\cc) \cdot \eq(\bb, \cc; \bm{\alpha}^{(i)}). 
    $$
    %
    At the end of the sumcheck protocol, the prover and verifier are left with a claim of the form
    %
    \begin{equation}\label{e:gkr_intermediate}
    L_{i+1}(\rr_\bb^{(i)},0,\rr_{\cc}^{(i)}) = a_{0}^{(i)}, \qquad L_{i+1}(\rr_\bb^{(i)},1,\rr_{\cc}^{(i)}) = a_{1}^{(i)}
    \end{equation}
    %
    for some $\rr_\bb^{(i)}\in \comfield^{i}$, $\rr_\cc^{(i)}\in \comfield^{r_2}$ and $a_{0}^{(i)},a_{1}^{(i)}\in \comfield$.
    %
    \item \textbf{$i$-th unification step.} The prover and verifier unify the two claims from \eqref{e:gkr_intermediate} into a single one via the following standard procedure:
    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
        \item The verifier sends a random element $\beta^{(i)}\in\KK$.
        \item The claim \eqref{e:gkr_intermediate} is replaced by
        %
        $$
        L_{i+1}(\rr_\bb^{(i)},\beta^{(i)},\rr_{\cc}^{(i)}) = C^{(i+1)} \defeq a_0^{(i)} \cdot (1-\beta^{(i)}) + a_1^{(i)} \cdot \beta^{(i)}.
        $$
        Let 
        $$
        \bm{\alpha}^{(i+1)} \defeq (\rr_\bb^{(i)},\beta^{(i)},\rr_{\cc}^{(i)}).
        $$
    \end{enumerate}
    %
    \end{enumerate}
    \item At this stage, the prover and verifier are left with the claim
    %
    $$
    L_{r_1}(\bm{\alpha}^{(r_1)}) = C^{(r_1)}.
    $$
    This claim is equivalent to
    %
    \begin{equation}\label{e:gkr_leaf_claim}
    \sum_{(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1} \times \bits^{r_2}} \gkrpol(\yy_{\bb},\bfg_{\bb,\cc}) \cdot \eq((\bb,\cc), \bm{\alpha}^{(r_1)}) = C^{(r_1)}.
    \end{equation}
    If $\gkrpol(Y,X)$ is linear in $X$, then \eqref{e:gkr_leaf_claim} is a linear claim on $\bfg$. Namely, it is equivalent to
    %
    $$
    \langle \uu, \bfg \rangle = C^{(r_1)} + c
    $$
    for some $\uu\in \KK^{\codedim}, c\in \KK$. In this case, prover and verifier output 
    %
        $$
    (\inp'=(\uu, C^{(r_1)}+c),\ \bot, \ \wit' = \bfg) \in \LIN(\comfield).
    $$
    %
    Otherwise, i.e.\ if $\gkrpol$ is not linear in $X$, prover and verifier run a sumcheck for the claim \eqref{e:gkr_leaf_claim}, which results in MLE claims for $\yy$ and $\bfg$. The verifier checks the claim for $\yy$ itself, and prover and verifier output the MLE claim for $\bfg$.

    Below we discuss what $\gkrpol$, $\uu$ and $C^{(r_1)}+c$ are specifically in our usage of $\Pi_\red$ as a subprotocol of \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin}, and how to keep the verifier succinct.
    %
\end{enumerate}
    
\end{construction}

\paragraph{Using $\Pi_\red$ in \ftwoz \ and succinctness of the verifier. }

If $\gkrpol(\yy_\bb, \bfg_{\bb,\cc}) = (\yy_\bb-1)\cdot \bfg_{\bb,\cc} +1$ as in \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin}, then $$\uu=((\yy_\bb - 1)\cdot \eq(\bb;\bm\alpha^{(r_1,1)}))_{\bb\in \bits^{r_1}} \otimes (\eq(\cc;\bm\alpha^{(r_1,2)}))_{\cc\in \bits^{r_2}}, \quad C^{(r_1)} + c =  C^{(r_1)} -1,$$  where $\bm{\alpha}^{(r_1)} = (\bm{\alpha}^{(r_1,1)}, \bm{\alpha}^{(r_1,2)})$.
    In this case, to avoid having the verifier pay a linear cost, the verifier constructs $\uu^{(1)}\defeq ((\yy_\bb - 1)\cdot\eq(\bb;\bm\alpha^{(r_1,1)}))_{\bb\in \bits^{r_1}} $ and $\uu^{(2)}\defeq (\eq(\cc;\bm\alpha^{(r_1,2)}))_{\cc\in \bits^{r_2}}$ separately, and outputs these two vectors instead of $\uu$.
    
     When used as a subprotocol of \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin}, after executing the GKR protocol above (\cref{c:gkr_grand_product}), we use an IOP $\Pi_\iopp$ to prove the linear claim $\langle \uu, \bfg\rangle= C^{(r_1)}-1$, with $\bfg=\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$ and  the verifier having oracle access to $f=\Enc_\cC \circ \pack \circ \pi_2(\bff)$. As we mentioned (\cref{s:instantiation}), in our instantiation $\Pi_{\iopp}$ first runs a   sumcheck, reducing the claim $\langle \uu, \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))\rangle= C^{(r_1)}-1$  to an MLE evaluation claim  of the vector $\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$ and of $\uu$. For the latter, we have, letting $\bm{\theta}=(\bm{\theta}^{(1)}, \bm{\theta}^{(2)})\in \KK^{r_1+r_2}$ be the MLE evaluation point,   \begin{align*}\mle{\uu}(\bm{\theta})&=\mle{\uu^{(1)} \otimes \uu^{(2)}}(\bm{\theta}^{(1)}, \bm{\theta}^{(2)}) = \mle{\uu^{(1)}}(\bm{\theta}^{(1)}) \cdot \mle{\uu^{(2)}}(\bm{\theta}^{(2)}),\\  
     \mle{\uu^{(1)}}(\bm{\theta}^{(1)})&=     \sum_{\bb\in\bits^{r_1}} (\yy_\bb-1)\cdot\eq(\bb;\bm\alpha^{(r_1,1)})\cdot\eq(\bb;\bm{\theta}^{(1)}),\\
     \mle{\uu^{(2)}}(\bm{\theta}^{(2)})& =  \eq(\bm{\theta}^{(2)};\bm\alpha^{(r_1,2)}),
     \end{align*} and so the verifier can compute $\mle{\uu}(\bm{\theta})$ by itself in time $O(\codedim_1 +\codedim_2)$. For the MLE claim on $\pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$, we first use ring-switching \cite{binius2} to reduce the claim to a batch of linear claims for $\pack\circ \pi_2(\bitstext(\bff))$, and then prove these with, e.g.\  WHIR or Ligerito.   


\paragraph{Costs.} In \cref{t:gkr_grand_product_complexity} we summarize the number of rounds, the proof size and the verifier work of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} when used within \ftwoz \ as described above.  These are standard costs corresponding to a standard GKR construction. We discuss an optimized prover, and its costs, in the next subsection. Recall that in our instantiations $\codedim_1\approx \sqrt{\codedim}$ and $\codedim_2\approx\sqrt{\codedim}$, hence the verifier is succinct.

\begin{table}[H]
  \centering
  \small
  \begin{tabular}{l|l|l|l}
    \toprule
     & Rounds & Proof (elements of $\comfield$) & Verifier \\
    \hline
    Step 1 (batching) & $1$ & -- & $O(\codedim_2)\cdot \comfield$ \\
    \hline
    Step 2, $i$-th sumcheck & $i+r_2$ & $3\cdot (i+r_2)$ & $O(i+r_2)\cdot \comfield$ \\
    \hline
    Step 2, $i$-th  unification & $1$ & $2$ & $O(1)\cdot \comfield$ \\
    \hline
    Computing $\uu$ & --    & -- & $O(\codedim_1 + \codedim_2)\cdot \comfield$ (only $\uu^{(1)}, \uu^{(2)}$)\\
    \hline
    \textbf{Total} & $t_\red = 1 + r_1\cdot r_2 + \binom{r_1+1}{2}$ & $3\cdot t_\red - r_1 - 3$ & $O(\codedim_1 + \codedim_2 + \log^2 \codedim)\cdot \comfield$ \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{ Rounds, proof size and verifier work of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} for the polynomial $\gkrpol(Y,X)=(Y-1)\cdot X +1$ in our deployment of \ftwoz.  By $N\cdot \comfield$ we indicate $N$ operations in $\comfield$. The proof size counts $3$ elements per sumcheck round. Using  \cite{gruen} we send $2$ elements per sumcheck round in our implementation.}
  \label{t:gkr_grand_product_complexity}
\end{table}


The soundness errors of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} are as follows.

\begin{lemma}[Soundness and completeness of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}]\label{l:gkr_grand_product}
 Assume $\gkrpol(Y,X)$ is linear in $X$\footnote{We focus on this case since it is the one in our instantiation. A generalization to arbitrary polynomials $\gkrpol(Y,X)$ would  add rounds  and error terms corresponding to the sumcheck protocol mentioned at the end of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}.}. Then \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} is a perfectly complete
$(1 + r_1 r_2 + r_1(r_1+1)/2)$-round IOR from $\PESAT(\comfield,\gkrpol)$ to $\LIN(\comfield)$. In it, the verifier makes no oracle queries, and the prover outputs the same witness $\bfg$ that it received as input.
