Additionally, \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} extends in a natural way to an IOR from $\PESAT(\comfield,\gkrpol, \cC,\pack)$ to $\LIN(\comfield, \cC,\pack)$ (see \cref{s:prelims_codes} for these relations). For every $\delta$ within the unique decoding radius of $\cC$, this IOR has round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\PESAT(\comfield,\gkrpol,\cC,\pack)^\delta,\LIN(\comfield,\cC,\pack)^{\delta})$, with error $r_2/|\comfield|$ in the first round, $3/|\comfield|$ in every sumcheck round, and $1/|\comfield|$ in every unification step.

Beyond the unique decoding radius, the same errors hold for the relations extended by an out-of-domain evaluation claim as in \cref{a:OOD}.
%so given $\yy$ the verifier performs $O(\codedim_1+\codedim_2+r_1 r_2)$ field operations.
\end{lemma}

We omit the  proof of \cref{l:gkr_grand_product}, since \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} is a well-known variation of the GKR protocol. Round-by-round soundness of GKR is proved in \cite{CCHLRRW19}, and the knowledge version with relaxation follows as in \cite[proof of Lemma 6.6]{BCFW25}.

\subsection{Prover optimization via lookup tables}%  
We optimize \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} as follows, when $\bfg$ consists entirely of bits. Alternatively, the techniques discussed here would also apply if the entries of $\bfg$ belong to a small subset of $\KK$.

%\john{Typing is a bit inconsistent here for $c$ and $f_{b,c}$. Namely, we say c seems to be a boolean vector but $f_{b,c}$ is not } 
\paragraph{Setup.} We start with the following observation: for every $(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1} \times \bits^{r_2}$, we have $\bfg_{\bb,\cc}\in \bits$, and so 
%
$$L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)=\gkrpol(\yy_{\bb}, \bfg_{\bb,\cc})= 
\begin{cases}
    \yy_{\bb}^{(1)}\defeq \gkrpol(\yy_{\bb},1) & \text{if } \bfg_{\bb,\cc}=1\\
     \yy_{\bb}^{(0)}\defeq \gkrpol(\yy_{\bb},0) & \text{if } \bfg_{\bb,\cc}=0.
\end{cases}$$
%
The values $\yy_{\bb}^{(0)}, \yy_{\bb}^{(1)}$   do not depend on $\cc$. Hence the prover can set up a lookup table $T_{r_1}\in (\comfield\times \comfield)^{\bits^{r_1}}$ defined as
%
$$
T_{r_1}[\bb]= (\yy_{\bb}^{(0)}, \yy_{\bb}^{(1)}) \qquad \text{for all } \bb \in \bits^{r_1}
$$
at the beginning of the protocol, and, whenever required, use it to obtain the values $L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)$. Concretely
%
$$
L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)=T_{r_1}[\bb][\bfg_{\bb,\cc}] \quad \text{for all } (\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1}\times \bits^{r_2}.
$$
%Since $\yy$ is part of the input, this is essentially a no-op 
In our instantiation, $\yy_{\bb}^{(1)}=g^{\bm{\gamma}_{\bb}}$ and $\yy_\bb^{(0)}=1$ (cf.\ \eqref{e:main_grand_product}). Hence additionally the prover precomputes the elements $g^{\bm{\gamma}_{\bb}}$ at the beginning of the protocol, before computing $T_{r_1}$.



As we will explain later, the prover will also precompute similar tables for the values $L_{r_1-j}(\bb,\cc)$ of the levels close to the leaves (i.e.\ for small $j$),
\begin{equation}\label{e:size_T_r1_i}T_{r_1-j}\in \left(\comfield^{2^{2^j}}\right)^{\bits^{r_1-j}},\end{equation}
as well as other tables relevant for the different sumcheck rounds of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}, constructed from the tables $T_{r_1-j}$ themselves. The superscript $\bits^{r_1-j}$ in \eqref{e:size_T_r1_i} indicates that the table $T_{r_1-j}$ has one entry per element in the hypercube  $\bits^{r_1-j}$; and this entry is a vector of $2^{2^j}$ field elements. Concretely, for $\bb\in \bits^{r_1-j}$, $T_{r_1-j}[\bb]$ consists of all possible products of  $2^j$ consecutive factors of the grand product, namely the factors indexed by $(\bb, \bb')$ with $\bb$ fixed and $\bb'$ ranging over $\bits^{j}$.  Each factor can take two values, depending on whether $\bfg_{\bb,\bb', \cc}$ is $1$ or $0$. Hence $T_{r_1-j}[\bb]$ consists of $2^{2^j}$ elements. Hence the size of $T_{r_1-j}$ in \eqref{e:size_T_r1_i}.

Because $|T_{r_1-j}|$   grows doubly-exponentially in $j$, these tables are viable only for the bottom few levels of the tree ($j=0,1,2$ in our implementation). In higher levels we use standard sumchecks.



%Indeed, a node $i$ levels above the leaves is the product of the $2^i$ leaves below it, so its value is one of $2^{2^i}$ position-indexed constants, selected by $2^i$ entries of $\bfg_{*j}$ and independent of $j\in [\codedim_2]$. 

Importantly, all these tables have size independent of $\codedim_2$.  This (among others, cf.\ \cref{s:why_the_tensor_split}) is one of the reasons why we use the tensor decomposition structure, i.e.\ the split given by $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2$, in \cref{c:core_iop}.

\begin{remark}
    All precomputed data in this section depends on the input vector $\yy$ of $\PESAT(\comfield,\gkrpol)$, and possibly on further verifier challenges. Since, in a wider context where \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} is a subprocess of a larger proof system, $\yy$ typically depends on verifier's challenges, these precomputations must be done at \emph{runtime}. 
\end{remark}





\paragraph{First prover message in the last sumcheck in Step 2 of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}.}
We start by analyzing Step 2 with $i=r_1-1$. In this step prover and verifier run a sumcheck for the claim
%
$$
C^{(r_1-1)}= \sum_{(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-1}\times \bits^{r_2}} L_{r_1}(\bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{r_1}(\bb,1,\cc) \cdot \eq(\bb,\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i)}).
$$
%
This is the most expensive sumcheck of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product} for the prover (if done naively), since it is the sumcheck over the largest number of variables throughout \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}. 

For $1\leq j\leq r_1-1$, the $j$-th prover message in this sumcheck is, if $\bm{\rho}^{(j)}=(\rho_1, \ldots, \rho_{j-1})$ is the vector of verifier challenges so far in this sumcheck,
%
\iflncs\begingroup\scriptsize\fi
\begin{align*}
h_j(X)&\defeq \sum_{(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-1-j}\times \bits^{r_2}} L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,1,\cc)\cdot \eq(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i)})\\
\iflncs
&= \sum_{\cc\in \bits^{r_2}}\Big( \underbrace{\sum_{\bb\in \bits^{r_1-1-j}}L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,1,\cc) \cdot \eq(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, \bb;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,1)})}_{\defeq h_{j,\cc}(X)}\Big)\\
&\qquad \cdot   \eq(X,\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,2)}) \\
\else
&= \sum_{\cc\in \bits^{r_2}}\left( \underbrace{\sum_{\bb\in \bits^{r_1-1-j}}L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,0,\cc) \cdot L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,1,\cc) \cdot \eq(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, \bb;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,1)})}_{\defeq h_{j,\cc}(X)}\right)\cdot   \eq(X,\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,2)}) \\
\fi
&= \sum_{\cc\in \bits^{r_2}}h_{j,\cc}(X)\cdot   \eq(X,\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,2)}),
\end{align*}
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
where $\bm{\alpha}^{(i)} = (\bm{\alpha}^{(i,1)}, \bm{\alpha}^{(i,2)})\in \KK^{r_1-2}\times \KK^{r_2+1}$.
%
We claim that
%
\iflncs\begingroup\small\fi
\begin{equation}\label{e:grand_product_Lr1}
L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,0,\cc) = \left(L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, 1, \bb,0,\cc) -  L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, 0, \bb,0,\cc)\right)\cdot X + L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, 0, \bb,0,\cc).
\end{equation}
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
Indeed, $L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,0,\cc)$ is a linear polynomial, and the right-hand side in the above expression is the line passing through the two evaluations of  $L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,0,\cc)$ at $X=0$ and $X=1$. An analogous expression holds for $L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb, 1, \cc)$:
\iflncs\begingroup\small\fi
\begin{equation}\label{e:grand_product_Lr2}
L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, X, \bb,1,\cc) = \left(L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, 1, \bb,1,\cc) -  L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, 0, \bb,1,\cc)\right)\cdot X + L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, 0, \bb,1,\cc).
\end{equation}
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
%
Renaming
%
$$
E_{\eps_1\eps_2}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] \defeq L_{r_1}(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, \eps_1, \bb, \eps_2,\cc) \qquad \eps_1,\eps_2\in \bits, \qquad \omega[\bb]\defeq \eq(\bm{\rho}^{(j)}, \bb;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,1)}),
$$
we obtain
%
$$
h_{j,\cc}(X) =\sum_{\bb\in \bits^{r_1-1-j}} X^2 \cdot A_2^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] + X\cdot A_1^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] + A_0^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]
$$
 where,
\iflncs\begingroup\scriptsize\fi
$$
\begin{aligned}
A_0^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] &\defeq E_{00}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]\cdot E_{01}^{(j)} [\bb,\cc]\cdot \omega[\bb],\\ 
A_1^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] &\defeq  \left((E_{10}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]-E_{00}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc])\cdot E_{01}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] + (E_{11}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]-E_{01}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc])\cdot E_{00}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]\right)\cdot \omega[\bb]\\
A_2^{(j)}[\bb,\cc] &\defeq (E_{10}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]-E_{00}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc])\cdot (E_{11}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc]-E_{01}^{(j)}[\bb,\cc])\cdot \omega[\bb].
\end{aligned}
$$
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
%
Now, for $j=1$, we have $\bm{\rho}^{(j)}= \bot$, and hence each value $E_{\eps_1\eps_2}^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$ can be obtained, whenever needed, by querying $T_{r_1}[\bb]$ and selecting $T_{r_1}[\bb][1]$ or $T_{r_1}[\bb][0]$  depending on whether $\bfg_{\bb,\cc} = 1$ or $0$. A consequence  is that  the coefficients themselves $A_k^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$ ($k\in \{0,1,2\}$) can be obtained through lookup tables, as follows:
%
 we have $$A_0^{(1)}[\bb,\cc] = 
    T_{r_1}[0,\bb,0][\bfg_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}] \cdot    T_{r_1}[0,\bb, 1][\bfg_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}] \cdot \omega[\bb].
$$
Hence
%
$$
A_0^{(1)}[\bb,\cc] = \begin{cases}
    \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(1)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(1)} \cdot \omega[\bb]&\text{if } \bfg_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}=1,\ \bfg_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=1 \\  
    \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(1)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(0)}\cdot \omega[\bb]&\text{if } \bfg_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}=1,\ \bfg_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=0\\
     \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(0)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(1)}\cdot \omega[\bb]& \text{if } \bfg_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}=0,\ \bfg_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=1 \\
    \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(0)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(0)} \cdot  \omega[\bb] &\text{if } \bfg_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}= 0,\  \bfg_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=0
\end{cases}
$$
%
Thus the prover can precompute the table $$T_{A_0} \in (\comfield^{4})^{\bits^{r_1-2}}=\comfield^{\codedim_1}$$ given by $$T_{A_0}[\bb]=(\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(1)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(1)} \cdot \omega[\bb],  \ \
    \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(1)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(0)}\cdot \omega[\bb],   \ \ \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(0)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(1)}\cdot \omega[\bb],   \ \ \yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(0)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(0)} \cdot  \omega[\bb])$$
and use it to obtain any value $A_{0}^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$ for $(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-2}\times \bits^{r_2}$ via a single read into $T_{A_0}$. %\begin{equation}\label{e: A0_factor_T0}A_0[\bb,\cc] = T_{A_0}[\bb,\cc] \cdot \eq(\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,2)}).\end{equation} 
%
The size of $T_{A_0}$, again, does not depend on $\codedim_2=2^{r_2}$. Building $T_{A_0}$ requires at most $8\cdot 2^{r_1-2} = 2^{r_1+1}$ multiplications and no additions. 

We now explain how to obtain the values $A_1^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$ and $A_2^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$, again via the construction of a lookup table. Note that these depend on the \emph{four} bits $\bfg_{(\eps_1,\bb,\eps_2),\cc}$, $\eps_1,\eps_2\in \bits$, so our tables will need $2^4=16$ entries (as opposed to $4$ as in $T_{A_0}$) per  index $\bb$.
The prover  proceeds as follows:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
    \item It computes a table $T_{S_1}\in (\comfield^4)^{\bits^{r_1-2}}$ analogous to $T_{A_0}$, which allows to obtain the values 
    $$S_1^{(1)}[\bb,\cc] \defeq E_{10}^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]\cdot E_{11}^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]\cdot \omega[\bb],$$
    (observe that $T_{A_0}$ allows to obtain the values $E_{00}^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]\cdot E_{01}^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]\cdot \omega[\bb]$).
    \item It computes the table $T_{A_2}\in (\comfield^{16})^{\bits^{r_1-2}}$ given by
    \begin{equation}\label{e:TA2}T_{A_2}[\bb]=\Big( \big(\yy_{1,\bb,0}^{(x_1)}-\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(x_0)}\big)\cdot \big(\yy_{1,\bb,1}^{(x_1')}-\yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(x_0')}\big)\cdot \omega[\bb] \Big)_{(x_0,x_1,x_0',x_1')\in \bits^4},\end{equation}
    so that $A_2^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$ is the element  $$A_2^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]=T_{A_2}[\bb][\bfg_{(0,\bb,0),\cc},\bfg_{(1,\bb,0),\cc},\bfg_{(0,\bb,1),\cc},\bfg_{(1,\bb,1),\cc}].$$ Building $T_{A_2}$ takes at most $20$ multiplications and $8$ additions per index $\bb$\footnote{For the multiplications, there are $4$ multiplications to compute the $4$ elements $t_{x_0',x_1',\bb}\defeq \big(\yy_{1,\bb,1}^{(x_1')}-\yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(x_0')}\big)\cdot \omega[\bb]$, and then $16$ more multiplications to compute $\big(\yy_{1,\bb,0}^{(x_1)}-\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(x_0)}\big)\cdot t_{x_0',x_1',\bb}$.}. I.e.\ at most $5\cdot 2^{r_1}$ multiplications and $2^{r_1+1}$ additions in total.
    \item It obtains the values $A_1^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]$ via the identity \iflncs\begingroup\small\fi$$S_1^{(1)}[\bb,\cc] = A_0^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]+A_1^{(1)}[\bb,\cc]+A_2^{(1)}[\bb,\cc] \qquad \text{for all } (\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-2}\times \bits^{r_2}.$$\iflncs\endgroup\fi{} %so the prover accumulates the three sums $\sum_\bb A_0^{(1)}, \sum_\bb S_1^{(1)}, \sum_\bb A_2^{(1)}$ over the slots and recovers $\sum_\bb A_1^{(1)}$ with two additions per $\cc$.
\end{itemize}
 Once the tables have been computed,  obtaining $h_{1,\cc}(X)$ for one $\cc\in \bits^{r_2}$ costs $3\cdot 2^{r_1-2}$ table reads (one read per table $T_{A_0}, T_{S_1}, T_{A_2}$ per index $\bb\in \bits^{r_1-2}$) and $3\cdot 2^{r_1-2} +2$ additions: this is the cost of adding together the values read at each table, providing $\sum_\bb A_0[\bb,\cc]$,  $\sum_\bb S_1[\bb,\cc]$,  $\sum_\bb A_2[\bb,\cc]$, and then doing two additions to obtain $\sum_\bb A_1[\bb,\cc] = \sum_\bb S_1[\bb,\cc] -\sum_\bb A_0[\bb,\cc] - \sum_\bb A_2[\bb,\cc]$. In total, this amounts to at most  
 $3\codedim/4$ table reads and $\approx 3\codedim/4 +2\cdot \codedim_2$ additions to compute $h_{1,\cc}$ for all $\cc$, and no multiplications (in our instantiation, additions in $\comfield$ are $128$-bit XORs). 
 
 Finally, the prover computes $h_1(X)$ from the polynomials $h_{1,\cc}(X)$ with at most $5\cdot 2^{r_2}$ additions and $3\cdot 2^{r_2}$ multiplications (plus $2^{r_2}$ multiplications to precompute the values $\eq(\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,2)})$, shared by all rounds of this sumcheck, and $2^{r_1-2}$ multiplications for the weights $\omega[\bb]$).
 
\bigskip

In \cref{t:gkr_step1_costs} we record the total upper bound costs for computing $h_1(X)$. In our application we obtain lower numbers since $\yy_\bb^{(0)}=1$ and we work in characteristic two, and so in \cref{e:TA2} some elements collapse to $0$. 



 On the other hand, a standard generic approach to compute $h_{1}(X)$ would require $O(\codedim)=O(\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2)$ multiplications and additions, even if using the few  sumcheck optimizations that apply in our settings (to our knowledge) %\albert{which ones, exactly?}. 
 Crucially, we note that our sumcheck features summands of full field bit size $\log|\KK|$, which voids some known powerful optimizations when the summands are small, e.g.\ the univariate skip \cite{gruen} and some optimizations from \cite{sumcheck_opt_1,sumcheck_opt_2}. From \cite{sumcheck_opt_1,sumcheck_opt_2} we can use the square-root split of the $\eq$ factor, and, in a similar way as in \cite{sumcheck_opt_2}, we use  precomputed tables in the first sumcheck rounds, applied here to summands which are not small, but are selected by witness bits among few values. The prover message degree reduction through factoring the ``eq factor'' from \cite{gruen} does apply to our setting, and our implementation uses it: we send $2$ elements instead of the $3$ in each sumcheck round. 

 


  %On the other hand, a standard linear-time sumcheck prover \cite{thaler} computes $h_1(X)$ with $\Theta(\codedim)=\Theta(\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2)$ multiplications in $\KK$, several per pair of summands, for the products $L_{r_1}(\cdot,0,\cdot)\cdot L_{r_1}(\cdot,1,\cdot)$ and their $\eq$ weights. The known techniques for lowering this cost do not apply here, because the polynomials being multiplied take arbitrary values in $\KK$: the values $L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc)\in\{y^{(0)}_\bb, y^{(1)}_\bb\}$ are full $\log|\KK|$-bit field elements (the elements $g^{\bm{\gamma}_\bb}$ in our instantiation), and the entries of every higher level of the tree are products of them. The univariate skip of \cite{gruen} gains by running the first rounds over a small base field, of which there is none here, and the small-value algorithms of \cite{DT24,BDT25} require the multilinear polynomials to take small values, which they do not. (The one technique that does carry over is the degree reduction of \cite{gruen} for the public factor $\eq(X;\bm{\alpha}^{(i)})$, which we use to shorten the round messages.) What is small in our setting is not the summands but their \emph{selectors}: each summand is one of a few position-dependent constants, chosen by the witness bits at that position, and 
  %....the tables $T_{A_0}, T_{S_1}, T_{A_2}$ are a Four-Russians-style precomputation \cite{ADKF70} of every such choice. This replaces the $\Theta(\codedim)$ multiplications by $\Theta(\codedim)$ table reads and additions (XORs in our instantiation) plus $O(\codedim_1+\codedim_2)$ multiplications, cf.\ \cref{t:gkr_step1_costs}.

  %We remark that the same multiplication count for $h_1(X)$ can be obtained by grouping the summands according to the pattern of witness bits they depend on and accumulating the $\eq$ weights of each group before multiplying, which is the transpose of our tables. We present the table form because it is the one that extends to the later rounds and to the higher levels of the tree, as we show next: the tables absorb the verifier's challenges, and the bottom levels of the tree never have to be written down.


\begin{lncstable}{GkrRound1Costs}
\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{c|c|c}
         & Our approach & Standard/generic \\
         \hline
        Multiplications & $\leq   10\cdot \codedim_1 +  4\cdot \codedim_2 $ &$\Theta(\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2)$\\
        \hline
        Additions & $\leq 3\cdot \codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2/4 + 2\cdot\codedim_1 +  5\cdot \codedim_2$  & $\Theta(\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2)$\\
        \hline
        Table reads & $3\cdot \codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2/4$, into tables of $6\cdot\codedim_1$ field elements & --
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{The cost in field operations of the first sumcheck round of the last sumcheck in our GKR protocol for batched grand products, cf.\ \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}, where $\codedim=\codedim_1\cdot \codedim_2 = 2^{r_1}\cdot 2^{r_2}$ is the witness size. In our instantiation $\codedim_1\approx \codedim^{0.6}\iflncs\else = 2^{r_1}\fi$ and $\codedim_2\approx \codedim^{0.4}\iflncs\else =2^{r_2}\fi$ (\cref{s:instantiation}). %The $\Theta(\codedim)$ additions and table reads are unavoidable --- every bit of the witness must be touched --- but additions in $\comfield$ are $128$-bit XORs; the gain over the generic prover is that only $O(\codedim_1+\codedim_2)$ \emph{multiplications} are performed instead of $\Theta(\codedim)$.
    }
\label{t:gkr_step1_costs}
\end{table}
\end{lncstable}





\paragraph{Subsequent prover messages in the last sumcheck in Step 2 of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}.} Next we focus on computing $h_2(X)$. Substituting $X$ by the verifier's first sumcheck challenge $\rho_1$ in \cref{e:grand_product_Lr1,e:grand_product_Lr2}, we have, for every $\bb\in \bits^{r_1-1}, \cc\in \bits^{r_2}$, 
%
\begin{align*}
L_{r_1}(\rho_1,\bb, \cc) &   =\left(L_{r_1}( 1, \bb,\cc) -  L_{r_1}( 0, \bb,\cc)\right)\cdot \rho_1 + L_{r_1}( 0, \bb,\cc)\\ &= \begin{cases}
\yy_{\bb}^{(11)}\defeq \left(\yy_{1,\bb}^{(1)} - \yy_{0,\bb}^{(1)}\right)\cdot \rho_1 + \yy_{0,\bb}^{(1)} &\text{if } \bfg_{(0,\bb),\cc}=1,\ \bfg_{(1,\bb),\cc}=1 \\  
\yy_{\bb}^{(10)}\defeq \left(\yy_{1,\bb}^{(0)} - \yy_{0,\bb}^{(1)}\right)\cdot \rho_1 + \yy_{0,\bb}^{(1)}&\text{if } \bfg_{(0,\bb),\cc}=1,\ \bfg_{(1,\bb),\cc}=0\\
\yy_{\bb}^{(01)}\defeq 
\left(\yy_{1,\bb}^{(1)} - \yy_{0,\bb}^{(0)}\right)\cdot \rho_1 + \yy_{0,\bb}^{(0)} & \text{if } \bfg_{(0,\bb),\cc}=0,\ \bfg_{(1,\bb),\cc}=1 \\
    \yy_{\bb}^{(00)}\defeq \left(\yy_{1,\bb}^{(0)} - \yy_{0,\bb}^{(0)}\right)\cdot \rho_1 + \yy_{0,\bb}^{(0)} &\text{if } \bfg_{(0,\bb),\cc}=0,\  \bfg_{(1,\bb),\cc}=0
\end{cases}
\end{align*}
%®
The idea now is that the prover can compute $h_2(X)$ in essentially  the same way as  $h_1(X)$, but this time, instead of starting with the table \iflncs$$\begin{aligned}T_{r_1}^{(1)}\defeq T_{r_1}\in (\comfield^2)^{\bits^{r_1}} \quad \text{such that } T_{r_1}[\bb]=(\yy_\bb^{(0)}, \yy_{\bb}^{(1)})\\ \text{and } L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc) = T_{r_1}[\bb][\bfg_{\bb,\cc}], \quad  (\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1}\times \bits^{r_2},\end{aligned}$$\else$$T_{r_1}^{(1)}\defeq T_{r_1}\in (\comfield^2)^{\bits^{r_1}} \quad \text{such that } T_{r_1}[\bb]=(\yy_\bb^{(0)}, \yy_{\bb}^{(1)}) \text{ and } L_{r_1}(\bb,\cc) = T_{r_1}[\bb][\bfg_{\bb,\cc}], \quad  (\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1}\times \bits^{r_2},$$\fi
it starts with the table
%
\iflncs\begingroup\small\fi
$$
T_{r_1}^{(2)} \in (\comfield^4)^{\bits^{r_1-1}} \quad \text{such that } T_{r_1}^{(2)}[\bb] = ( \yy_{\bb}^{(11)}, \yy_{\bb}^{(10)},  \yy_{\bb}^{(01)},\yy_{\bb}^{(00)}) \quad \text{for } \bb\in \bits^{r_1-1}
$$
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
for which we have, indexing the  entries of  $T_{r_1}^{(2)}[\bb]$ with the $4$ values $(1,1),\lncsbreak (1,0),\lncsbreak (0,1),\lncsbreak (0,0)$,
%
$$
L_{r_1}(\rho_1, \bb,\cc) = T_{r_1}^{(2)}[\bb][(\bfg_{(0,\bb),\cc},\bfg_{(1,\bb),\cc})].
$$
This allows the prover, when needed, to obtain $L_{r_1}(\rho_1,\bb,\cc)$ for any $(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-1}\times \bits^{r_2}$ with a simple read in $T_{r_1}^{(2)}$.  

We now have, for $(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-3}\times \bits^{r_2}$, 
 \iflncs\begingroup\footnotesize\fi\begin{align*}A_0^{(2)}[\bb,\cc] &= L_{r_1}(\rho_1,0,\bb,0,\cc)\cdot L_{r_1}(\rho_1,0,\bb,1,\cc)\cdot\omega[\bb]\\ &= 
    T_{r_1}^{(2)}[0,\bb,0][(\bfg_{(0,0,\bb,0),\cc},\bfg_{(1,0,\bb,0),\cc})] \cdot    T_{r_1}^{(2)}[0,\bb, 1][(\bfg_{(0,0,\bb,1),\cc}, \bfg_{(1,0,\bb,1),\cc})] \cdot \omega[\bb]
\end{align*}
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
%
%$$
%A_0^{(2)}[\bb,\cc] = \begin{cases}
    %\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(1)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(1)} \cdot \omega[\bb]&\text{if } \bfg_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}=1,\ \bfg_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=1 \\  
    %\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(1)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(0)}\cdot \omega[\bb]&\text{if } \bfg_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}=1,\ \bfg_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=0\\
     %\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(0)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(1)}\cdot \omega[\bb]& \text{if } \bfg_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}=0,\ \bfg_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=1 \\
    %\yy_{0,\bb,0}^{(0)} \cdot  \yy_{0,\bb,1}^{(0)} \cdot  \omega[\bb] &\text{if } \bfg_{(0,\bb,0),\cc}= 0,\  \bfg_{(0,\bb,1),\cc}=0
%\end{cases}
%$$
where $\omega[\bb]=\eq(\rho_1, \bb;\bm{\alpha}^{(i,1)})$ is the weight defined above for $j=2$.
%
Hence the elements $A_0^{(2)}[\bb,\cc]$ can be obtained by querying a table $$T_{A_0}^{(2)}\in (\KK^{16})^{\bits^{r_1-3}}$$ with
%
$$T_{A_0}^{(2)}[\bb]= (T_{r_1}^{(2)}[0,\bb,0][\xx]\cdot T_{r_1}^{(2)}[0,\bb,1][\xx']\cdot \omega[\bb])_{\xx,\xx'\in \bits^{2}}.$$
%
A  table $T_{S_1}^{(2)}$ is computed analogously to $T_{S_1}$. The value $A_2^{(2)}[\bb,\cc]$, however, now depends on the value taken by \emph{eight} witness bits, this being because for $\eps\in \bits$, the value $E_{1\eps}^{(2)}-E_{0\eps}^{(2)}$ depends on  four bits. Hence computing a table for the values $A_2^{(2)}[\bb,\cc]$ analogous to $T_{A_2}$  would have $2^8=256$-sized vectors as entries. Instead of computing $T_{A_2}^{(2)}$, we compute two tables for the values $E_{1\eps}^{(2)}-E_{0\eps}^{(2)}$ (one table for each $\eps\in\bits$, with one of the tables also including multiplication by $\omega[\bb]$). Then the value $A_2^{(2)}[\bb,\cc]$ can be obtained by performing a lookup into each table, followed by  a single multiplication.

\bigskip

This can be iterated in further sumcheck rounds, for computing $h_3(X), h_4(X),$ etc. The limitation is that the entries in the tables are vectors whose dimension is squared at each round. More precisely, each further round squares the number of cases per index of every table, but it only halves the number of indices.
Hence, in practice we stop using lookup tables after a few rounds of each sumcheck and GKR, and after that we execute the sumcheck protocol normally. Concretely, in our implementation we never compute a table with more than $16$ elements per index. This is done so that the tables can be kept in cache, since we observe that, when this is not the case, table lookups become more and more expensive compared to  multiplications in $\FF_{2^{128}}$.     \cref{tab:gkr_tables} displays the different tables built or not built in our simplest implementation, the arithmetic cost of doing so, and their size at $\codedim_1=2^{r_1}=2^{16}$ (corresponding to a total witness size of $\approx 2^{26},2^{27}$ bits in our implementation). In our final implementation we do not explicitly compute all of the tables, similarly as done with the table $T_{A_2}^{(2)}$ above.


\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \small
    \begin{tabular}{c|c|c|c|l}
        Table & Dimension & Adds/mults.\ in $\KK$ & Size (MB), $\ell_1=2^{16}$ & Built? \\
        \hline
        \hline
        $T_{r_1}^{(1)}$ & $(\KK^{2})^{2^{r_1}}$ & 0/0 & $2.1$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{A_0}^{(1)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{2}})^{2^{r_1-2}}$  &  $0$ / $2^{r_1 +1}$ & $1.0$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{S_1}^{(1)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{2}})^{2^{r_1-2}}$  &  $0$ / $2^{r_1 +1}$ & $1.0$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{A_2}^{(1)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{4}})^{2^{r_1-2}}$  &  $2^{r_1+1}$ / $5\cdot 2^{r_1}$ & $4.2$ & yes \\
        \hline
        \hline
        $T_{r_1}^{(2)}$ & $(\KK^{2^2})^{2^{r_1-1}}$ & $2^{r_1+2}$ / $2^{r_1+1}$ & $2.1$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{A_0}^{(2)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{4}})^{2^{r_1-3}}$  &  $0$ / $2^{r_1 +2}$ & $2.1$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{S_1}^{(2)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{4}})^{2^{r_1-3}}$  &  $0$ / $2^{r_1 +2}$ & $2.1$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{A_2}^{(2)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{8}})^{2^{r_1-3}}$  &  --- & $33.6$ & no  \\
        \hline
        tables for $E^{(2)}_{1\eps}-E^{(2)}_{0\eps}$, $\eps\in\bits$ & $(\KK^{2^{4}})^{2^{r_1-3}}$ each  &  $2^{r_1+2}$ / $2^{r_1+1}$ & $2.1$ each & yes \\
        \hline
        \hline
        $T_{r_1}^{(3)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{4}})^{2^{r_1-2}}$  & $2^{r_1+3}$ / $2^{r_1+2}$ & $4.2$ & yes \\
        \hline
        $T_{A_0}^{(3)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{8}})^{2^{r_1-4}}$  &  --- & $16.8$ & no \\
        \hline
        $T_{S_1}^{(3)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{8}})^{2^{r_1-4}}$  &  --- & $16.8$ & no  \\
        \hline
        $T_{A_2}^{(3)}$ & $(\KK^{2^{16}})^{2^{r_1-4}}$  &  --- & $4295$ & no
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Dimensions, build costs, and sizes of the tables for computing $h_1(X), h_2(X), h_3(X)$ in the last sumcheck of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}, with $\log|\KK|\approx 128$. We use the example size $\codedim_1=2^{r_1}=2^{16}$, which, following our instantiation ($\codedim_1\approx \codedim^{0.6}$, \cref{s:instantiation}), corresponds to a total witness size $\codedim  \approx 2^{26},2^{27}$.}
\label{tab:gkr_tables}
\end{table}




\paragraph{The rest of the sumchecks in Step 2 of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}.} So far we have discussed the last sumcheck from Step 2 of \cref{c:gkr_grand_product}, corresponding to the lower layer of the binary tree of products of our GKR circuit for the grand products. 

The sumchecks run for $i=r_1-2, r_1-3,\ldots$ can be optimized in the exact same way as we did for the last sumcheck $(i=r_1-1)$. In this case, instead of starting with the table $T_{r_1}^{(1)}$, which at each index contains the two values $(\yy_\bb^{(0)}, \yy_\bb^{(1)})$, one starts with the table
%
$$
T_{r_1-j} \in (\KK^{2^{2^{j}}})^{\bits^{r_1-j}}
$$
described in \cref{e:size_T_r1_i}. Since $T_{r_1-j}$ has, per index, the square of the number of entries of $T_{r_1-j+1}$, all subsequent tables also have, per index, the square of the number of entries of the analogous tables from the $(i+1)$-th sumcheck, and they require correspondingly more operations to compute.


In our implementation we want to keep all tables in cache (otherwise we observe that multiplication in $\FF_{2^{128}}$ is cheaper than a table lookup). Because of this, the smaller $i$ is (i.e.\ the further we get from the last sumcheck in the GKR protocol), the fewer sumcheck rounds we use tables in. Concretely, for $i=r_1-2$ ($j=1$) we build and use the table $T_{r_1-1}^{(1)}$, the analogues of $T_{A_0}^{(1)}$ and $T_{S_1}^{(1)}$ (now with $16$ entries per index), and $T_{r_1-1}^{(2)}$. Similarly as done for $A_2^{(2)}$, the analogue of $T_{A_2}^{(1)}$ (which would have $2^8$ entries per index) is replaced by two tables of $16$ entries per index and one multiplication per read. For $i=r_1-3$ $(j=2)$ we only use the table $T_{r_1-2}^{(1)}$.

\begin{comment}
\begin{remark}[Factored tables]\label{r:factored_tables}
The tables and operation counts in \cref{t:gkr_step1_costs,tab:gkr_tables} describe the algorithm as presented above. In our implementation, the four $16$-entry tables of the second round of the last sumcheck (namely $T_{A_0}^{(2)}$, $T_{S_1}^{(2)}$ and the two tables for the values $E^{(2)}_{1\eps}-E^{(2)}_{0\eps}$), their analogues in the first round of the sumcheck for $i=r_1-2$, and the table $T_{r_1-2}^{(1)}$ are not built explicitly. Instead, the prover builds smaller $4$-entry tables and recomputes each required value from two of their entries with one multiplication.%: the prover keeps only the $4$-entry tables they are products of, and recomputes the required values from their entries with one multiplication per read. This costs $\Theta(\codedim)$ additional multiplications (between $\codedim/4$ and $\codedim/2$ per sumcheck round concerned), and in exchange it saves the $O(\codedim_1)$ multiplications needed to build the tables and reduces their footprint by a factor between $2$ and $8$. As observed above, this is a net win at the sizes we target ($\codedim\geq 2^{28}$), since the prover is memory-bound and a multiplication in $\KK$ is cheaper than a data-dependent load from a table that does not fit in the L2 cache. The proofs are unaffected. We omit the details for brevity.
\end{remark}
\end{comment}



%\paragraph{Using $4$ factor multiplication (instead of $2$) in the last step of the GKR grand product}



%In Step 2 we reduce the number of sumchecks by $1$ (so now there are $r_1-2$ sumchecks) by making the last sumcheck certify the claim
%$$
%C^{(r_1-2)}= \sum_{(\bb,\cc)\in \bits^{r_1-2}\times \bits^{r_2}} L_{r_1}(\bb,0,0,\cc) \cdot L_{r_1}(\bb,0,1,\cc)\cdot L_{r_1}(\bb,1,0,\cc)\cdot  L_{r_1}(\bb,1,1,\cc) \cdot \eq(\bb,\cc;\bm{\alpha}^{(i)})
%$$
%I.e.\ 

\begin{comment}
\begin{remark}[Lookup tables in Round 2 of \cref{c:core_iop}]
In Round 2 of \cref{c:core_iop}, the prover computes the elements $\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_{\codedim_2}$ of \eqref{e:tensored_equality} again by means of a lookup table $T$, which is indexed over  $i\in [\codedim_1/4]$ and contains the $16$-length vectors $$T[i]= (\sum_{k\in [4]} \eps_k \cdot \pi_q^{-1}(\vv^{(1)})_{4(i-1) + k})_{\eps_1,\ldots, \eps_4 \in \bits} \in \ZZ^{16}.$$
\end{remark}
\end{comment}

\begin{lncsappendix}[Detailed experiments\label{s:further_experiments}]{experiments}
\iflncs\else\section{Experiments} \label{s:experiments}\fi % LNCS: the label is re-attached to the body's Experiments section

\begin{lncsmove}{ExpSetup}
We implement the \ftwoz\ PCS (\cref{c:LinModFieldBin}) and an end-to-end proof system, called \ftwoz-SNARK, for integer R1CS constraints, as described in the introduction (\cref{s:intro_contributions}) and in \cref{s:to_snark} (note that \ftwoz \ can be used to prove much more general constraints, such as the ones in Zinc+ \cite{zincplus}, cf.\ \cref{s:to_general_bitz}). 





% [AI edit (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-21: the footnote gained a sentence on how the 100-bit level is set for the compared schemes; please review]
Our \ftwoz-PCS is instantiated with Reed-Solomon codes over $\FF_{2^{128}}$ of rate $1/2$ or $1/8$. As  backend of \ftwoz \ we use the Ligerito implementation from Flock \cite{flock} (commit \texttt{e636760}, with local modifications) with folding arity $4$. We set our experiments to 100 bits of round-by-round knowledge soundness\footnote{This means  that the largest round-by-round knowledge soundness error of the protocols is at most $2^{-100}$. Our own figure is a round-by-round bound that includes grinding ($16$ query bits and up to $9$ fold bits per level). For the schemes we compare against, we use each implementation\'s own $100$-bit setting, under its own soundness analysis (e.g.\ Binius64\'s $100$-bit target for the query phase of its Basefold opening).}, except in the Limber \cite{limber} comparison, cf.\  \cref{s:multiswap}, where we target 114 bits to match Limber's implementation. %We do not run experiments at $128$ bits of security, since several of the implementations we compare against (e.g.\ Binius64, Limber and Flock) cannot be configured at that level 

When targeting 100 bits of round-by-round soundness we use the Johnson bound as proximity parameter, and perform out of domain sampling. When targeting more security (in \cref{s:multiswap}) we use the unique decoding radius. Further instantiation details are given in \cref{s:instantiation}.


% [AI-drafted (Claude Opus 5), 2026-09-18: the anonymous-artifact sentence of the submission build, at the author's request; please review]



\end{lncsmove}

\begin{lncsmove}{ExpBinius}
\iflncs\else\paragraph{The Binius configuration suite.}\fi\label{s:binius_suite} When  comparing against Binius64 (commit \texttt{c28940a}, with local benchmarking patches), we use four configurations: Binius64 as in its repository, with rates $\rho=1/2$ and $\rho=1/8$ and with proximity parameter within the unique decoding radius. We call this ``Binius (UDR) $\rho$''. The original Binius64 repository uses Basefold \cite{basefold} as its PCS. We also experiment with Binius64 using the same Ligerito instantiation as the one we use in \ftwoz. In this case we use the Johnson bound as the proximity parameter, add out of domain sampling, and call the resulting scheme  ``Binius (Johnson) $\rho$''.

\iflncs
% [AI-drafted (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-16: LNCS-only one-sentence pointer from the Experiments section to the appendix experiments, as requested; please review]
We explain in \cref{s:further_experiments} why we do not include comparisons with Flock \cite{flock}, and anonymous credential proof systems like Longfellow \cite{longfellow} and Vega \cite{vega}. In short, for the first, \ftwoz \ is not a scheme designed to prove only large amounts of classic hashes: for that, a pure binary field approach like Flock's or Binius is better suited as of today. For the second, we do not support zero-knowledge in \ftwoz\ yet. This is ongoing work of ours.

The remaining experiments, including those summarized in \cref{tab:intro_table,tab:int_highlights}, are reported in \cref{s:further_experiments}.
% [AI-drafted (Claude Opus 5), 2026-09-16: LNCS-only one-sentence pointer to the Flock non-comparison paragraph, which moved to the start of the appendix experiments at the author's request; please review]
\fi
\end{lncsmove}

\iflncs We next provide more information regarding each of the  experiments  in \cref{tab:intro_table,s:experiments}, and provide results for a larger amount of configurations and parameters.  \else\fi



\paragraph{Non-comparisons.} The recent paper Flock \cite{flock} builds upon the Binius64 scheme, and manages  to prove over 300k SHA-256 compressions (and other hashes) per second on ten cores of an Apple M4 Max. Several of the innovations and optimizations of Flock have already been incorporated in the Binius64 repository. Flock was built with the purpose of proving many hash compressions in mind, while Binius64 is  designed to prove much wider classes of computations. Because of this, we do not include comparisons with Flock, i.e.\ because Flock does not immediately support proving the computations we test \ftwoz\ on. 

We make no claim that \ftwoz-SNARK is competitive with Flock or Binius64 for the purpose of proving a large amount of hashes (besides offering a simpler arithmetization via the $\mathsf{CM}$ relation from \cref{e:CM_rel}). Indeed if seeking raw performance, Flock or Binius64 is, as of now, better suited for that task.


 There is a series of works  on anonymous credentials that prove computations similar to SHA-256 followed by ECDSA signature verification \cite{longfellow,vega}. These works, additionally, prove JSON/CBOR document parsing, and are zero-knowledge. Because we do not support the latter at the moment of writing, we do not compare against them.

 %When proving computations with SHA-256 in them, we do not benchmark against Zinc+\cite{zincplus} because at these amounts of hash compressions it`' runs out of memory.
%We also do not compare against Flock \cite{flock}, for the reasons explained in \iflncs\cref{s:further_experiments}\else\cref{s:experiments}\fi.

\begin{lncsmove}{ExpAI}
\paragraph{Usage of AI.} The scheme was initially implemented with AI. After that we thoroughly reviewed the verifier and, following the approach of Flock \cite{flock} and other usages of AI \cite{zkgolf} to optimize against verified specs, we  used AI extensively to optimize the prover while keeping the verifier unchanged. Subsequently we manually reviewed, refactored and further optimized both the prover and verifier. The reported benchmark results correspond to this PoC implementation, which is the one provided \iflncs in the anonymous repository \url{https://github.com/bitzcodes/BitZ.git}.\else \url{https://github.com/albert-garreta/BitZ-PoC}.\fi

Additionally, we are currently developing a parallel production-grade  handcrafted implementation,  using more conventional methods. The repository is available at \url{https://github.com/worldfnd/BitZ}. 

As an experiment, we asked an AI to take the \ftwoz-PCS prover of the production-grade implementation and optimize it (keeping the verifier unchanged). The resulting optimized prover reproduced or even significantly improved the performance of the \ftwoz-PCS in the PoC implementation discussed above.  This suggests that the published benchmark numbers still have room for improvement.

%(and) The AI optimized implementation of \ftwoz\ from \cref{c:core_iop} produces the same transcripts as our handcrafted implementation of the same protocol. 


\iflncs\else


Additionally, AI was used to prepare the benchmarks in other repositories, which underwent revision by us. It was also used to run the benchmarks and organize the results into the  tables \iflncs of \cref{s:experiments}\else in this section\fi.
\fi
\end{lncsmove}

\subsection{Raw performance of the \ftwoz \ PCS for the core LinBitsRings relation}

In \cref{tab:f2z-raw-performance} we measure the performance of \cref{c:core_iop} for proving the Core LinBitsRings relation $\LinBitsDual(\bits, \pi, \FF_q,\cC)$ \iflncs\else following the instantiation in \cref{s:instantiation}\fi. Namely, we prove that $\langle \vv, \bff \rangle=\mu$ over $\FF_q$ for a vector of $\codedim$ bits $\bff$, and $\vv\in \FF_q^{\codedim}, \mu\in \FF_q$.  We set $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$ and $\cC$ is a Reed-Solomon code. We choose $|\FF_q|  < (2^{128}-1)/\codedim_1$, so that Round 1 of \cref{c:core_iop} does not trigger. The proximity parameter is set at the Johnson bound, so Round 0 (OOD sampling) is executed.  In later experiments, Round 1 also triggers. 

 We set $\codedim_1 = 2^{\lceil 0.6\, n\rceil}$ and $\codedim_1 \cdot \codedim_2 = \codedim$ (\cref{s:instantiation}).

\subsection{RSA accumulation and Poseidon hashing}\label{s:multiswap}

We compare Limber \cite{limber} (commit \texttt{853c6c4}), Zinc+ \cite{zincplus} and our \ftwoz-SNARK (\cref{s:to_snark}) on proving the same circuit as in the Limber paper. This consists in verifying four  exponentiations modulo a $2048$-bit RSA modulus, with $352$-bit exponents, plus a Poseidon-based hash-to-prime evaluation  (see Section 7.1 of \cite{limber}). We take the corresponding R1CS constraint over the integers  from the Limber repository. See \cref{s:related_work} for further details on Limber, Zinc+, etc. 

We set \ftwoz \ at $114$ bits of security, matching Limber\footnote{Limber states in the paper that it achieves $128$ bits of security, but its fingerprint step only achieves $114$ bits of security in the implementation, because the random prime sampled to perform the fingerprint is of $128$ bits, and no other measures (like, say, grinding) are taken.}. %For the \ftwoz\ opening we use Ligerito in the unique-decoding regime (as opposed to the other benchmarks where we use the Johnson bound) at rate $1/8$ with fold arity $4$, with grinding to achieve $114$ bits of security.

\subsection{SHA-256 hashing followed by ECDSA signature verification}


We experiment with proving the SHA-256 hash of a message of a few KB followed by an ECDSA signature verification on the message digest. This is a common application of proof systems in anonymous credentials. We  arithmetize the computation as an integer R1CS constraint with $\FF_2$-virtualization (\cref{s:to_f2_virtual}), i.e.\ as an instance of $\Relation_{\mathsf{R1CS-bits-virtual}}(\ZZ)$. 
The  SHA-256  constraints in particular are  an instance of the $\mathsf{CM}$ relation from \cref{e:CM_rel}, obtained using the methods from \cref{r:CM_is_NP_complete}.

% [AI edit (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-21: "upstream" added, since the pinned fork carries an unoptimized P-256 circuit written for this comparison, and a typo fixed; please review]
We benchmark \ftwoz\ both for proving SHA + ECDSA verification over the curve P-256 and over secp256k1. In the latter case, we compare \ftwoz-SNARK with the Binius64 \cite{binius64} suite (\cref{s:binius_suite}). To do so, we compose two circuits in the Binius64 repository, one for SHA-256 and one for ECDSA   verification (on the secp256k1 curve). Binius64 does not include a circuit for ECDSA verification on P-256, and hence we do not benchmark it.


For ECDSA over secp256k1, \ftwoz\  circuit matches mathematically the circuit in Binius64, i.e.\ we prove the same sequence of secp256k1 operations, with these operations being arithmetized over $\ZZ$ in our case. This arithmetization was produced with AI, while the rest of arithmetizations for SHA and for ECDSA verification over P-256  were obtained from a lean specification, see \url{https://github.com/reilabs/freigen/blob/324935e/Freigen/Circuit/Examples/Sha256.lean}


%Similarly,  Further circuit optimizations could be applied. 

 %We compare \ftwoz-SNARK with the Binius64 \cite{binius64} suite (\cref{s:binius_suite}). For the latter we compose two circuits in the Binius64 repo, one for SHA-256 and one for ECDSA (on the secp256k1 curve) signature verification.

We emphasize that, as done in the next experiment \cref{s:experiments_sha_modmul}, when seeking to prove a large amount of hashes, it is in  general preferable to arithmetize the hash over a binary field and follow the approach described in \cref{s:experiments_sha_modmul}. If one wishes for small proofs, or natural arithmetizations, one then may   arithmetize SHA as a $\mathsf{CM}$ instance (\cref{e:CM_rel}).

\subsection{Modular multiplications and bit operations (mod $2^{32}$ multiplication and SHA-256 hashing)}\label{s:experiments_sha_modmul}

We prove simultaneously a large chain of SHA-256 compressions, together with a large amount of multiplications modulo $2^{32}$. In this experiment, contrary to the previous one, SHA-256 is arithmetized over a binary field, using the R1CS constraint from Binius64 \cite{binius64}. The modular multiplications are arithmetized as an R1CS constraint over the integers, where each modular multiplication  $x\cdot y = z \mod 2^{32}$ is expressed as an integer constraint of the form $x\cdot y = z + 2^{32}\cdot u$, where $u$ is a new integer witness.  

This is an artificial circuit. For example, the mod $2^{32}$ integers are not related in any manner to the SHA-256 compressions. We see it as a proxy of computations that require many bit operations and integer/modular multiplications, e.g.\ RISC-V derived computations. The cost of ``gluing'' witness elements between one type of computation and another, both in \ftwoz-SNARK and Binius64, is essentially non-existent, because  both schemes commit to all the bits of witness elements separately, and these can be interpreted as  integers or binary field elements freely.

Our end-to-end \ftwoz-SNARK is a hybrid construction combining the PIOP over $\ZZ$ from \cref{s:to_snark}, together with the Binius64 PIOP over binary fields, as  described in \cref{s:hybrid_snark}.    To arithmetize modular multiplication in Binius64  we use an approach similar to that for multiplying 32-bit integers, explained in \cref{s:integer_exps}.

We measure computations where the witness bits are evenly split between  SHA hashing and modular multiplication. Reducing the amount of modular multiplications while keeping the amount of hashing constant, would bring the two proof systems to become the same in theory (though implementation-wise, there currently are harnesses that prevent that from fully happening). Conversely, reducing the amount of hashes and keeping the amount of modular multiplication constant would bring the experiment closer to the one where we multiply 32 bit integers (in both \ftwoz\ and Binius, 32-bit integer multiplication and multiplication modulo $2^{32}$ is proved through essentially the same circuits, respectively).

\subsection{Integer multiplication}\label{s:integer_exps}



% [AI edit (Claude Fable 5.1), 2026-09-21: the tables now carry Limber and Zinc+ rows at every bit-size, so the sentence restricting the comparison was updated; please review]
We measure the cost of proving $N$ integer multiplications of the form $x\cdot  y = z$, for $x,y$ random $t$-bit integers, and $z$ a $2\cdot t$-bit integer, for $t\in \{32,64,128\}$.  We compare against the Binius suite (\cref{s:binius_suite}), Limber and Zinc+.

 Binius64 operates natively with $64$ bit words, and has  a native constraint for the product of two such words $x,y$. Its output is two $64$-bit words $z_1, z_2$ such that $x\cdot y = z_1 + 2^{64} \cdot z_2$. Our 64-bit multiplication in Binius64 uses this functionality.  For $32$ bit products we constrain the word $z_1$ to be $x\cdot y$ and the high word $z_2$ to be zero. We also constrain the upper $32$ bits of $x$ and $y$, to be zero. For $t=128$ bit integer multiplication we use the  multiplication circuit from the Binius64 repository \url{https://github.com/binius-zk/binius64/blob/e0ddeb91d3826457322e3b7434a8ca0625f2f56e/crates/circuits/src/bignum/mul.rs#L27-L42}. 

 In the case of Limber (commit \texttt{853c6c4}, with some local patches for benchmarking), we use the integer multiplication benchmark that is already in its repository. This benchmark proves a chain of multiplications, i.e.\ each multiplication uses the output of the previous one. We modify it slightly so that the multiplications are independent from each other. We run Limber with its Brakedown PCS, set at $100$ bits of security for the column openings. In the case of Zinc+ \cite{zincplus} (commit \texttt{878fbd8}), we wrote the multiplication constraint ourselves using Zinc+'s constraint system. Here the witness is committed in $16$-bit limbs, and these are range checked via the lookup argument of Zinc+. These modifications were done with AI, and reviewed by us afterwards.

\subsection{Comparison with Fields-witch}\label{s:exp_fields_witch}


We compare experimentally \ftwoz\ with Fields-witch \cite{lev}, a PCS over the prime field $\FF_{2^{127}-1}$ that commits its witness over the binary field $\FF_{2^{127}}$. See \cref{r:comparison_grand_product_binius_lev} for a discussion of \cite{lev} and a conceptual comparison of both.  The Fields-witch repository is available at \url{https://github.com/morgana-proofs/fields-witch} (commit \texttt{30cca8c}). In Fields-witch we enabled assembly acceleration for  SHA-256, making Merkle commitments in it faster than in the original repository.

% [2026-09-20: the instantiation section moved here, to the appendix of the full version, at the author's request; the LNCS build places it through the spine list, unchanged]
\begin{lncsappendix}{instantiation}
\iflncs\subsection{Instantiation and achieving 100 or 128 bits of soundness}\label{s:instantiation}\else\section{Instantiation and achieving 100 or 128 bits of soundness}\label{s:instantiation}\fi
We instantiate \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin} by letting $\Pi_{\red}$ be an optimized GKR protocol for multiple grand product claims of certain form (cf.\ \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}). The IOP $\Pi_\iopp$ is taken to be the Ligerito \cite{Ligerito} implementation from Flock \cite{flock} with minimal modifications (see \cref{s:experiments}), with the ring switching step from \cite{binius2}. Note that the inner product claims to be proved when $\Pi_\iopp$ is called are not MLE evaluation claims. Since  the ring-switch implementation in \cite{flock} does not support arbitrary inner product claims,  we run a sumcheck to reduce those to MLE evaluation claims.  We instantiate \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin} with the following parameters:
\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{c|c}
        Parameter & Choices \\
        \hline\hline
        $R$ & A finite field (possibly non-prime)\\
        \hline
        $S$ &  Integers $\ZZ$\\
        \hline
        $\pi$ & Reduction mod $\Char(R)$\\
        \hline
        $\psi$ & Reduction mod $\Char(R)$\\
        \hline
        $\comfieldexp$ & $128$\\
        \hline
        $\cC$ & Reed-Solomon code over $\comfield$ of rate $1/2$ or $1/8$\\
        \hline
        Proximity parameter $\delta$ & Unique decoding radius, or up to the Johnson bound (\cref{r:johnson_instantiation})\\
        \hline
        $L$ & The standard bitification matrix (\cref{e:canonical_bitification_matrix})\\ 
         \hline
        $\cP$ & Set of primes. Discussed below.\\
         \hline
        $\cM_q=\{\pi_q\}$ & Reduction modulo $q$\\
        \hline $\codedim_1,\codedim_2$ & $\codedim_1\in \range{\codedim^{0.5}}{\codedim^{0.6}}$, $\codedim_1\cdot\codedim_2=\codedim$
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Parameter choices in our deployment of \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin}.}
    \label{tab:instantiation_params}
\end{table}





%
We observe experimentally that setting $\codedim_1 \approx \codedim^{0.6}$  is the optimal value for $\codedim_1$ in terms of prover speed and proof size. However choosing $\codedim_1\approx \codedim^{0.5}$ leads to smaller soundness errors, which can make a difference when  targeting $128$ bits of security.  Hence, depending on the security parameter and the rest of parameters, we select $\codedim_1$ in the range
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:codedim_1_proportion}
\codedim_1 \in [\codedim^{0.5}, \codedim^{0.6}].
\end{equation}
%
We next discuss how to achieve $\lambda=100$ or $128$ bits of round-by-round (rbr) knowledge soundness while using $\comfield=\FF_{2^{128}}$ (specifically, in GHASH form). This choice is crucial for efficiency, since the cost of the GKR batched grand product, the most expensive step of \ftwoz, is dominated by arithmetic in $\comfield$. Our experiments (\cref{s:experiments}) run at $\lambda=100$ (and at $\lambda=114$ in \cref{s:multiswap}), and achieving $\lambda=100$ is relatively straightforward via grinding. Achieving $\lambda=128$ requires a more careful choice of parameters, combined with grinding (\cref{s:IORs}); \cref{r:beyond_gridning} discusses alternatives requiring little or no grinding. The configurations below are examples. Other configurations may be perfectly plausible and practical. 
\bigskip


\noindent We need that the rbr knowledge soundness errors of \cref{c:core_iop}, namely $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}$ (if Round 0 is executed), $\kappa_0$ (if Round 1 is executed), $(\ksred_i)_{i}$ and $(\ksiopp_i)_{i}$, are all at most $2^{-\lambda}$. We assume first that the proximity parameter $\delta$ is within the unique decoding radius, so that Round 0 is skipped and so the error $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}$ is absent (\cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}). In \cref{r:johnson_instantiation} we discuss the regime where $\delta$ is larger.
%

 


%
%To ground our analysis, we two witness bounds $\codedim\leq2^{35}$ (so the witness consists of $\leq 2^{35}$ bits), say, and $\codedim_1 \leq 2^{21}$ following our rule of thumb.   






\paragraph{The errors $\ksiopp_i$.} Recall that $\Pi_\iopp$ consists of a preliminary sumcheck, the ring switch, and then Ligerito (or WHIR). The rounds of the sumcheck and of the ring switch have rbr knowledge soundness errors of the form $c/|\comfield|$ for some small constant $c$ (for example, $c=7$ for the ring switch). Hence for $\lambda=100$ there is nothing to do, and for $\lambda=128$ it suffices to grind a few bits  at each of these rounds. As for Ligerito (or WHIR), we simply configure it so that it achieves the target $\lambda$ according to its own soundness analysis \cite{Ligerito,whir,stir}, as done in \cite{flock,whir-repo}. 

\paragraph{The errors $\ksred_i$.} In our instantiation $\Pi_\red$ is the GKR protocol from \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}. By \cref{l:gkr_grand_product}, its first round has error $\log\codedim_2/|\comfield|$, and the rest of rounds are either sumcheck rounds for a degree $3$ polynomial, or steps that unify two evaluation claims into one, both with error at most $3/|\comfield|$. Hence, with $|\comfield|=2^{128}$, all these errors are already below $2^{-100}$. To achieve $\lambda=128$ it suffices to grind $2$ bits at each sumcheck and unification round, and a few bits at the first round. 

\paragraph{The error $\kappa_0$.} The error $\kappa_0$ is the most delicate, as it demands at the same time that $|\cP|$ be large and that $\max\cP < \fieldboundsizeexponent$. Recall that Round 1, and with it $\kappa_0$, is skipped in many scenarios. We assume that \ftwoz\ is used as a PCS inside a larger proof system, as done in \cref{s:to_snark}. In \cref{s:to_snark} specifically, the initial constraint is projected to a finite field $\FF$ (unless it is already expressed over a finite field), then a PIOP over $\FF$  is executed, which outputs an inner product claim on the  witness projected onto $\FF$, and then this claim is proved with the \ftwoz\ PCS, \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin}. We aim to configure \cref{c:core_iop,c:LinModFieldBin} at $128$ bits of security in this setting. We discuss two strategies. 


\paragraph{Strategy 1: the PIOP achieves $128$ bits of security through grinding and we avoid the error term $\kappa_0$.} If allowing, say, $18$--$19$ bits of grinding during each round of the PIOP (which adds $0.6$--$1.1$\,ms per round, cf.\ \cref{tab:grinding-cost}), then, say, a Spartan-like PIOP will achieve $128$ bits of security if run on a field $\FF$ of size $\approx 2^{112}$ to  $2^{113}$  (since each one of its rounds is either a sumcheck round for a degree $\leq 3$ polynomial, or a step that batches some claims with error $1/|\FF|$). Hence, if the initial constraint is over $\ZZ$, then we may project it to a $112$-$113$ bit random prime field $\FF$ and run a PIOP over $\FF$. We study this case next. Our analysis also applies if the original constraint was already expressed over a prime field of such size.  

Note that the projection of the constraint to $\FF$ also requires to be set up to 128 bits of security. We discuss this in \cref{r:first_projection_security}. 




%in this case we may assume $R$  is a finite field of size $|R| \approx 2^{112}- 2^{113}$.  In this latter case, we do need to make sure that this projection step achieves 128 bits of security, while still being able to project to a $112$-$113$ bit field. We discuss this below in this section \cref{?}


Let $q=|\FF|$. If
%
$$
q < \fieldboundsizeexponent,
$$
then $\kappa_0$ is not present as an error term, and there is nothing to adjust. Since $|\comfield|=2^{128}$  and $q\approx 2^{113}, 2^{112}$, this occurs as long as $\codedim_1 \lessapprox 2^{15},2^{16}$, and using \eqref{e:codedim_1_proportion}, as long as 
%
$$
\codedim\lessapprox 2^{25}, 2^{26} \qquad \text{if } \codedim_1\approx\codedim^{0.6}
$$
 and
$$
\codedim\lessapprox 2^{30}, 2^{32} \qquad \text{if } \codedim_1\approx\codedim^{0.5}.
$$
%
The specific upper bounds depend on how much grinding one is doing at the PIOP level. 

%Belo only analyse the scenario where $R$ is a finite field, and thus do not consider the case $R=\ZZ$, which is unlikely to occur in practice, unless \ftwoz\ is used in isolation, and not as a step subsequent to a PIOP. 





\paragraph{Strategy 2: No or essentially no grinding during the PIOP, and $\kappa_0$ is present and  grinding is used during the first projection step (Round 1) of \cref{c:core_iop}.} We now assume $\FF$ is either a prime field with  $|\FF|\geq \fieldboundsizeexponent$,  or a proper extension field. In this case  \cref{c:core_iop} goes through an initial projection step with soundness error  $\kappa_0$, i.e.\ Round 1 of \cref{c:core_iop}.

Recall (\cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}) that $\kappa_0 = \theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$. We use $\picanon: \ZZ[X]  \to \FF$ as in \cref{e:presentations}, so that $\picanon(h(X))= \pi_{\Char(\FF)}(h)(x)$ where $\pi_{\Char(\FF)}(h)$ is the polynomial $h$ after reducing its coefficients modulo $\Char(\FF)$, and  $x$ is a root of an irreducible polynomial defining $\FF_{q^d}$ (if $d=1$ and so $\FF_{q^d}$ is a prime field, then we can use a morphism $\picanon:\ZZ\to \FF_q$). We have $D=d-1$, $T\leq d$, and $N_\FF\leq q-1$ (see \eqref{e:ring_theta_params} and the text below it for a definition of these quantities). We instantiate the morphism sets $\cM_q$ as in \cref{l:examples_morphisms}, i.e.\ $\pirand$ reduces coefficients modulo a sampled prime and evaluates $X_1$ at a random $\alpha\in \FF_q$. In this case, by \cref{l:examples_morphisms},
    $$
    \kappa_0 \leq \frac{2\cdot (d-1)}{q_{\min}} + \frac{\log\left(2\cdot d\cdot \codedim\cdot (q-1)^2\right)}{|\cP|\cdot \lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor},
      $$
    where $q$ is  the characteristic of $\FF$.

%
%
    We select $\cP$ to be the set of all primes in a range $[2^a, 2^b]$. There are approximately  $$|\cP|\approx (2^b/b - 2^a/a)/\log_e(2)$$ such primes.  Using $b= 128 - \tau \cdot \log(\codedim)$,  $\codedim_1= 2^{\tau\cdot \log(\codedim)}$,  for a parameter $\tau \in \range{0.5}{0.6}$ and plugging these values into $\kappa_0$ we obtain
     %
     \begin{equation}\label{e:kappa_0_practical_upper_bound}
     \begin{aligned}
    \kappa_0&\lessapprox \frac{2\cdot (d-1)}{2^a} + \frac{\log\left(2\cdot d\cdot \codedim\cdot (q-1)^2\right)\cdot \log_e(2)}{\frac{a}{b}\cdot 2^{b} - 2^a},\\
    b&\defeq 128 - r_1, \qquad r_1\defeq \tau\cdot \log(\codedim).
    \end{aligned}
    \end{equation}
    %
    We calculate several minima for the right hand side of \eqref{e:kappa_0_practical_upper_bound} when $\FF$ is a  field of size $\approx 2^{128}$ (not necessarily a prime field), for varying values of $\codedim$ and $\tau \in \{0.5, 0.6\}$. We include the corresponding grinding times required to  achieve $128$ bits of security for the step with error $\kappa_0$.  

    %We also include minima of $f(a)$ for other configurations where $R$ is a finite field of size between $2^{128}$ to $2^{256}$, not necessarily a prime field.
    
   \begin{table}[H]
  \centering
 \begin{tabular}{rrrrrrrrr}
    \toprule
    $\log |\FF|$ & $d$ & $\log q$ & $\log \codedim$ & $\tau$ & $a^{*}$ & $\kappa_0\leq$ & Bit-deficit to $128$ & Grinding time (ms) \\
    \midrule
    128 & 1 & 128 & 24 & 0.5 & 110 & $2^{-108.3}$ & 20 & 3.4 \\
    128 & 1 & 128 & 24 & 0.6 & 107 & $2^{-105.9}$ & 23 & 25.9 \\
    128 & 1 & 128 & 28 & 0.5 & 108 & $2^{-106.3}$ & 22 & 11.9 \\
    128 & 1 & 128 & 28 & 0.6 & 105 & $2^{-103.5}$ & 25 & 98.1 \\
    128 & 1 & 128 & 30 & 0.5 & 107 & $2^{-105.3}$ & 23 & 25.9 \\
    128 & 1 & 128 & 32 & 0.5 & 106 & $2^{-104.3}$ & 24 & 54.9 \\
    \midrule
    128 & 4 & 32 & 24 & 0.5 & 114 & $2^{-109.2}$ & 19 & 1.8 \\
    128 & 4 & 32 & 24 & 0.6 & 111 & $2^{-106.8}$ & 22 & 11.9 \\
    128 & 4 & 32 & 28 & 0.5 & 112 & $2^{-107.1}$ & 21 & 6.4 \\
    128 & 4 & 32 & 28 & 0.6 & 109 & $2^{-104.4}$ & 24 & 54.9 \\
    128 & 4 & 32 & 30 & 0.5 & 111 & $2^{-106.1}$ & 22 & 11.9 \\
    128 & 4 & 32 & 30 & 0.6 & 108 & $2^{-103.1}$ & 25 & 98.1 \\

By choosing primes in the interval $[2^{a^*}, 2^{128-r_1}]$, we have that the first error term $\kappa_0$ is bounded by the quantity in the $\kappa_0\leq$ column. We include the bits left required to achieve  $\kappa_0 < 1/2^{128}$ and the cost of grinding such an amount of bits on a MacBook Air M5 24\,GB multi-threaded, using Blake3 as hash function.}
  \label{tab:min-f}

\begin{remark}[128 bits of security for larger witness sizes $\codedim$, without  or with minimal grinding]\label{r:beyond_gridning}
    Grinding costs scale exponentially in the number of grinding bits. Because of this, we cannot resort to grinding for very large witness sizes $\codedim$. There are at least two ways to achieve $128$ bits of security (or more), in this case.

    \medskip 
    
    \noindent \textbf{Method 1}: Use a larger binary field $\comfield$, i.e.\ simply take $\comfield$ with $|\comfield|$ comfortably larger, rather than $|\comfield|=2^{128}$.
 
 \medskip
 
 \noindent \textbf{Method 2}: We generalize an idea from \cite{binius64}. With it we relax the requirement $\max\cP< \fieldboundsizeexponent$ to $\max\cP<|\comfield|-1$ (and the conditions under which Round 1 is skipped are relaxed likewise). This way, the bound on $\cP$ no longer depends on the witness size and the primes gain $\log \codedim_1$ bits, making the soundness errors shrink by $\log \codedim_1$ too. Recall that Phase 3 of \cref{c:core_iop} proves the equalities $\langle \bm{\gamma}, \bff_{*j}\rangle =\mu_j$ ($j\in [\codedim_2]$) modulo $\mathsf{ord}(g)=|\comfield|-1$, which is why the entries of $\bm{\gamma}$ had to be smaller than $(|\comfield|-1)/\codedim_1$. If they are only smaller than $|\comfield|-1$, one can additionally prove the same equalities modulo $\codedim_1$. This works because, since $\codedim_1$ and $|\comfield|-1$ are coprime (because $\codedim_1$ is a power of two), by the CRT, if both equalities are true, then  $\langle\bm{\gamma}, \bff_{*j}\rangle -\mu_j$ is an integer multiple of $\codedim_1\cdot (|\comfield|-1)$. However, because the entries of $\bff$ are bits and the entries of $\bm{\gamma}$ are at most $|\comfield|-2$, this means that   $\langle \bm{\gamma}, \bff_{*j}\rangle = \mu_j$ as integers.
 
 To prove the equality modulo $\codedim_1$, the prover sends $a_j\in \range{0}{\codedim_1-1}$ (this is range-checked by the verifier) and proves in the exponent the integer equality $\langle \bm{\gamma}', \bff_{*j}\rangle = \mu_j'  +  a_j\cdot \codedim_1$, where $\bm{\gamma}'$ and $\mu_j'$ are $\bm{\gamma}$ and $\mu_j$ reduced modulo $\codedim_1$.  Both sides of the equality are smaller than $\codedim_1^2$, and so no overflow occurs as long as $\codedim_1^2 < |\comfield|-1$. 

  Note that if $\bm{\gamma}$ admits a tensor decomposition   $\bm{\gamma}=\bm{\gamma}^{(1)} \otimes \bm{\gamma}^{(2)}$, then we can take, instead of $\bm{\gamma}'$, the tensor-product $\bm{\gamma}^{(1)}{}' \otimes \bm{\gamma}^{(2)}{}'$, where  $\bm{\gamma}^{(i)}{}'$ denotes $\bm{\gamma}^{(i)}$ reduced modulo $\codedim_1$. This allows to use all of our ``low-entropy'' grand product optimizations from \cref{s:gkr_low_entropy}, though in this case the required bound becomes $2\cdot (\codedim_1^2+1)\cdot \codedim_1 < |\comfield|-1$.
  
    Overall, this method allows to use $\max\cP < |\comfield|-1$, meaning we can choose primes that are $\log(\codedim_1)$ bits larger than we otherwise would. This makes soundness errors smaller by this same amount of bits, which leads to small grinding requirements.  The cost is an extra batch of low entropy grand products. 
\end{remark}



\begin{remark}[Proximity parameter up to the Johnson bound]\label{r:johnson_instantiation}
When targeting $\lambda=100$, we set the proximity parameter to $\delta=1-\sqrt{\rho}-\eta$ with $\eta=0.02$, i.e.\ slightly below the Johnson bound. In this case $\cC$ is $(\delta,L_\delta)$-list-decodable with $L_\delta\leq 1/(2\eta\sqrt{\rho})$ \cite{GS99,stir}, which gives $L_\delta\leq 71$ for $\rho=1/8$ and $L_\delta\leq 36$ for $\rho=1/2$. With this proximity parameter, Round 0 (OOD sampling) is executed, and by \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP_OOD} the only new error term is $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}\leq \binom{L_\delta}{2}\cdot (\codedim/\comfieldexp-1)/|\comfield|$.

Plugging in the values of $L_\delta$ above, we obtain $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}<2^{\log\codedim-123.7}$ for $\rho=1/8$, and $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}<2^{\log\codedim-125.7}$ for $\rho=1/2$. Hence $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}\leq 2^{-100}$ as long as $\codedim\leq 2^{23}$ (for $\rho=1/8$) or $\codedim\leq 2^{25}$ (for $\rho=1/2$), and in these cases there is nothing else to do (recall that when using the Johnson bound we seek $100$ bits of security, and not $128$). For larger witnesses, the prover  grinds $\lceil \log\codedim-23.7\rceil$ bits at Round 0 if $\rho=1/8$, and $\lceil \log\codedim-25.7\rceil$ bits if $\rho=1/2$. For example, for $\codedim=2^{30}$ this is at most $7$ bits.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Projecting polynomial constraints to a $112$-$113$ bit prime field with $128$ bits of security]\label{r:first_projection_security}
    The error term of the projection step (also called fingerprinting) in \cite{zincplus,zinc,zaratan} is (for integer constraints) bounded by (cf.\ \cref{l:reduction_lemma} with $d=0$)
    %
    $$
    \eps_{\mathsf{PIOP-proj}}\leq \frac{\log(N)}{|\cP'| \cdot \lfloor\log(q_{\min})\rfloor}
    $$
    where $\cP'$ is a set of primes; $q_{\min}$ is the smallest prime in $\cP'$; $N$ is the largest  absolute value that an unsatisfying constraint can attain. More precisely, assume integer witness entries $\ww$ are guaranteed to have at most a certain bit-size $B'$, and assume the constraints have the form $q_i(\xx,\ww)=0$ for all $i$, for $\xx$ a public input vector and $q_i$ constraint polynomials. Then $N$ is the maximum possible value of the form $|q_i(\xx,\ww)|$ among all $i$, all possible inputs $\xx$ and all  $\ww$ with entries having bit-size at most $B'$. 
    
    Using a similar analysis as in Strategy 2 above, where we let $\cP'$ be the set of primes in the interval $[2^{112}, 2^{113}]$,  we obtain that, as long as $\log(N)< 1.4\cdot 2^{6}\approx 90$ we have $   \eps_{\mathsf{PIOP-proj}}< 2^{-106}$. In that case we could attain $128$ bits of security during this projection step by performing $22$ bits of grinding ($12$\,ms on a MacBook Air M5).
\end{remark}

\end{lncsappendix}

\section{Deferred proofs}
\iflncs

\subsection{Proof of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}}\label{a:core_iop_proofs}
\fi

\subsection{Completeness and soundness of \cref{c:LinModFieldBin}}\label{a:LinModFieldBin}


We prove \cref{t:thm_LinModFieldBits}. For readability, we temporarily denote   $\pi:\ZZ\to R$ for the reduction modulo $\Char(R)$ and we let
%
\iflncs\begingroup\scriptsize\fi
$$
\Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}\defeq \LinModFieldBin(\moduleonebound,\modulemorphism,\moduletwo,  \cC,L), \qquad \Relation_{\mathsf{core}}\defeq \LinBitsDual(\bits,\pi, R,\cC), \qquad \inp'\defeq (\mu,\uu),
$$
\iflncs\endgroup\fi
%
where $\uu=\psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv$ is the vector from \cref{c:LinModFieldBin}. As in \cref{s:soundness_core_iop}, we also write $\PESAT=\PESAT(\comfield,\lncsbreak Q_{\mathbb{B}\text{it}\ZZ},\lncsbreak\cC,\lncsbreak \pack)$ and $\LIN = \LIN(\comfield,\lncsbreak \cC,\lncsbreak \pack)$.
Recall (\cref{d:bitification}) that a bitification of $\bff\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}$ is any $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}$ with $\bff=L\cdot \bfg_S$, and that $L\cdot \bfg_S\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}$ for all $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}$. Given $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}$, we say that $((\mu,\vv),f,\bff)$ belongs to $\Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}^{\delta}$ \emph{via} $\bfg$ if $\hamming(f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack\circ \pi_2(\bfg))<\delta$ and $\langle \psi(\bff),\vv\rangle=\mu$. Then, by definition of $\Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}$ and $\Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}^{\delta}$, we have that  $((\mu,\vv),f,\bff)\in \Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}^{\delta}$ if and only if $((\mu,\vv),f,\bff)$ belongs to $\Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}^{\delta}$ via some bitification $\bfg$ of $\bff$. The same statement holds for $\Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}$  with $\delta=0$.

Now, given  $\bfg\in \bits^{\codedim}$, let $\bff_{\bfg}\defeq L\cdot \bfg_S\in (\moduleonebound)^{\codedim_0}$. By \cref{l:bitify} we have $\langle \psi(\bff_{\bfg}), \vv\rangle = \langle \psi(\bfg_S), \psi(L)^{\mathsf{T}}\cdot \vv\rangle = \langle \pi(\bfg),\uu\rangle$, and hence
%
\begin{equation}\label{e:appendix_bitify}
(\inp', f, \bfg)\in \Relation_{\mathsf{core}}^{\delta} \quad \Longleftrightarrow\quad  ((\mu, \vv), f, \bff_{\bfg}) \text{ belongs to } \Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}^{\delta} \text{ via } \bfg,
\end{equation}
%
and the same holds for $\Relation_{\mathsf{core}}$ and $\Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}$. Completeness of \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} follows from \eqref{e:appendix_bitify} and from the completeness of \cref{c:core_iop} (\cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}).

\bigskip

\noindent \emph{\underline{Round-by-round knowledge soundness.}} We decompose \cref{c:LinModFieldBin} as we did with \cref{c:core_iop} in the proof of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}. The first IOR, which we call $\Pi_1'$, computes $\inp'$ from the input $(\mu,\vv)$, and then runs the IOR $\Pi_1$ from the mentioned proof (together with the OOD sampling step if Round 0, cf.\ \cref{a:OOD}). The other two IORs are $\Pi_\red$ and $\Pi_\iopp$, which are exactly as in the proof of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}. Note that $\Pi_1'$ is perfectly complete by \eqref{e:appendix_bitify}.
To prove round-by-round knowledge soundness, assume first that Round 0 (OOD sampling) is not executed. By the IOR composition result \cref{r:ior_compositoin}, it suffices to prove that $\Pi_1'$ has round-by-round knowledge soundness with relaxation $(\Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}^{\delta},\PESAT^{\delta})$, and with the same errors as $\Pi_1$, i.e.\ $\kappa_0=\theta(\cP,\cM,\codedim)$. We also assume that Round 1 triggers, so that the verifier of $\Pi_1'$ sends a single challenge $\rho=(q,\pirand)$, with $q\in\cP$ and $\pirand\in\cM_q$ (if Round 1 does not trigger, the verifier sends no challenge at all, and the argument below applies equally after replacing $\rho$ by an empty message and $\kappa_0$ by $0$). Both $\Pi_1$ and $\Pi_1'$ have two rounds, with the last verifier message being empty.


Let $\kstate_1$ be the knowledge state function of $\Pi_1$, whose extractor, recall, was defined as the identity map. We define a state function $\kstate$ of $\Pi_1'$ as follows: if $\tr$ contains no verifier message, then $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr,\ww)=1$ if and only if $(\inp,\oracle,\ww)\in \Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}^{\delta}$. Otherwise se set $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr,\ww)\defeq \kstate_1(\inp',\oracle,\tr,\ww)$. As for the extractor, we define $\rbrextractor(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr,\ww)\defeq \bff_{\ww}$ if $\tr$ ends with the first verifier message $(q,\pirand)$ and $\ww\in \bits^{\codedim}$, and $\rbrextractor(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr,\ww)\defeq \ww$ otherwise. We claim that $\kstate$ satisfies the three conditions of \cref{def:knowledge-state-function}. Ineed, the first one (empty transcript) holds by definition. The second one (prover moves) holds because $\kstate$ does not change before the verifier message, and after it $\kstate$ coincides with $\kstate_1$, which satisfies the condition. The third one (full transcript) holds because $\Pi_1'$ and $\Pi_1$ have the same output.



It remains to bound the error at the verifier message $\rho=(q,\pirand)$. Let $\tr=(\mu')$ be the first prover message, and suppose that $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr\Vert\rho,\ww)=1$ but $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr,\rbrextractor(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr\Vert\rho,\ww))=0$. We claim that then $(\inp',\oracle,\ww)\notin\Relation_{\mathsf{core}}^{\delta}$. Indeed, if it was, then $\ww\in\bits^{\codedim}$, hence  $\rbrextractor(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr\Vert\rho,\ww)$ outputs $\bff_{\ww}$. Since   $(\inp',\oracle,\ww) \in \Relation_{\mathsf{core}}^{\delta}$, we have by \eqref{e:appendix_bitify}  that $(\inp,\oracle,\bff_\ww) \in \Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}^{\delta}$. By definition of $\kstate$ we then obtain  $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr,\bff_{\ww})=1$, contradicting the assumption $\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr,\rbrextractor(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr\Vert\rho,\ww))=\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr,\bff_{\ww})=0$. Hence $(\inp',\oracle,\ww)\not\in\Relation_{\mathsf{core}}^{\delta}$. By  definition of $\kstate_1$, $\kstate_1(\inp',\oracle,\tr,\ww)=0$. On the other hand, $1=\kstate(\inp,\lncsbreak\oracle,\lncsbreak\tr\Vert\rho,\ww)=\kstate_1(\inp',\oracle,\tr\Vert\rho,\ww)$. The latter two conditions hold with probability at most $\kappa_0$ by the proof of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}. Hence the round-by-round knowledge error of $\Pi_1'$ at $\rho$ is at most $\kappa_0$.

Finally, if Round 0 is executed, then we need to prove round-by-round knowledge soundness of $\Pi_1'$ with relaxation $(\Relation_{\mathsf{gen}}^{\delta},\PESAT^{\delta,\zeta,y})$ (see \cref{a:OOD} for a definition). The same arguments apply essentially word by word:  it suffices to let the extractor output $\bff_\ww$ when $\tr$ ends with the first verifier message in this case (the OOD sample) and $\ww\in\bits^{\codedim}$, and otherwise the extractor outputs $\ww$. The errors are $(\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}},\kappa_0,0)$ in this case (cf.\ \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP_OOD}). Here $\kappa_0$ and $0$ follow from the same arguments as in the proof of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP_OOD}, since after Round 0 the IOR $\Pi_1'$ proceeds exactly as $\Pi_1$, with $\kstate=\kstate_1$.


\subsection{Soundness of Out Of Domain Sampling (OOD) in \cref{c:core_iop}}\label{a:OOD}


%When $\delta$ exceeds the unique decoding radius of $\cC$, Round 0 of \cref{c:core_iop} is executed.\iflncs\ In that case, the verifier samples and sends a random $\zeta\in \comfield$, and the prover responds with $$y=\mle{\pack\circ\pi_2(\bff)}(\bm{\zeta}), \qquad \text{where } \bm{\zeta}\defeq \left(\zeta^{2^0}, \zeta^{2^1}, \ldots, \zeta^{2^{\log (\codedim/\comfieldexp) -1}}\right).$$\fi{} In this case the oracle $f$ can be $\delta$-close to up to $L_\delta$ codewords, and not just to one. As in STIR and WHIR \cite{stir,whir}, the out-of-domain sample is used to ``bind'' the prover to one of these codewords: with high probability over the choice of $\zeta$, there is at most one bit vector $\bfg$ with $f$ close to $\Enc_\cC\circ\pack(\bfg)$ and with $\mle{\pack(\bfg)}(\bm{\zeta})=y$. Once we know that this latter condition holds, the analysis is the same as in the unique decoding regime, after adding the OOD evaluation claim to the relations $\PESAT$ and $\LIN$, following \cite{whir}. Concretely, we 



We adapt the arguments from \cite{stir,whir} to our context. Given $\zeta,y\in \comfield$, let $\bm{\zeta}= \left(\zeta^{2^0}, \zeta^{2^1}, \ldots, \zeta^{2^{\log (\codedim/\comfieldexp) -1}}\right)$, and  let $\PESAT^{\zeta,y}$ be the set of triples $((\inp, \zeta, y), \oracle, \wit)$ with $\zeta,y\in\comfield$, $(\inp,\oracle,\wit)\in \PESAT$, and $\mle{\pack(\wit)}(\bm{\zeta})=y$, where $\bm{\zeta}=(\zeta^{2^{i-1}})_{i\in [\log (\codedim/\comfieldexp)]}$ is the evaluation point from Round 0. The relations $\LIN^{\zeta,y}$, $\PESAT^{\delta,\zeta,y}$ and $\LIN^{\delta,\zeta,y}$ are defined in the same way. 

\begin{theorem}[\Cref{t:thm_core_IOPP} when Round 0 is executed]\label{t:thm_core_IOPP_OOD}
Let $\delta\in (0,1)$ and $L_\delta\geq 1$ be such that $\cC$ is $(\delta, L_\delta)$-list-decodable, let $\codedim/\comfieldexp$ be a power of two, and assume that Round 0 of \cref{c:core_iop} is executed and that the rest of hypotheses of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP} hold, where now the relations in the hypotheses on $\Pi_{\mathsf{red}}$ and $\Pi_{\iopp}$ are $\PESAT^{\zeta,y}, \LIN^{\zeta,y}, \PESAT^{\delta,\zeta,y}, \LIN^{\delta,\zeta,y}$. Then the conclusion of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP} holds, except that now there is one more round, and one additional error term at the beginning, namely $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}} \leq \binom{L_\delta}{2}\cdot (\codedim/\comfieldexp-1)/|\comfield|$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}[OOD collision probability]\label{l:ood_collision}
Let $f\in \comfield^{\codelength}$, let $\mathsf{List}(f,\delta)$ be the set of $\bfg\in \FF_2^{\codedim}$ with $\hamming(f, \Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack(\bfg))<\delta$, and let $\mathsf{Coll}(f,\zeta)$ be the event that two distinct $\bfg, \bfg'\in \mathsf{List}(f,\delta)$ satisfy $\mle{\pack(\bfg)}(\bm{\zeta}) = \mle{\pack(\bfg')}(\bm{\zeta})$. Then
%
$$
\Pr_{\zeta\gets \comfield}[\mathsf{Coll}(f,\zeta)] \leq \binom{L_\delta}{2}\cdot \frac{\codedim/\comfieldexp-1}{|\comfield|}.
$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Since $\Enc_{\cC}\circ \pack$ is injective, $|\mathsf{List}(f,\delta)|\leq L_\delta$. Now take two distinct $\bfg, \bfg'$ in the list, and let $P\defeq \mle{\pack(\bfg)}-\mle{\pack(\bfg')}$, which is a nonzero multilinear polynomial on $r\defeq \log (\codedim/\comfieldexp)$ variables. After substituting $X_i \gets \zeta^{2^{i-1}}$, each monomial $\prod_{i\in I} X_i$ of $P$ becomes the power $\zeta^{\sum_{i\in I} 2^{i-1}}$. Hence $P(\bm{\zeta})$ is a nonzero univariate polynomial in $\zeta$ of degree at most $\codedim/\comfieldexp-1$, and so it vanishes for at most that many values of $\zeta$. The lemma follows by taking a union bound over the $\binom{L_\delta}{2}$ pairs $\bfg,\bfg'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof sketch of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP_OOD}]
Let $\Pi_1$ be the IOR from the proof of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP}, which now consists of Rounds 0, 1 and 2, and outputs $\left(\inp'=(g^{\bm{\gamma}}, g^{\bm{\mu}},\ \zeta,\ y),\ \oracle'= f, \ \wit'=\picomfield(\bff)\right)$. This IOR is perfectly complete, since an honest prover sends $y=\mle{\pack\circ\pi_2(\bff)}(\bm{\zeta})$. Regarding soundness, by \cref{l:ood_collision}, except with probability $\kappa_{\mathsf{OOD}}$ over the choice of $\zeta$, there is at most one $\bfg\in \FF_2^{\codedim}$ with $\hamming(f,\Enc_{\cC}\circ\pack(\bfg))<\delta$ and with $\mle{\pack(\bfg)}(\bm{\zeta})=y$, for the value $y$ sent by the prover. This $\bfg$ is fixed before the verifier sends any further message, and so from this point on the proof of \cref{t:thm_core_IOPP} applies to it word by word, replacing the relations $\PESAT, \LIN, \PESAT^\delta, \LIN^\delta$ by $\PESAT^{\zeta,y}, \LIN^{\zeta,y}, \PESAT^{\delta,\zeta,y}, \LIN^{\delta,\zeta,y}$.
\end{proof}
\end{lncsappendix}
